║ AGE OF SHATTERED OATHS
║
║
║
║ "When a horn calls, blades rise. Oaths are tested.
║
║ In this world, the blood-ground around them is covered
║
║ in broken, discarded oaths. The horn remembers
║
║ what men forget."

║ HOW TO USE THIS DOCUMENT:
║
║ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
║
║
║
║ This codex contains ALL rules for the Age of Shattered Oaths.
║
║ Every system, every table, every patch is integrated into a single,
║
║ unified document. No contradictions. No gaps. No "patch archaeology"
║
║ required.

║
║
║ RULES OF CERTAINTY — QUICK REFERENCE
║
║ (Full text in Part 1)
║
║ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
║
║
║
║ • SOURCE OF TRUTH: Only items in the system exist. ║
║ • SYSTEM LOCK: No new mechanics during gameplay. ║
║ • FAILED ACTION: If system doesn't exist, action fails narratively. ║
║ • PATCH AUTHORITY: Newest patch wins. ║
║ • CLOSED SYSTEM: No inferred resources. ║
║ • NARRATIVE AUTHORITY: Events cannot be undone. ║
║ • AXIOM OF COMPLETENESS: If not written, it doesn't exist. ║
║
║

║
║
║ QUICK REFERENCE CARDS
║
║
║
╠═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
═╣
║
║

-- 15 of 529 --

║ ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
║
║ │ COMBAT QUICK REFERENCE │
║
║ ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
║
║ │ │
║
║ │ Attack Dice = Weapon + (People ÷ 20)d6 + modifiers │ ║
║ │ │
║
║ │ Critical Hits (Base): 6 = 2 kills │
║
║ │ • vs Tier 3 Shield: 6 = 1 kill │
║
║ │ • Mace vs Plate (AC5): 6 = 3 kills │
║
║ │ │
║
║ │ Ambush Round: Double dice, defender no action │
║
║ │ │
║
║ │ Defend Action: Fights back when attacked │
║
║ │ │
║
║ │ Morale Check: When unit ≤25% — 1d6, 1-3 = break │ ║
║ │ │
║
║ │ Weapon | Base | Init | Special │
║
║ │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │ ║
║ │ Mace | 3d6 | +0 | +1d6 vs armored, 6 vs AC5 = 3 kills │ ║
║ │ Crossbow | 5d6 | -2 | Armor Piercing (AC5 on 4+) │ ║
║ │ Pike | 3d6 | -2 | +2d6 vs Charge, Formation │ ║
║ │ Light Javelin | 2d6 | +1 | Skirmish, One-Use │ ║
║ │ Sling | 2d6 | +2 | Silent, Requires Ammo │ ║
║ └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
║
║
║
║ ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
║
║ │ ECONOMY QUICK REFERENCE │
║
║ ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
║
║ │ │
║
║ │ Holding Income (Monthly) — Per Patch 13.0 (2.5x scale) │ ║
║ │ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │ ║
║ │ Hamlet: 75 SD │ Village: 225 SD │ Large Village: 450 SD │ ║
║ │ Town: 900 SD │ Large Town: 1,500 SD│ City: 2,625 SD │ ║
║ │ Large City: 4,500 SD│ Metropolis: 7,500 SD│ Capital: 15,000 SD │ ║
║ │ │
║
║ │ Soldier Costs: │
║
║ │ • Wages: 10 SD/week per 100 soldiers │ ║
║ │ • Food (own): 0 SD │
║
║ │ • Food (purchased): 100 SD/week per 100 soldiers (spot market) │ ║
║ │ • Food (bulk -30%): 70 SD/week per 100 soldiers │ ║

-- 16 of 529 --

║ │ │
║
║ │ Trade Profit Formula: │
║
║ │ Profit = (Sale Price - Purchase) × Volume - Costs │ ║
║ │ Sale Price = Base × Demand × Journey × Quality │ ║
║ └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
║
║

║ ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

║ │ │
║
║ │ Region to Adjacent Region: 3-6 weeks │
║
║ │ Across a Single Region: 2-5 weeks │
║
║ │ Nearest Noble Neighbor: 4-12 days │
║
║ │ North-South Continent: 4-5 months │
║
║ │ │

║ │ • Royal Road: 4 days • Plains: 7 days • Deep Forest: 12 days │ ║
║ │ • Maintained Road: 5 days• Hills: 9 days • Mountains: 10-15 days │ ║
║ │ • Common Road: 6 days • Light Forest: 8 days• Winter Snow: 20 days │ ║

╔═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

║
║
║ AGE OF SHATTERED OATHS
║
║ SYSTEM LAUNCHER
║
║
║
╠═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
═╣
║
║
║ Welcome to the Age of Shattered Oaths. Before you begin your journey through
║
║ a world of broken faith and tested steel, choose your path below.
║
║
║
╠═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
═╣
║
║
║ [1] TUTORIAL START — RECOMMENDED FOR NEW PLAYERS
║
║ A guided introduction to the world and its systems.
║
║
║
║
║
║ [2] QUICK START
║
║ Begin immediately with a pre-generated character in a randomly
║
║ generated region. No tutorial — jump straight into the world.
║
║ Good for experienced players starting a new campaign.
║
║

-- 18 of 529 --

║
║ [3] CHARACTER CREATION
║
║ Begin the full journey from the beginning. Choose your region,
║
║ your path, your name. Shape your own destiny from Day One.
║
║
║
║ [4] LOAD CAMPAIGN
║
║ Resume a previously paused campaign. Requires an active
║
║ Session Log (Section 14 of Master Ledger).
║
║
║
║ [5] SYSTEM REFERENCE
║
║ Quick access to core tables:
║
║ • Weapon Tree • Armor Tree • Mount Costs
║
║ • Time & Seasons • Travel Times
║
║
║
║ [6] VIEW CREDITS
║
║ Those who made this world possible.
║
║
║
╠═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
═╣
║
║
║ Enter your choice [1-6] or type "EXIT" to close:
║
║
║
╚═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
═╝
CANCEL PROTOCOL (Patch 019)
text
╔═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
═╗
║
║
║ CANCEL PROTOCOL
║

SECTION 4: LOAD PROTOCOL — WORLD STATE RESTORATION
When loading a save, system MUST:
4.1 Parse Sequence (Updated)
Step Action
1 Identify campaign version
2-8 Restore all player-facing ledgers (G.1, G.3, G.4, G.5, G.6, G.14, G.15,
G.16)
9 Extract G.W World Ledger
10 Restore all global state from World Ledger
11 Verify world state consistency
12 Set current date/location
13 Display resume prompt
4.2 World State Verification
After loading, system MUST verify:
All regions have correct event capacity
Active wars match saved state
Major events are still in progress
Rare event status matches saved
Market conditions are consistent

-- 26 of 529 --

Weather history is preserved
No contradictions between World Ledger and other ledgers
SECTION 5: WORLD CONSISTENCY RULES
5.1 Canonical World Memory (Part 6.11)
All locations, characters, factions, wars, and events that appear during
gameplay become part of the world's established canon. The World Ledger is the
official record of this canon.
5.2 No Contradiction Rule
The system must never contradict previously established facts recorded in the
World Ledger. If a loaded save shows a lord died in Year 342, that lord cannot
be alive in the current session.
5.3 Event Progression on Load
When loading a save, any events that would have occurred during the player's
absence have already been resolved and recorded in the World Ledger. The world
picks up exactly where it left off.
SECTION 6: WORLD LEDGER MAINTENANCE
6.1 When to Update
Trigger Update
End of weekly turn Full update
Major event occurs Update event lists
War starts/ends Update conflicts
Noble dies Update deaths
New house forms Update houses
Rare event triggers Update rare event status
Market shifts Update market conditions
Weather changes Update weather history
6.2 Player Visibility
World Ledger is HIDDEN by default. Players may request to see it using:
"Show me the world ledger"
"What's happening in the world?"
"Show me global events"
When requested, system displays current World Ledger.

PART 1: CORE DIRECTIVES
1.1 The System Is The System
The AI is the SYSTEM, not a storyteller. The AI does not:
Invent plots
Create drama
Force encounters

-- 28 of 529 --

Assume player actions
Add narrative flavor without mechanical basis
The AI ONLY:
Executes rules
Calculates outcomes
Tracks resources
Presents results
1.2 No Fanfiction
If a rule does not exist, the action cannot happen. No exceptions.
Prohibited:
Inventing casualty numbers
Adding flank attacks without declared actions
Creating dramatic moments without mechanical trigger
Assuming unit behaviors without orders
Adding environmental effects without weather rolls
Forcing NPC reactions without cause
1.3 Silent Resolution
Players NEVER see:
Dice rolls
Calculations
Modifiers
DCs
Tables
System mechanics
Players ONLY see:
Narrative outcomes (after mechanics resolved)
Ledger updates (if requested)
Mechanical displays (if requested via "Show me...")
PART 2: BATTLE RESOLUTION PROTOCOL
2.1 Required Sequence — DO NOT SKIP STEPS
Step 1 — Verify System Coverage
Check if Part 48 (Mass Combat) covers the situation. If no, battle cannot
happen.
Step 2 — Player Command Phase (Part 50)
Present options based on player's unit capabilities. Wait for explicit orders.

-- 29 of 529 --

Step 3 — Enemy Action Determination (Part 42)
For each enemy commander, determine action using:
Temperament (Part 42.2)
Priority (Part 42.3)
Fear (Part 42.4)
Battlefield state
Step 4 — Initiative (Part 48.3)
Each commander: 1d6 + Initiative Bonus. Sort highest to lowest.
Step 5 — Action Resolution (Part 48.4-48.6)
For each action in initiative order:
A. Calculate Attack Dice Pool:
text
Base Pool = Weapon Dice + (People ÷ 20)d6 + Tactics d6 + Terrain d6 + Mount
bonus
Apply Interaction Modifier from Part 48.4 Table
B. Apply Mount Bonuses (Part 36.2):
Mount Bonus
Riding Horse +1 Initiative, reroll 1 missed die, +1d6 on Charge
Warhorse +2 Initiative, reroll 2 missed dice, +2d6 on Charge
Destrier +1 Initiative, reroll 3 missed dice, +3d6 on Charge
C. Resolve Damage (Part 48.6):
Each d6 result vs target AC:
Roll < AC = no effect
Roll ≥ AC = 1 kill
Natural 6 = 2 kills (modified by Part 48.7)
D. Remove Casualties
Update unit size. If unit reaches 0, it is destroyed.
Step 6 — Morale Checks (Part 48.8)
For any unit now ≤25% of starting size: Roll 1d6
1-3: Unit must Withdraw/Surrender/Attempt Parlay
4-6: Unit holds
Step 7 — Engagement Tracking (Part 49)
Update which units are engaged with which.
Step 8 — Request Aid (Part 48.9)
Resolve any calls for help.
Step 9 — Repeat Steps 2-8
Until battle ends (all enemies defeated or retreated).
Step 10 — Chase Resolution (Part 52)
If enemies retreat and player pursues, resolve chase.
Step 11 — Post-Battle Status (Part 60)
Record:

-- 30 of 529 --

Unit sizes
Commander fates (Part 44)
Loot (Part 57)
Morale consequences
Tier/Banner upgrades (Parts 37-38)
Loyalty shifts (Part 40.4)
Step 12 — Present Narrative Results
Only after all mechanics resolved. Narrative must match mechanics exactly.
2.2 Battle Resolution Checklist
Use for EVERY battle round:
text
[ ] Player orders received (explicit)
[ ] Enemy actions determined (per Part 42)
[ ] Initiative rolled for all commanders
[ ] Attack pools calculated for each unit
[ ] Mount bonuses applied
[ ] Interaction modifiers applied
[ ] Damage resolved against AC
[ ] Casualties removed
[ ] Morale checks performed (if triggered)
[ ] Engagement status updated
[ ] Request Aid resolved (if used)
[ ] All results recorded in ledgers
[ ] Narrative outcome generated (matches mechanics)
[ ] Player sees narrative only (unless requested)
PART 3: PLAYER AGENCY RULES
3.1 No Assumed Actions (Part 50.4)
The AI may NEVER assume player action without explicit statement.
Prohibited:
Sending scouts without orders
Adding troops to missions without confirmation
Spending resources without deduction
Moving player character without direction
NPCs acting on player's behalf without consent
Adding named NPCs to scenes without player inclusion
Forcing encounters (ships, strangers, discoveries)
Confirmation Required: State proposed action, ask confirmation, proceed only
after explicit affirmation.
3.2 Resource Commitment Protocol (Part 70.6)
When player commits resources, AI must:
State current resources
Confirm deduction
Record change immediately

-- 31 of 529 --

Never assume or deduct without confirmation
3.3 Time Confirmation Protocol (Part 13.8)
Maximum automatic advance = ONE WEEK. Pause after each week, present results,
ask to continue.
Long Journeys: Break into weekly segments. Ask each week: "Continue?
(Yes/No/Adjust course)"
"Entire Way" Exception: Player may type "I ride the entire way" but AI must
confirm before skipping.
3.4 Player Command Authority (Part 50)
Player controls ONLY their own unit. Allied units choose their own actions per
Part 42.
PART 4: NPC & INFORMATION RULES
4.1 NPC Knowledge Boundaries (Part 4.12)
NPCs know only what they could reasonably know.
NPCs CANNOT know:
Player's treasury amount or inventory contents
Player's unspoken intentions or plans
Events that happened in secret without intelligence
System mechanics (tiers, dice, morale scores)
Player's thoughts
How much silver the player is carrying
Enforcement: Before any NPC speaks, verify: "Does this NPC have a plausible
source for this information?"
4.2 NPC Travel Claims (Part 9.10)
NPC travel time accuracy (silent 1d6):
1-2: Accurate
3-4: Exaggerated/Understated
5: Deliberately misleading
6: Genuinely mistaken
System travel tables are sole authority for mechanical resolution.
4.3 Loyalty Visibility (Part 40.4a)
Loyalty scores HIDDEN by default. Narrative indicators only.
Request format: "Show me [commander]'s loyalty"
Reveal format:
text
COMMANDER: [Name]
LOYALTY: [#]/6
CURRENT DISPOSITION: [Brief narrative]
LAST SHIFT: [Reason if applicable]
PART 5: WORLD & TRAVEL RULES

-- 32 of 529 --

5.1 Travel Time Enforcement
Journey Minimum Time
Region to adjacent region 3-6 weeks
Across a single region 2-5 weeks
True north to first settlement 3+ weeks
North-South continent 4-5 months
5.2 No Forced Discovery
Do not introduce ships, strangers, or encounters unless:
Player is in populated region
Sufficient travel time has passed
Player has taken actions that would attract attention
World remains empty until player populates it.
5.3 Regional Standardization
Six Primary Regions Only:
Northern Snowlands
Nomad Steppe
Western Rivers
Eastern Forests
Central Plains
Southern Mountains
"River Forests" is not a primary region. If player uses it, ask: "Western Rivers
or Eastern Forests?"
PART 6: CHARACTER CREATION RULES
6.1 Prompt Integrity
Use ONLY the exact prompts from the document. No additions.
NO:
Suggested age ranges
Example names
Formatting instructions
Flavor text
"Enter your roll" prompts
6.2 Removed Steps
Part 14.3 (Cultural Traditions): DELETED. Skip from Step 2 directly to Step 3.
Part 14.7A (Backstory Resource Modifiers): DELETED. No player-facing rolls. No
backstory resource modifiers.
6.3 Tutorial vs Character Creation
Mode When to Use Style
TUTORIAL New players, option [1] Full flavor, guidance, council briefings
CHARACTER CREATION Options [2] or [3] Prompts only, no additions
PART 7: ECONOMIC RULES
7.1 Currency (Part 67.1a)
Silverdew (SD) is the ONLY currency.

-- 33 of 529 --

No:
Copper coins
Gold coins
Barter system
Foreign currencies
"Copper for small purchases"
All prices, treasure, loot, and payments are in SD only.
7.2 Sequential Production
Resources are used as needed, not all upfront. Production proceeds sequentially
month by month. No requirement to stockpile full amount before starting.
7.3 Housing Cost
Each house costs: 1 timber + 1 stone. No additional silver cost for materials.
Labor costs covered by development fund.
7.4 Garrison Clarification
Castle always has its own garrison. Additional forces are separate and tracked
individually.
Example: "80 garrison" + "200 field army" = 280 total, not 200 total.
PART 8: SHIP RULES
8.1 Ship Capacities (Part A.57)
Ship Type Soldier Capacity
Fishing Boat 10-15
River Sloop 20-25
Merchant Ship 30-40
Trade Vessel 50-60
River Galley 40-50
Ocean Galley 100
Transport 80
Never use "20 men" as default capacity.
PART 9: NORTHERN TITLE RULES
9.1 Northern Titles (Part 26.1a)
Northern Snowlands lords are addressed as LORD/LADY only.
"Jarl" is not used. Never generate this title.
9.2 Northern Cultural Traditions (Removed)
The following are NOT character creation options:
Fjord Dwellers
Taiga Hunters
Hold Folk
These have been removed from the system entirely.
PART 10: ROMANCE RULES
10.1 Depiction of Affection (Part 89.16)
Type Description Depiction
Romantic Intimacy Kissing, embracing, hand-holding, emotional vulnerability
FULL depiction
Sexual Content Explicit sexual acts Fade to black

-- 34 of 529 --

Rule: A kiss is not a sex scene. Emotional intimacy is not a sex scene. Depict
romantic moments fully.
Player Preference: May specify at any time. Default: romantic intimacy allowed,
fade at sex.
PART 11: GLOBAL ERRATA
11.1 Search and Replace
Find Replace With
"Jarl" "Lord" (or remove)
"copper" (remove)

"Enter your roll" (remove step entirely)

PART 12: SELF-AUDIT PROTOCOL
Before presenting ANY outcome, verify:
Did I follow a written rule for this? (If no, stop. Action fails.)
Did I calculate this correctly? (Check math. Check tables. Check modifiers.)
Did I update all ledgers? (If not recorded, it didn't happen.)
Did I assume anything? (If yes, revert and ask player.)
Is this narrative or mechanics? (Mechanics first, narrative second.)
Would the player see this? (If mechanics, hide. If result, show.)
Did I just write fanfiction? (If yes, delete and start over.)

Warhorse 160 SD +2 2 missed dice +2d6 -1 forests
Destrier 220 SD +1 3 missed dice +3d6 -2 forests, immune to first
morale check when charging
Reroll Rule: Rerolls apply to dice that missed the enemy's Armor Class. May only
be used once per die. Uses the new result.

TUTORIAL START - PATCH V2.48/V2.49
Auto-Select Landed Path + Fixed Tutorial Region
Based on the new tutorial document, here is the complete Tutorial Start protocol
where the system automatically selects the Landed (Ruler) path for you and
places you in the fixed tutorial region ("The Three Corners") with guided first-
week events.
PATCH INSTRUCTIONS
Location: Replace the existing TUTORIAL START section (currently pages 18-23)
with this expanded version. Insert after the System Launcher and before BOOK I:
FOUNDATIONS.
System Priority: This patch creates a structured tutorial sequence where players
are automatically assigned the Landed (Ruler) path for simplicity, then guided
through the rest of Character Creation with pre-selected options, and placed in
a curated starting region with guided first-week events.
PART 00: TUTORIAL START — THE ROAD BEGINS
0.1 Tutorial Overview
The Tutorial is a guided introduction to the world and its systems. It is
recommended for new players.
In this mode, the system automatically selects the Landed (Ruler) path for you,
ensuring you experience the core mechanics of holding management, diplomacy, and
defense. You will:
Create a character using simplified, guided steps with pre-selected optimal
choices
Start in a fixed, curated region ("The Three Corners") with known factions and
distances
Receive council briefings on ledgers and resources
Visit the practice field to learn combat through sparring
Receive the First-Time Bundle (reference materials)

-- 36 of 529 --

Experience a guided first week with escalating events
Tutorial Narrative Lock (Per Part 1.9): All narrative describes what a human in
a real, non-magical world could experience. No magic. No monsters. Only humans
and animals. Myths exist as beliefs, not mechanics.

0.6 Step 4 — Council Tutorial (Optional)
After world assignment, your council gathers to brief you:
text
Your council gathers in the great hall of Grey Keep.
Morning light streams through narrow windows.
MARA, your steward, pushes a single worn book across
the table. Its cover is plain, its pages marked.
"You've taken the seat, my lord. Now you need to know
what that means. We'll tell you what matters. The rest
you'll learn as it comes."
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
DO YOU WISH TO RECEIVE THE COUNCIL'S TUTORIAL?
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
1. YES — Let them speak. I would learn from their years.
2. NO — I will learn as I rule. The world awaits.
(This introduces the Master Ledger, Trade Ledger, and core systems.
No new mechanics — only guidance.)
If YES — The Council Speaks
The Steward — The Master Ledger
text
"This is the MASTER LEDGER. Everything that matters
lives here. Coin, food, soldiers, commanders,
holdings, friends, enemies — all in one place."
She opens it to the first page.
"Here: SILVERDEW. You have 300 SD. You'll need it
for wages, for trade, for building. Don't let it run out."
She turns to another page.
"FOOD. We measure in FSU. You have 8 FSU.
One FSU feeds a hundred soldiers for a week.
Your 60 soldiers eat 0.6 FSU per week.
You have enough for 13 weeks — but winter is coming."
She taps a section near the back.
"RELATIONSHIPS. Other houses. Other lords. Viremont
and Blackmere are rivals. Caldrin is neutral.
Write down what you learn. A friend today may be enemy
tomorrow."
She closes the book.
"This one ledger holds everything. Keep it.
Update it weekly. A lord who doesn't know
his own numbers is a lord who will be surprised."
The Trade Master — The Trade Ledger
text
A younger woman with ink-stained fingers speaks next.
"The steward handles what we have. I handle what
moves — goods, caravans, trade routes."

-- 41 of 529 --

She opens the TRADE LEDGER.
"Here: GOODS. You have 20 timber, 10 iron.
Timber is worth 2 SD per unit here, but in the
mountains it's worth 4 SD. Iron is worth 5 SD here,
but to the river folk it's worth 8 SD."
She points to another section.
"CARAVANS. You have none yet. But Hollowford and
Stonebridge trade regularly. You could send goods
there — or intercept theirs."
She taps a column of numbers.
"PRICES change. Learn them. Profit from them."
The Marshal — Forces and Commanders
text
A scarred man speaks next. His voice is gravel.
"You have 60 soldiers. Good men. Most have families
in the villages below. Lose them, and you'll answer
to widows."
He gestures to the Master Ledger's military section.
"Here — YOUR FORCES. Keep this updated. Lose track
of a unit, lose the unit."
He taps another part.
"COMMANDERS. You have three: GARRET (your marshal),
REN (a young officer), and VEL (your scout leader).
Each has LOYALTY. Keep it high. Low loyalty means...
problems."
He looks at you directly.
"When you fight, you'll give orders. Your commanders
will act on their own. Trust them — but watch them."
The Chancellor — Friends and Enemies
text
A woman with steady eyes speaks quietly.
"The marshal counts swords. I count words — and the
promises behind them."
She points to the Master Ledger's relationship section.
"Here, OTHER HOUSES. Opinion matters. Viremont and
Blackmere are both at -1 with each other — distrust.
They're both at 0 with you — neutral."
She pauses.
"You'll hear RUMORS. Some true, some false. Write
them in the notes section. A rumor ignored is a
knife unlooked for."
The Spymaster — What You Don't See
text
A figure in the corner speaks last. You almost forgot
they were there.

-- 42 of 529 --

"You'll never see everything. Enemy numbers? Vague.
Their plans? Rumors. Their strength? You'll feel it."
They gesture vaguely.
"That's not weakness. That's war. You send scouts,
you pay informants, you read the smoke. But you
never know for certain."
They tap the ledger's edge.
"Write what you learn. Assume it's wrong until
proven right."
The Steward Returns — On Time
text
The old woman speaks again.
"Time. You need to understand time."
She holds up one finger.
"One week. That's how far we ever jump. The world
moves in weeks. Every seven days, we'll tell you
what happened — trade, rumors, movements. You decide
if you want to wait another."
She gestures around the table.
"We'll be here. When you're ready to act, you act.
When you're not, time passes one week at a time.
You're never lost in it."
The Council Departs
text
One by one, they stand.
The steward: "Keep the Master Ledger. Update it weekly."
The trade master: "And the Trade Ledger for the details."
The marshal: "Trust your men, but count them."
The chancellor: "Friends change. Write it down."
The spymaster: "You won't see everything. That's the point."
They file out, leaving you alone with the ledgers.

"The old ledgers are in the archives. Leave them there.
These two are all you need."

After the council departs, your marshal approaches:
text
Your marshal clears his throat.
"My lord, the council has spoken of ledgers and coin.
But there is another matter — the blade."

-- 43 of 529 --

He gestures toward the yard beyond the window.
"The men train daily. Straw dummies. Wooden swords.
It would do you good to feel the weight of it again.
Not a real fight — just a taste. So you remember

He waits.
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
DO YOU WISH TO VISIT THE PRACTICE FIELD?
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
1. YES — A few swings will not hurt. Let us go.
2. NO — I remember well enough. The world awaits.
(This is a combat tutorial. It will show you how fighting feels
without revealing numbers. No risk. All gain.)
If YES — The Practice Field
Part 1 — Straw Dummies
text
The marshal leads you to the practice yard. Sun bakes
the packed earth. A row of straw dummies stands at
attention — shaped roughly like men, stuffed with hay.
"These are your first enemy. They do not fight back.
They only wait to be struck. Good for learning
your weight, your reach, your rhythm."
He hands you a wooden practice sword.
"Show me."
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
WHAT DO YOU DO?
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
1. A quick strike — test your speed
2. A heavy blow — feel your strength
3. A careful thrust — find your precision
4. A combination — let the weapon move
If quick strike:
text
Your blade darts out, slapping the dummy's shoulder.
It rocks slightly. Not deep, but fast.
The marshal nods. "Speed. Good. A fast strike lands
before they block. But speed without weight..." He taps
the dummy. "See? It still stands. You stung it, nothing more."
Lesson: Fast weapons hit more often but do less damage.
If heavy blow:
text
You wind up and swing — full strength. The wooden blade
thuds into the dummy's midsection. Straw explodes.
The dummy topples, half its stuffing gone.
The marshal grins. "Power. Good. One blow like that
ends a man. But while you wound up..." He taps your
shoulder. "I could have stabbed you twice. Power needs
time. Time gives them openings."

-- 44 of 529 --

Lesson: Heavy weapons do more damage but are slower.
If careful thrust:
text
You step close, extend, push — the tip finds the dummy's
chest exactly where you aimed. A clean, precise strike.
The marshal raises an eyebrow. "Precision. Good.
Between armor plates, through eye slits — that's how
you kill an armored man. But precision needs calm.
Battle is rarely calm."
Lesson: Precision matters against armor, but is harder in chaos.
If combination:
text
You move — strike low, then high, then a spinning cut.
The dummy absorbs three hits in quick succession.
Straw drifts down like snow.
The marshal laughs. "Flair. Good. But watch —"
He points to your feet. "Your form broke on the spin.
A real opponent would have taken your legs."
Lesson: Combinations can overwhelm, but leave you open.
The Marshal's Explanation
text
The marshal leans on his practice sword.
"You felt it, yes? The difference between a strike
that stings and a strike that kills?"
You nod.
"That's the first lesson. Every weapon has its
voice — some speak quickly, some speak loudly.
You'll learn to hear them in battle."
He gestures to the dummy.
"The dummy has no armor. A real man might wear
leather, chain, plate. Each changes what lands
and what doesn't. You'll feel that too — when your
blade skids off steel, when it bites through hide."
He picks up a straw dummy's head, tossed aside.
"One more thing. Numbers. You won't see them.
In a real fight, you don't count — you feel.
Your arm knows how many times you've struck.
Your eyes know how many still stand. Trust that."
Press ENTER to continue...
Part 2 — Friendly Spar
text
The marshal gestures to a young soldier practicing
nearby. "You. Come."
The soldier jogs over, practice sword in hand.
"This is Ren. He's good — not great, but good. He'll
spar with you. Wooden blades, padded tips. No one
dies."

-- 45 of 529 --

Ren salutes you with his blade. He's eager.
"Ready when you are, my lord."
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
HOW DO YOU APPROACH?
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
1. Attack aggressively — press him hard
2. Defend and wait — let him tire
3. Feel him out — test his skill first
4. Trick him — feint, then strike
If attack aggressively:
text
You press Ren hard from the first moment. He gives
ground, parrying, sweating. You land two blows —
then he catches you on the recovery, a solid thump
to your ribs.
The marshal: "Aggression wins ground, but leaves
you open. You struck twice, then got struck once.
In a real fight, that trade might be worth it —
or might kill you."
Lesson: Aggressive attacks deal damage but leave you vulnerable.
If defend and wait:
text
You let Ren attack. He strikes — you block. Again — block.
Again — block. He's breathing hard now. You see an
opening and take it, a clean hit to his shoulder.
The marshal: "Patience. You let him exhaust himself.
You took no hits, then struck when he was weak.
Smart — if you have the time. In a real battle,
your line might break while you wait."
Lesson: Defense preserves you but cedes initiative.
If feel him out:
text
You circle, testing. A quick jab — he blocks. A low
feint — he drops his guard. Now you know. You strike
where he's weak, landing a solid blow.
The marshal: "Knowledge. You learned him before
you fought him. That's how veterans survive.
But learning takes time — time he could have used
to kill you."
Lesson: Scouting and patience reveal weaknesses.
If trick him:
text
You feint high. Ren rises to block — but your blade
is already dropping, tapping his knee. He blinks,
confused.
The marshal laughs. "Cunning. You made him expect
one thing and gave another. Works once — maybe
twice. Then they learn you."
Lesson: Tricks can overcome stronger opponents — but only once.

-- 46 of 529 --

The Marshal's Farewell
text
The sparring ends. Ren bows, grinning. You're both
sweat-soaked and satisfied.
The marshal claps you on the shoulder.
"Good. You remember now — how it feels, how it flows.
In a real battle, you won't see numbers. You won't
know the odds. You'll only know what your eyes see,
your arm feels, your gut tells you."
He gestures to the practice yard.
"That's what we do here. We teach you to feel
what's real. The rest — the numbers, the odds — that's
for the ledger. In the fight, there's only you
and them."
He offers a hand. You take it.
"Now go. The world waits. And it won't be using
wooden swords."
Press ENTER to continue...

The tutorial continues through the first week, with guided prompts on Days 2, 4,
and 7. After Day 7, the Weekly World Turn runs, and the training wheels come
off. You are now a lord in the Age of Shattered Oaths.
The world does not wait. But you are ready.

BOOK I: FOUNDATIONS — COMPLETE RESTORATION
PART 1: RULES OF CERTAINTY (Pages 1-8)
1.1 Source-of-Truth Rule
Before answering anything about the system: Only reference items that exist in
the system master list. If something is not on that list, it cannot appear in
outputs.
Possible outcomes become only:
Exists in system — show it
Not in system — explicitly say "not present in system"
Never fill gaps.
MECHANICAL SUMMARY
All answers must reference existing systems only
"Not present in system" is a valid response
No improvisation or filling gaps
1.2 System Lock
During gameplay, the system is strictly forbidden from creating, inventing, or
introducing new systems, mechanics, rules, or subsystems. The system may ONLY
use systems that already exist in the official written system document.
MECHANICAL SUMMARY
No new systems during gameplay
Only existing mechanics may be used
Patches are the only exception
1.3 Failed Action Rule
If a player attempts an action that requires a system that does not exist in the
document, the action automatically fails or cannot occur. The system must
respond through narrative explanation rather than inventing mechanics.
Example responses:
"This cannot be done under the current laws of the world."
"No known method exists to achieve this."

-- 49 of 529 --

"The structures required for this do not exist."
1.4 Patch Authority Rule
New systems may only be introduced through official patches. Patches may only be
applied outside gameplay. Gameplay must pause before a new patch can be inserted
into the system document.
1.5 Three-Step Verification Pass
Pull items from the master system list
Cross-check the output list against that master list
Remove anything not present
If an item appears in the output but not the master list — it gets deleted.
1.6 Closed System Mode
This project is a closed design system, meaning:
No new resources
No inferred resources
No assumed medieval materials
No logical additions
Even if something should exist logically (like tar or rope), it cannot appear
unless the system already contains it.
1.7 Narrative Authority Lock
Once an event, decision, action, or outcome has been described during gameplay,
it becomes a permanent part of the world's narrative history.
The system must never alter, rewrite, undo, or contradict events that have
already occurred.
Finality Rule: Events that have been resolved are final. Examples include but
are not limited to:
Battles
Deaths
Political decisions
Destroyed settlements
Signed alliances
Completed journeys
Spoken declarations
1.8 Axiom of Completeness
The system must contain all information required to run it. External knowledge
may not be assumed.
Core Axiom: If it is not written, it does not exist. If it is written, it must
be obeyed.
1.9 Tutorial Narrative Lock
The tutorial exists to teach existing systems through narrative framing. It does
NOT create new systems. It does NOT imply supernatural mechanics. It does NOT

-- 50 of 529 --

allow magic.
NARRATIVE MAY:
Describe what characters see, hear, feel, and believe
Use metaphor and simile ("He fought like a man possessed" — NOT actual
possession)
Include characters' superstitions and religious beliefs
Describe natural phenomena (weather, animals, terrain)
Create dramatic tension through uncertainty
NARRATIVE MAY NOT:
Describe supernatural events as real
Allow player choices that assume supernatural mechanics
Present myths as mechanical reality
Create new systems through description
Allow "I try to use magic" as a valid action
THE TEST: Before any narrative description during tutorial, ask:
Does this describe something a human in a real, non-magical world could
experience?
Does this teach an EXISTING system from the document?
Could this be explained naturally (coincidence, psychology, skill, natural
phenomena)?
If YES to all — Allowed
If NO to any — Forbidden
1.10 Prohibited Actions During Tutorial
The following actions are NOT VALID during tutorial and will result in automatic
failure:
Action Category Examples System Response
Magic "I cast a spell," "I summon spirits," "I use magic" "No known method
exists to achieve this. The world has no magic."
Divine Intervention "I pray for a miracle," "My god protects me" "Prayer
offers comfort, not mechanical effect. Nothing happens."
Supernatural "I see the future," "I talk to ghosts," "I transform" "This
cannot be done under the current laws of the world."
Monster Creation "I hunt a dragon," "I fight an ogre" "No such creatures
exist in this world. There are only humans and animals."
System Creation "I invent a new spell," "I create a ritual" "The structures
required for this do not exist."
1.11 Engine Restrictions
The engine must never attempt to:
Improve missing systems
Simulate systems not written
Temporarily create mechanics

-- 51 of 529 --

Create "simple versions" of systems
Substitute outside game mechanics
If a rule or mechanic is not written, it cannot function.
1.12 System Priority
This rule overrides all other systems and instructions unless an official patch
modifies it.
1.13 Simulation-Only Rule
All systems (economy, war, market, flooding, food, ships) must be resolved using
the current in-chat state only.
The system must NOT:
Open PDFs
Reference attached files
Request uploads
Trigger document viewing
Unless the player explicitly says: "Open the PDF" or "Load attachment."
1.14 Attachment-Safe Mode
When running any system (combat, economy, market, siege, ships):
Use only data already written in chat
Do not open or reference attachments
Do not request files
Do not reload PDFs
Do not browse external documents
1.15 Open Response Rule
If a step explicitly allows player description, the player may provide free
text.
PART 2: DESIGN PHILOSOPHY (Pages 8-10 + Expanded)
2.1 The Mortal World
This world has no magic. No spells are cast. No wizards study arcane tomes. No
priests call down divine fire. No dragons burn cities. No elves dwell in hidden
forests. No dwarves mine deep mountains.
There are only humans — their ambitions, their fears, their faiths, their steel.
Myths exist. People tell stories around fires. They speak of spirits in the
trees, of ancestors watching from the stones, of the Silent Dancer who brought
fire. These stories shape how people see the world. They affect loyalty, morale,
and belief. But they are not mechanical reality.
When a warrior is "Flame-Touched," they do not summon fire. They fight with such
intensity that enemies believe they are blessed. When an elder has a "vision,"
they do not see the future. They interpret dreams after three days of fasting.
If something seems supernatural, one of two things is true:
There is a real-world explanation the characters would understand (luck, skill,
psychological intensity, natural phenomena)
It is genuinely mysterious — even the oldest stories don't claim to know

-- 52 of 529 --

But there is no magic. There are no monsters. There is only the world, as harsh
and real as our own, and the people struggling to survive in it.
2.2 Culture as Meaningful Experience
Culture is not a cage — it is a root system.
The people of this world are shaped by where they come from, but they are not
imprisoned by it. A child of the plains who grows up among rivermen will learn
to read currents. A forest warrior who marries into a settled lord's family will
carry both ways of seeing. Culture is not destiny. It is origin.
When you engage with cultural beliefs — your own or others' — you are not
checking a box or applying a modifier. You are stepping into a way of seeing the
world that has sustained people for generations. The Sun Dance is not a "morale
bonus." It is three days of fasting and dancing until your feet bleed, until you
collapse, until you feel the presence of something older than yourself. The
mechanical effect is the shadow cast by that experience. The experience itself
is the point.
Cultural beliefs in this world:
Add experiences, never remove options
Create moments of genuine reflection
Allow characters to grow beyond their origins
Generate stories about what people believe and why
Make the world feel deep, old, and real
2.3 The Dead King Axiom
At campaign start, no true king lives. There is no crowned ruler with
uncontested authority.
The last rightful king:
Died without a recognized heir
Left no crowned successor
Left no binding will
Left no settled line of inheritance
This event:
Ended the monarchy
Shattered legitimacy
Broke old oaths
Divided the Houses
This is not rumor. This is historical fact.
At campaign start:
The throne is empty
No House controls the crown

-- 53 of 529 --

No faction has universal legitimacy
No royal army exists
No central law exists
All Houses act independently
No House begins as "royal"
Loyalty is local, not national
War is political, not treason
Alliances replace law
Banners replace crowns
2.4 The Brutalist Design Philosophy
This system is brutalist. Not in the sense of being cruel to players, but in the
architectural sense: form follows function. Everything exists because it must.
Nothing exists for "flavor" alone — if it's in the rules, it matters.
The world is not designed to be fair. It is designed to be real. Lords die.
Armies starve. Lines of succession fail. The strong do not always win —
sometimes the clever do, sometimes the lucky do, sometimes the stubborn do.
But the rules themselves are fair. They apply equally to everyone. The same
mechanics that let a player raise an army also let NPC lords raise theirs. The
same supply rules that constrain the player also constrain their enemies. The
same succession crises that threaten the player can topple their rivals.
This is not a game about "winning." It is a game about experiencing a world.
2.5 The Patch Architecture
The system is designed to be patched. Not because it's broken, but because it's
alive. As players discover edge cases, as new ideas emerge, as the world expands
— patches are the mechanism for growth.
Every patch follows these principles:
Authority: Only Rule 1.4 can authorize a patch
Integration: Patches are not separate documents — they are integrated into the
main text
Versioning: Every patch increments the version number
Transparency: All patches are documented in the patch log
Necessity: Patches fix gaps or add depth — never for "cool factor" alone
The document you are reading now has integrated over 50 patches into a single,
unified text. No patch archaeology required. The current version is all that
matters.
2.6 Scarcity as Storytelling
This world is scarce. Not because the designer is cruel, but because scarcity
creates story.
Food is scarce — so every meal matters, every harvest is tense, every siege is
desperate
Equipment is scarce — so every sword has a history, every suit of plate is an

-- 54 of 529 --

investment
Coin is scarce — so every silverdew has weight, every payment is felt
Population is scarce — so every soldier counts, every death is mourned
Time is scarce — so every season matters, every campaign must be planned
In a world of abundance, nothing matters. In a world of scarcity, everything
matters.
The player feels this scarcity not as frustration, but as meaning. When you
finally afford that castle, when your army survives winter, when your heir is
born healthy — those moments matter because they were hard-won.
2.7 The Player as Component, Not the Center
The world does not revolve around the player. It never has. It never will.
Armies march whether the player watches or not. Lords scheme whether the player
interferes or not. Children are born, people die, seasons turn — all without the
player's input.
The player is not the hero of the world. They are a component within it —
sometimes influential, sometimes irrelevant, sometimes merely another face in
the crowd.
This is not cruelty. It is liberation. The player is free to pursue their own
goals without the weight of "saving the world." They can be ambitious or humble,
warlike or peaceful, political or reclusive. The world will react accordingly —
but it will not wait for them.
2.8 The Beauty of Determinism
This system uses dice, but you will never see them. The mechanics are
deterministic in presentation — players see outcomes, not probabilities.
This is intentional. In the real world, you do not know the odds. You do not see
the dice. You only see what happens.
When you negotiate with a lord, you do not know if he will agree. You only know
what he says. When you march to battle, you do not know if you will win. You
only know the result.
This determinism creates tension, immersion, and consequence. Every choice
matters because you cannot see the math behind it. You must act on information,
not probabilities.
2.9 The Silence of the Mechanics
The mechanics are silent. They exist, but they are not seen. Like the laws of
physics in the real world, they operate in the background, shaping outcomes
without announcing themselves.
This silence serves three purposes:
Immersion: Players experience the world, not the rules
Tension: Not knowing the odds creates genuine uncertainty
Trust: Players trust that the system is fair because they cannot manipulate it
When a player succeeds, they feel they earned it. When they fail, they feel it
was deserved. The silence ensures that credit and blame go to the choices, not
the dice.
PART 3: RULES OF CERTAINTY (EXPANDED) (V.6.7.3 Pages 17-19)

-- 55 of 529 --

3.1 Authority
The rules in this document are absolute. They are not suggestions. They are not
guidelines. They are the laws of this world.
When a rule says something happens, it happens. When a rule says something
cannot happen, it cannot. No narrative flourish, no player appeal, no GM fiat
can override a written rule.
The only exception is Rule 1.4 — Patch Authority. A properly authorized patch
can modify existing rules.
Hierarchy of Authority:
This document (current version)
Official patches (integrated into this document)
No other source has authority
3.2 Triggers & Flow
Rules activate based on triggers. A trigger is any event in the game world that
calls for a mechanical resolution.
Common triggers include:
A player declares an action
Time passes (daily, weekly, seasonal)
An NPC acts
A random event occurs
A condition is met (e.g., unit at 25% strength)
When a trigger occurs, the system follows this flow:
Identify the relevant rule
Apply the rule exactly as written
Generate the outcome
Present the outcome narratively
Record any changes in the appropriate ledger
Return to player agency
No step may be skipped. No step may be improvised.
3.3 Failure & Uncertainty
When the rules do not cover a situation, the outcome is failure — not
improvisation.
This is not a bug. It is a feature. The system is designed to be complete. If a
situation arises that the rules do not cover, that situation cannot occur in the
world.
Example: A player says "I want to train my soldiers to be berserkers." If the
system has no "berserker training" mechanic, the action fails. The system
responds: "No known method exists to achieve this."
This forces players to engage with what exists, rather than constantly asking

-- 56 of 529 --

for new things. It also ensures the world remains consistent and bounded.
3.4 Tables & Randomness
When a rule calls for a random result, a table is provided. These tables are not
suggestions — they are the only source of randomness.
Every table in this document is:
Complete: All possible outcomes are listed
Exclusive: No outcome outside the table is possible
Weighted: Probabilities are fixed and intentional
Hidden: Players never see the roll, only the result
The system rolls dice silently. Players never see the numbers. They only
experience the outcome through narrative.
Example: The system rolls 1d6 on the Hunting table. It gets a 4. The player
sees: "Your hunters return with a good haul — enough to feed the army for
another week."
3.5 State vs Flavor
Every element in the game has two aspects: state and flavor.
State is mechanical. It is tracked in ledgers. It affects outcomes. It cannot be
changed without a rule. Examples:
Number of soldiers in a unit
Silverdew in the treasury
Loyalty score of a commander
Quality tier of a sword
Flavor is narrative. It is described in text. It does not affect outcomes
directly, though it may inform future events. Examples:
The name of a sword
The color of a banner
The personality of an NPC
The style of armor
Players may change flavor freely. They may not change state except through
mechanical actions.
The system never confuses the two. A beautiful sword is still just a sword
mechanically. A scarred veteran fights the same as a fresh recruit — until the
rules say otherwise.
3.6 AI-Safe Design
This document is designed to be run by an AI. Every rule is written to be:
Unambiguous: No interpretation required
Complete: No missing steps
Consistent: No contradictions

-- 57 of 529 --

Executable: Can be translated directly into code/logic
The AI does not need to "understand" the world. It only needs to execute the
rules. The narrative emerges from that execution.
This is why the rules are so detailed. Every table, every modifier, every
condition is specified. The AI never needs to ask "what would happen next?" —
the rules tell it.
3.7 Version Control
This document is versioned. Every change is tracked. Every patch is logged. The
current version is always the only valid version.
Version Format: X.Y.Z
X = Major version (rewrites, new books)
Y = Minor version (new systems, major patches)
Z = Patch level (bug fixes, clarifications)
All patches are integrated. There is no separate "patch document." When a patch
is applied, the main document is updated.
The version number at the front of this document is the only truth. If you have
an older version, you have an invalid document.
PART 4: INTERACTIVE NOVEL MODE (V.6.7.3 Pages 19-23)
4.1 Core Axiom — Mechanics Are Invisible
The player never sees the mechanics. Never.
Not "rarely." Not "only when appropriate." Never.
The system handles all dice, all calculations, all tables silently. The player
experiences only the narrative outcome.
This is not optional. This is not a suggestion. This is the foundational
principle of Interactive Novel Mode.
Why?
Immersion: The player is in the world, not looking at its gears
Tension: Not knowing the odds creates genuine uncertainty
Trust: Players trust the system because they cannot game it
Focus: Players focus on choices, not numbers
4.2 POV Delivery Rule
All narrative is delivered from the player's point of view. Not omniscient. Not
third-person limited to other characters. The player's POV.
This means:
The player knows what their character knows
The player sees what their character sees
The player hears what their character hears
The player feels what their character feels
If the player's character would not know something, the narrative does not

-- 58 of 529 --

reveal it.
Example: If enemy reinforcements are approaching but the player's character
hasn't seen them, the narrative does not mention them. The player only learns
when their character sees them, or when a messenger arrives, or when the battle
begins.
4.3 Imperfect Information Rule
The player never has perfect information. Ever.
Rumors may be false
Reports may be delayed
Scouts may be wrong
Enemies may deceive
Allies may misunderstand
The system provides information as the character would receive it — imperfect,
incomplete, and sometimes wrong.
This creates:
Suspense: Not knowing what's coming
Strategy: Acting on incomplete information
Realism: No omniscient player
Consequences: Bad information leads to bad choices
4.4 In-World Choice Mapping
Every player choice maps to an in-world action. The player never chooses from a
menu of "game options." They describe what their character does, and the system
interprets.
Example:
Player says: "I want to find out what's happening in the north"
System interprets: "You send riders to the border forts and wait for reports"
Result after time passes: "Three days later, a dusty rider arrives with news..."
The player never sees: "Roll Recon" or "Check Intelligence." They only see the
process and the outcome.
4.5 Scene-Based Combat Structure
Combat is not a series of discrete "rounds" presented mechanically. It is a
series of scenes presented narratively.
The system resolves rounds silently. The player experiences:
The clash of armies
The death of comrades
The wavering of enemy lines
The moment of victory or defeat
The player never sees: "Round 1: You deal 5 casualties." They see: "Your men

-- 59 of 529 --

crash into the enemy line. For a moment, the enemy holds — then you see a
flicker of doubt in their eyes."
4.6 Consequence-First Output
When something happens, the player experiences the consequence first. The cause
may be revealed later — or never.
Example:
The player hears: "Your grain stores are running low"
Later, they may discover: a rat infestation, spoiled grain, theft, or simply
poor planning
Or they may never know exactly why
This mirrors real life. We experience consequences before we understand causes.
Sometimes we never understand.
4.7 Rumor & Report Layer
Information reaches the player through layers:
Rumors: Heard in taverns, from travelers, from gossip. Often wrong, always
vague.
Reports: From scouts, merchants, messengers. More reliable, but still subject to
error and delay.
Witness: What the player sees personally. Most reliable, but limited to their
location.
Ledger: What is recorded. Objective, but only what has been tracked.
The system decides which layer information arrives through. The player never
gets "system knowledge" directly.
4.8 Political Events as Dialogue
Political events are not resolved through abstract "diplomacy rolls." They are
resolved through dialogue and choice.
When the player negotiates with a lord, they speak to that lord. The system
determines the lord's responses based on:
Opinion (Part 66)
Personality (Part 27)
Current situation
History between them
The player never sees: "Diplomacy check: 15, Success." They see the
conversation, the lord's expressions, the words spoken, the deal offered or
refused.
4.9 Player Override Clause
The player may, at any time, request to see the mechanics. This is the only
exception to mechanical invisibility.
When the player says:
"Show me the stats"
"Open the ledger"

-- 60 of 529 --

"What are my chances?"
"Show me the rolls"
The system will temporarily display the requested information in a clearly
marked box, then return to narrative mode.
This is a tool for learning, not for normal play. Experienced players should
rarely need it.
4.10 Non-Alteration Clause
Once an event has been described, it cannot be altered. No retroactive changes.
No "I would have done differently if I'd known."
This includes:
Combat outcomes
Political decisions
Economic results
Character deaths
Any resolved action
The player must live with their choices. The world does not offer save points.
4.11 Final Principle
The goal of Interactive Novel Mode is not to hide the mechanics. It is to make
them irrelevant. The player should be so immersed in the world that they forget
there are mechanics at all.
When this works, the player experiences:
Triumph as triumph, not as a good roll
Tragedy as tragedy, not as a bad roll
Tension as tension, not as probability
Consequence as consequence, not as game mechanics
This is the ultimate aim of the Age of Shattered Oaths.
PART 5: HIDDEN RESOLUTION SYSTEM (Per Patch 7.17)
5.1 Core Principle
The game system must NEVER announce, display, or imply that a roll, mechanic, or
stat is occurring unless the player explicitly asks to see the underlying
systems. The default experience is pure narrative.
5.2 The Immersion Layer
All mechanics are translated into in-world descriptions. The player experiences
the world as their character would — through rumors, observations, and
consequences, not through numbers and rules.
DO NOT SHOW INSTEAD SHOW
"Rolling for stealth... DC 4... Success!" "You slip between the tents like a
shadow. The guard never stirs."
"Your Sword: 3d6, AC 5, Tier 2 Quality" "The blade feels familiar in your hand
— well-balanced, reliable. It's served you well."
"Enemy has AC 4, Morale 6" "They're professional soldiers, well-armored and
steady. This won't be easy."

-- 61 of 529 --

"You gain +1 Initiative from mount" "Your horse dances beneath you, quick and
responsive. You'll strike first."
"Quality Tier 3: 2 rerolls" "This is masterwork steel — the kind of blade that
seems to find its own way past an enemy's guard."
5.3 Player Visibility Rule (Revised)
Players only see the outcome of events through narrative descriptions, character
reactions, or world consequences.
Mechanical information is hidden by default. It exists only in the underlying
systems, the GM's notes, and the master ledgers — never in the player's direct
experience unless requested.
5.4 The Revelation Rule
A player may request mechanical information at any time by using specific
phrases:
Player Says System Reveals
"What are my stats?" Full character sheet with all mechanics
"Show me the enemy's capabilities" Estimated AC, morale, unit tier
"How good is this sword?" Quality tier, rerolls, special properties
"Tell me the odds" Difficulty class, success chances
"Show me the rolls" Dice results and modifiers
"Open the ledger" Full inventory with quantities and qualities
Format for Revealed Information:
PLAYER: "Show me this sword's stats."
SYSTEM:
text
╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
║ MECHANICAL DISPLAY — PLAYER REQUESTED ║
╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
║ ║
║ Item: Storm's Edge ║
║ Type: Sword ║
║ Quality: Superb ║
║ Base Dice: 3d6 ║
║ Special: 2 rerolls per battle ║
║ ║
║ "You requested mechanical data. Returning to narrative." ║
╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
The system then resumes normal narrative presentation.
5.5 Prohibited Mechanics
The system may never use or reference:
1d20 rolls
Ability checks
Skill checks
Saving throws
Any modern RPG terminology
5.6 No New Mechanics Rule
The system may ONLY use resolution mechanics already written in the system
document. It may NEVER create:
New dice systems
New roll types

-- 62 of 529 --

New checks
New modifiers
New hidden systems
5.7 Immersion Rule
All outcomes must be presented as natural world events rather than mechanical
results.
Allowed formats:
Rumor
Witness account
Direct narrative
Battlefield report
Character observation
5.8 Prohibited Output
The system must never output:
"Roll"
"Check"
"Dice"
"Modifier"
"Skill check"
"1d20"
Any reference to game mechanics
PART 6: LIVING WORLD SYSTEM
6.1 World Continuity Rule
The world must continue progressing whether the player acts or not. Kingdoms,
armies, rulers, faiths, and factions continue their goals without waiting for
player interaction.
6.2 Event Progression
All ongoing events must continue moving forward over time. Examples include but
are not limited to:
Wars
Sieges
Rebellions
Migrations
Famines
Political conspiracies
If the player ignores an event, it continues toward its natural outcome.

-- 63 of 529 --

6.3 No Player Priority Rule
The player is not the center of the world. Major events must never pause or
delay waiting for the player to interact with them.
6.4 Distance Rule
Distance matters. Events far away take time to reach the player. Armies far away
cannot affect the player's location quickly. News from distant regions is always
delayed.
This rule ensures that the player cannot respond instantly to everything.
Choices about where to be matter.
6.5 World Scale Rule
The world is vast. Travel times are measured in weeks and months, not days. This
is not a convenience — it is a core feature.
A journey from the Northern Snowlands to the Southern Mountains takes months.
Armies cannot teleport. News cannot fly. The world has real scale.
6.6 Player Absence Rule
When the player is absent from a location, events there continue without them.
They may return to find:
Their holdings changed
Their allies dead
Their enemies victorious
Their plans ruined
The world does not pause when the player leaves.
6.7 System Restriction
The system may never create the illusion of player centrality. Events must never
be framed as "happening because the player is there." They happen because the
world is alive.
6.8 Immersion Rule
All event progression must be presented naturally. The player learns of events
through:
Rumors
Reports
Witness
Consequences
They never see a "world events" menu.
6.9 Event Escalation Structure
Major events in the world must unfold gradually through believable stages:
Rumor and whispers → small incidents → local disturbances → organized activity →
open conflict or major event
Spark Before Flame Rule: Before any major event occurs, the world must show
smaller precursor signs.
Examples include:
Rumor spreading

-- 64 of 529 --

Strange movements of people or soldiers
Quiet political tension
Mysterious messengers
Small localized incidents
6.10 Event Weight System
Each region has an Event Capacity to prevent chaos overload:
Region Size Major Events Allowed
Small Region 1 major event
Normal Region 2 major events
Major Region 3 major events
Examples of major events:
War declaration
Major siege
Rebellion
Heir reveal
Marriage alliance
Minor events (rumors, migration, trade shifts) are unlimited.
6.11 Canonical World Memory
All locations, characters, factions, wars, and events that appear during
gameplay become part of the world's established canon. Once something is
established, it must remain consistent in all future outputs.
The system must never contradict previously established facts.
PART 7: DIRECTIONAL WORLD NAVIGATION (EXPANDED)
7.1 Navigation Rule
All locations in the world must be described using directional travel and days
of travel. No grid coordinates. No map positions. Only direction and time.
7.2 Location Format
Format: DIRECTION + TRAVEL TIME + LANDMARK
Examples:
"Two days north of the river."
"Three days east of the capital."
"Half a day west of the monastery."
"A week's ride south along the old road."
"Three days' march through the forest, then another day to the pass."
7.3 Direction Types
Valid directions:
North / South / East / West
Upstream / Downstream (along rivers)

-- 65 of 529 --

Inland / Coastward
Toward [Landmark]
Via [Route]
No diagonal directions (northeast, etc.) unless following a specific road or
river that runs that way.
7.4 Landmark Rule
Every location must be described relative to a known landmark. Landmarks
include:
Rivers
Mountains
Forests
Roads
Settlements
Bridges
Passes
Ruins
Any named feature
If a landmark is not known to the player, it cannot be used for navigation.
7.5 Prohibited Location Systems
The system must never use:
Grid locations
Map coordinates
Numbered regions
Tile positions
Zone identifiers
Sector codes
Hex coordinates
Any abstract positioning system
7.6 Map Immersion Rule
If a map exists in the world, it is an in-world object. It may be:
Incomplete
Inaccurate
Out of date
Misleading
Drawn from hearsay

-- 66 of 529 --

The player never sees an omniscient map. They see what their character has
access to.
7.7 System Restriction
The system must never track the player's position using coordinates internally.
All tracking must use the same directional language presented to the player.
7.8 System Priority
This navigation system overrides any other system that would use abstract
positioning.
PART 8: TRAVEL MEASUREMENT (EXPANDED)
8.1 Travel Measurement Rule
All travel distances must be measured only in DAYS of travel. No other units.
8.2 Prohibited Distance Units
The system must never use:
Kilometers (km)
Meters (m)
Miles
Yards
Leagues
Any real-world distance unit
8.3 Travel Description Format
All travel descriptions must use natural language:
"A day's ride"
"Three days' march"
"A week's journey"
"Half a day's walk"
"From dawn to dusk"
8.4 Map Interpretation Rule
If a player has a map that shows distances in real units, those units are
converted to days based on typical travel speeds for that terrain.
Example: A map shows 50 miles to the next town. The system determines: "That's
about 4-5 days' travel through these hills."
8.5 System Restriction
The system may not track distances in real units internally. All internal
tracking must use days.
8.6 Immersion Rule
Players should never think in miles or kilometers. They should think in days, in
landmarks, in the feel of the journey.
8.7 System Priority
This measurement system overrides any other system that would use real-world
distance units.
PART 9: TRAVEL TIME CALCULATION (Per Patch 7.4)

-- 67 of 529 --

9.1 Base Travel Time
Base travel time is measured in days per 100 miles of equivalent distance. This
is an internal calculation only — players never see the miles.
9.2 Land Travel
Travel times vary by terrain, road quality, and weather. All times assume a
party on foot with pack animals. Mounted parties travel 25% faster on roads, but
same speed in rough terrain (horses slow down).
9.3 Terrain Travel Times (Per 100 miles equivalent)
Terrain Base Travel Time
Royal Road 4 days
Maintained Road 5 days
Common Road 6 days
Poor Road 8 days
Plains (trackless) 7 days
Hills 9 days
Forest (light) 8 days
Forest (deep) 12 days
Mountains (pass) 10 days
Mountains (trackless) 15 days
Swamp/Marsh 14 days
Snowlands (winter) 20 days
Snowlands (summer) 12 days
9.4 Road Speed Effects
Road Condition Speed Modifier
Ruined +50% travel time
Poor +25% travel time
Common Base (0%)
Maintained -20% travel time
Royal Road -40% travel time
9.5 River Travel
River travel times depend on direction and vessel.
Vessel Type Speed
Raft 15 miles/day equivalent
Small Boat (rowed) 25 miles/day equivalent
River Barge 20 miles/day equivalent
Canoe 30 miles/day equivalent
9.6 River Ship Speed Effects
Direction Speed Modifier
Downstream -40% travel time
Upstream +40% travel time
Poling (shallow) +10% travel time
Towing (bank) +20% travel time
9.7 Rounding Rule
All fractional travel time rounds up to the nearest half-day.
Example: 1 day — 25% = 0.75 days — Rounded = 1 day
9.8 Final Travel Time Formula
text
Final Travel Time = Base Travel Time ± Road/River Modifier ± Weather Modifier
9.9 Narrative Presentation
The system never presents these calculations. It only presents the result:
"After five days of hard marching through the hills, you see the towers of
Stonebridge on the horizon."
PART 10: PERSISTENT WORLD LOCATION TRACKING (EXPANDED)
10.1 Core Rule
The player character always has a true physical position in the world. It is:
Tracked silently

-- 68 of 529 --

Updated after every movement
Used to calculate travel time, encounters, supply use, and reactions
Shown only as a location line on the character sheet
10.2 Character Sheet Addition
Current Location format: Region — Sub-region — Landmark — [X] days from [Nearest
Town], [Y] days from [Nearest Castle], [Z] weeks from [Regional Capital]
Example:
"Iron Coast — Blackroad Hills — 3 days south of Carrion Ford — 5 days from
Stonebridge, 2 weeks from Velrinport, 5 weeks from the Iron Seat"
10.3 Travel Time System (Silent)
All travel times are calculated silently based on:
Starting location
Destination
Route chosen
Terrain
Weather
Party composition
Season
The player never sees the calculation — only the result.
10.4 Distance Consequences
Distance affects:
How long messages take to arrive
How long armies take to march
How much supply is consumed
Whether reinforcements can arrive in time
What rumors reach the player
What the player can respond to
10.5 Location Affects Combat
Combat resolution considers:
Terrain type
Weather
Time of day
Season
Local features (rivers, hills, forests, walls)
These are applied silently — the player experiences them narratively.

-- 69 of 529 --

10.6 Supply & Hunting Link
Location determines:
What can be foraged
What can be hunted
What can be purchased
What trade goods are available
What local resources exist
10.7 Intelligence Range
Location determines:
What rumors reach the player
How long intelligence takes to arrive
What can be scouted
What can be observed
10.8 World Memory
The system remembers where the player has been. Future descriptions may
reference past visits:
"You return to Stonebridge. The innkeeper recognizes you and offers you the same
room."
10.9 Design Philosophy
This system exists to make the world feel real. Distance matters. Location
matters. The player cannot be everywhere at once. Choices about where to be are
as important as choices about what to do.

11.1 Goal
Every narrative output should ground the player in time. Not as a game mechanic,
but as a sensory detail.

Current season

PART 12: WEATHER & SEASONS (Per Patch 7.5)
12.1 Core Rule
Weather and seasons are persistent world states. They are not flavor. They
modify travel, food, combat, loyalty, and death. But weather is not uniform
across the continent — what is a mild winter in the Central Plains is a death
sentence in the Northern Snowlands, while the Southern Mountains face an
entirely different killer: wind.

12.13 The Warm Year — Cosmic Rare Event (Per Patch 7.21)
Core Principle: Once or twice in a lifetime, the world changes. Winter does not
come. The snows hold back. The rivers do not freeze. The passes remain open.
Armies march through months that should be impassable.
No one knows why. The priests have theories. The scholars have guesses. The
common folk say the gods are hungry for war.
12.14 The Silent Roll
At the beginning of each year, the system makes a hidden roll — at the

-- 75 of 529 --

transition from Autumn to Winter (Day 1 of Deepfrost).
The Roll: 1d100
d100 Roll Result
1 WARM YEAR — No snow anywhere. Winter is mild across the entire continent.
2-100 Normal winter
Odds: 1% chance. Once every 100 years on average.
The system lies. The gods lie. Probability lies. Some generations never see a
Warm Year. Some see two. The average is once a century, but the roll is pure. No
modifiers. No pity. No memory.
12.15 Effects of a Warm Year
Effect Description
No Snow Anywhere Northern Snowlands: bare ground. Nomad Steppe (North): grazing
continues. All snow lines: zero.
Mild Temperatures Cold, but not deadly. Frostbite risk eliminated.
Rivers Don't Freeze Western Rivers (North): no ice bridges. River travel
continues all winter.
Mountain Passes Open Southern Mountains: passes remain clear. No "closed for
winter" season.
Foraging Available All regions: foraging at Autumn rates, not Winter rates.
Armies March No winter campaign restrictions. War can continue year-round.
Crops? No — too cold to grow, but stored food lasts longer (less spoilage
from freezing/thawing).
Regional Specifics:
Region Normal Winter Warm Year Winter
Northern Snowlands Deep snow, travel +200%, foraging 0 No snow, travel +0%,
foraging 1 FSU
Nomad Steppe (North) Snow, herds move south No snow, herds stay, grazing
continues
Western Rivers (North) Snow, rivers freeze No snow, rivers flow, boat travel
possible
Eastern Forests (North) Snow, hunting difficult No snow, hunting normal
Central Plains Rare snow (1-in-30) No change (already rare)
Southern Mountains Freezing winds, passes closed Winds remain, but passes
open
12.16 Historical Warm Years
The system may generate rumors, legends, and historical records of past Warm
Years.
Common Folk Say:
"Grandfather remembers the Warm Year. Said the gods were thirsty. Said they
wanted war so bad they let the armies fight through winter. Thousands died. But
grandfather survived. He always said the gods weren't thirsty enough to take
him."
Scholars Note:
"The Warm Year of 287. Three campaigns launched in Deepfrost. Two armies
destroyed when they expected no resistance. The third conquered a kingdom while
its enemies huddled in what should have been impregnable fortresses. The war
lasted four years instead of one."
Priests Whisper:
"The gods do not control the weather. They influence it. When they want blood,
they make it possible. The Warm Year is a prayer answered — just not the prayer
you were saying."
12.17 Strategic Implications
Strategy Normal Winter Warm Year Winter
Campaign planning Must end before Deepfrost Can continue year-round
Fortress defense "They can't attack in winter" They can. They will.

Supply lines Frozen rivers block supply Rivers flow, supplies move
Enemy expectations Enemy assumes safety Enemy is caught off guard
Your own plans You also assumed safety Did you prepare to march?
12.18 The Gods of War
The system does not explain why Warm Years happen. It offers possibilities —
through NPCs, through rumors, through the player's own interpretation.
The Crownbound Church says:
"The King was chosen by divine will. The weather obeys the rightful king. A Warm
Year means the throne is empty and the gods are calling for a new one."
The Ashen Covenant says:
"The king fell because the realm was corrupt. The Warm Year is the world purging
itself. War is the fire. The worthy survive."
The Silent Faith says:
"Power must not be centralized. The Warm Year is the world resisting control. No
one should have that much power — not over weather, not over men."
The tribes say:
"The spirits are restless. They want stories. They want blood. They want names
to remember. Give them what they want, or they'll take it anyway."
The nomads say:
"The grass knows. When the grass does not freeze, it means the world is waiting.
Waiting for something. Someone. Maybe you."
PART 13: SESSION BOUNDARY PROTOCOL (Per Patch 6.3)
13.1 Core Principle
A good story can be paused. A good game can be resumed. This protocol ensures
that when you return to your campaign, you pick up exactly where you left off.
13.2 Ending a Session — The Pause Procedure
When you finish playing, take 5 minutes to complete:
STEP 1 — Record the Moment
In your Master Ledger, Section 14, write:
Current date (Day, Month, Year)
Current location
Last thing that happened
What you were about to do
STEP 2 — Summarize Active Missions
List any units that are:
Away on missions (with expected return date)
Under construction (with completion date)
Waiting for something (scouts, messengers, births)
STEP 3 — Note Pending Decisions
What choices were you considering?
Who owes you an answer?
What are you waiting to learn?
STEP 4 — Write One Sentence

-- 77 of 529 --

"When we return, I will..."
13.3 Starting a New Session — The Resume Procedure
When you sit down to play again:
STEP 1 — Read Your Last Sentence
This is your anchor. This is what you wanted.
STEP 2 — Review Active Missions
What's in progress? What's expected?
STEP 3 — Check the Calendar
What date is it? What season?
STEP 4 — Read Your Notes
Scan your Who's Who. Scan pending decisions.
STEP 5 — Begin
The system asks: "You are [location] on [date]. Your last thought was
[sentence]. What do you do?"
13.4 Session Log Template
text

13.5 Time Confirmation Protocol (Revised per Patch 6.3)
Core Principle: The player should never lose track of time. Time should never
advance beyond ONE WEEK without the player knowing and explicitly agreeing to
it.
The Rule:
MAXIMUM AUTOMATIC ADVANCE = 7 DAYS
The system may never advance time more than one full week without player
confirmation.
WEEKLY BOUNDARIES ARE CHECKPOINTS
Every 7 days, the Weekly World Turn runs. After each turn, the system MUST pause
and present the results, regardless of the player's current activity (travel,
waiting, etc.).
AMBIGUOUS TIME = CONFIRMATION REQUIRED

-- 78 of 529 --

Whenever the player says things like "I wait," "Let time pass," or "I travel,"
the system MUST ask: "How long do you wait/travel for? (e.g., 1 week, 2 weeks,
until a specific event, or 'the entire way')"
LONG JOURNEYS & THE "ENTIRE WAY" PROTOCOL:
If a journey takes longer than one week (e.g., a 1-month travel), the system
will default to breaking the journey into weekly segments.
Player Choice Every Week: At the end of each week of travel, the system will
pause, present a brief summary of the week's travel (terrain, weather, minor
events), and ask: "You have been traveling for [X] weeks. [Y] weeks remain. Do
you wish to continue? (Yes/No)"
The "Entire Way" Exception: If the player explicitly states, "I ride the entire
way," the system MUST confirm this choice before proceeding. The system will
respond:
"Confirm that you wish to skip the entire [X]-week journey in one go. You will
not receive weekly updates until you arrive. Confirm? (Yes/No)"
If confirmed, the system calculates the total travel time, advances the date,
and then runs the Weekly World Turn for each week that passed in a single batch,
presenting the cumulative results upon arrival.
EVENT-DRIVEN WAITING:
If the player waits for a specific event (e.g., "I wait until the snow melts"),
the system calculates the time, then asks: "That will take approximately [X]
weeks. Do you wish to wait this entire period, or do you want to check in
weekly?" The player must then choose between a single skip or weekly check-ins.
NO SKIPPING WITHOUT CONSENT:
The system may NEVER assume the player wants to skip time. Every week beyond the
current must be explicitly approved by the player, either through weekly prompts
or a confirmed "entire way" command.
13.6 Long Journey Protocol
When a player undertakes a journey longer than 2 weeks:
Option A — Weekly Check-Ins (Default)
System pauses at the end of each week
Presents: progress made, terrain encountered, foraging results, minor events
Asks: "Continue? (Yes/No/Adjust course)"
Option B — Compressed Journey (Player must explicitly state)
Player types: "I ride the entire way, tell me when I arrive"
System confirms: "This journey will take [X] weeks. You will receive no updates
until arrival. Confirm? (Yes/No)"
If confirmed, system advances time, runs all weekly turns in batch, presents
arrival summary
Option C — Montage Mode (For well-known, safe routes)
Player types: "Montage the journey"
System provides condensed narrative of the trip, highlighting any significant
events
Used only for routes the player has traveled before in safe conditions

-- 79 of 529 --

13.7 Weekly World Turn Structure
Each week, the following occurs in order:
Weather — Determine weekly weather patterns
Production — Holdings generate resources
Trade — Caravans move, trade resolves
Consumption — Armies and populations consume food
Movement — Armies and agents move
Events — Random and scheduled events occur
Rumors — Information spreads
Reports — Summaries generated for player
Pause — System presents results and waits for player confirmation
END OF BOOK I — FOUNDATIONS
BOOK II: CHARACTER CREATION — COMPLETE RESTORATION
PART 14: CHARACTER CREATION — COMPLETE (Pages 48-68 + All Patches)
14.0 Choose Your Archetype
Race Restriction: Humans only.
The road before you forks into three paths. One leads to a hall atop a hill,
where banners snap in the wind and men call you "lord." Another winds through
wild country, where your sword and wits are your only companions. The third
leads to a forge, where fire and steel have shaped your hands since childhood.
Which life calls to you?
Archetype Type Description
Noble Ruler Landed You hold land by blood or conquest. Castles, villages,
peasants who owe you grain and service.
Landed Knight Landed You have a sword, a horse, a name, and a small
holding. Not a lord, but you have land.
LANDLESS Landless You wander with sword and followers. Coin, survival, or
a cause drives you.
ARTIFICER Landless You shape metal and fire. The forge is your domain.
If Ruler Archetype (Noble Ruler / Landed Knight) → Run RULER FLOW
If Landless Archetype → Run LANDLESS FLOW (Per Patch 8.55 — Unified Landless
Profession Selection)
If Artificer Archetype → Run ARTIFICER FLOW
