PART 61: WAR MARSHAL & GRAND ARMY SYSTEM (Pages 228-238)
61.1 Core Principle
When multiple armies unite under a single commander for a single purpose — a
great invasion, a desperate defense, a crusade — that commander becomes a War
Marshal. The army becomes a Grand Army.
A War Marshal is not a king. They do not hold land or collect taxes. They hold
something rarer: the trust of other commanders, the authority to coordinate
multiple forces, the weight of a shared purpose.
61.2 War Marshal Requirements
A commander may be declared War Marshal if:
Requirement Details
Multiple Armies Must command at least 2 separate armies (not just units)
Commander Approval All other army commanders must agree (Loyalty check or
negotiation)
Shared Purpose The armies must have a common enemy or objective
Reputation Commander's reputation ≥ 5 OR all subordinate commanders have
Loyalty ≥ 3
Banner Tier Commander's personal banner Tier ≥ 3
A War Marshal may be:
Self-declared (if conditions met)
Elected by council of commanders
61.3 Grand Army Structure — Command Hierarchy
text
┌─────────────────┐
│ WAR MARSHAL │
│ (Overall Command)│
└────────┬────────┘
│
┌─────────────────┼─────────────────┐
│ │ │
▼ ▼ ▼
┌───────────┐ ┌───────────┐ ┌───────────┐
│ GENERAL 1 │ │ GENERAL 2 │ │ GENERAL 3 │
│ (Army A) │ │ (Army B) │ │ (Army C) │
└─────┬─────┘ └─────┬─────┘ └─────┬─────┘
│ │ │
┌─────┴─────┐ ┌─────┴─────┐ ┌─────┴─────┐
│COMMANDER 1│ │COMMANDER 3│ │COMMANDER 5│
│COMMANDER 2│ │COMMANDER 4│ │COMMANDER 6│
└───────────┘ └───────────┘ └───────────┘
61.4 Ranks Defined
Rank Commands Responsibilities
War Marshal All armies Overall strategy, final decisions, coordinates between
armies
General 1 army (2+ units) Tactical command of their army, reports to War
Marshal
Commander 1 unit Leads their unit, follows General's orders
61.5 Grand Army Size Tiers
Tier Armies Total Soldiers Command Complexity
Small Grand Army 2-3 armies 500-1,500 -1 to coordination
Standard Grand Army 4-6 armies 1,500-5,000 0 coordination
Large Grand Army 7-10 armies 5,000-15,000 +1 to coordination DC
Epic Grand Army 11+ armies 15,000+ +2 to coordination DC
61.6 The War Council
Before a Grand Army can form, a War Council must be held. All army commanders
gather to:

-- 193 of 529 --

Declare the common purpose
Elect or confirm the War Marshal
Establish the chain of command
61.7 Commander Approval Roll
For each potential subordinate commander, roll to determine if they accept the
War Marshal:
Base DC: 4
Modifier Effect
War Marshal's Reputation ≥ 6 -1 DC
War Marshal's Banner Tier ≥ 4 -1 DC
Shared enemy -1 DC
Previous successful cooperation -1 DC
Personal grudge +2 DC
Different faith +1 DC
Ambitious commander (Priority: Glory) +1 DC
Cautious commander (Temperament: Wary) +1 DC
Roll 1d6 + modifiers:
Success (≥ DC): Commander accepts and serves loyally
Failure: Commander refuses or demands terms
61.8 Demands of Refusing Commanders
If a commander refuses, they may demand:
A greater share of loot (+10-20%)
First choice of captured territory
Their army to act independently (not under direct command)
A specific role in the battle (vanguard, reserve, etc.)
Payment upfront (1d6 × 100 SD)
To be War Marshal instead (if they have support)
61.9 The War Marshal's Authority
Once confirmed, the War Marshal gains:
Authority Effect
Strategic Command Sets overall battle plan, chooses when and where to engage
Coordination Bonus +1 to all initiative rolls for the Grand Army
Morale Boost +1 Morale for all units (they fight for a united purpose)
Loot Distribution Decides how spoils are divided (must be fair or face
consequences)
Final Say Can override individual commander decisions in critical moments
61.10 Grand Army in Battle — Initiative Order
When a Grand Army fights, each army operates as a separate entity but can
coordinate.
Initiative Order:
War Marshal's army
All other armies (roll initiative within this group)
61.11 Coordination Actions

-- 194 of 529 --

Instead of normal actions, a General may:
Coordination Action Effect
Follow War Marshal's Lead Matches War Marshal's action; +1 to initiative
next round
Coordinate with Another Army Both armies gain +1d6 when attacking same target
Hold Reserve Army does not act this round; can respond to enemy moves (+2
initiative next round)
Request Support Ask War Marshal for reinforcements; War Marshal may commit 1
unit
Signal Retreat All armies aware of withdrawal plans; Coordinated withdrawal
easier
61.12 The War Marshal's Special Actions
The War Marshal may choose one special action per battle:
War Marshal Action Effect
Grand Charge All armies with Aggressive commanders gain +2d6 on next attack
Feigned Retreat One army pretends to flee; if enemy pursues, all other armies
gain Ambush round
Concentrated Assault Focus 2+ armies on a single enemy army; +3d6 total (max
12d6)
Defensive Line All armies gain +1 AC for 2 rounds
Rally One army that failed morale immediately rallies (no retreat)
61.13 Command Range
A War Marshal can only coordinate armies within:
Terrain Command Range
Open plains Visual range (all armies visible)
Rolling hills 2 armies within sight
Forest Adjacent armies only
Mountains Must have messengers (1 round delay)
Night/darkness Reduced by half
If an army is out of command range, its General acts independently that round.
61.14 War Marshal's Personal Command Capacity
The War Marshal's personal Command Capacity (see Part 39) applies only to their
own army — the troops they directly lead. This represents their ability to
control their immediate forces.
61.15 Strategic Command Capacity
In addition to personal capacity, a War Marshal has a Strategic Command Capacity
representing how many total soldiers they can effectively coordinate across all
armies:
text
Strategic Capacity = War Marshal's Personal Capacity × Number of Subordinate
Generals
Example: A War Marshal with Personal Capacity 2,000 commanding 3 Generals:
Strategic Capacity = 2,000 × 3 = 6,000 total troops across all armies
Each General leads their own army (using their own capacities)
61.16 Exceeding Strategic Capacity
If total Grand Army size exceeds Strategic Capacity:
Excess Penalty
Up to 25% over -1 to all coordination rolls
26-50% over -2 to coordination, orders delayed
51%+ over Coordination impossible — armies act independently
61.17 Banner Tier and Strategic Capacity
A War Marshal's Banner Tier enhances their ability to coordinate:
Banner Tier Strategic Capacity Bonus
Tier 3 +10% to Strategic Capacity

-- 195 of 529 --

Tier 4 +25% to Strategic Capacity
Tier 5 +50% to Strategic Capacity
61.18 Unified Supply
A Grand Army must coordinate supply across all armies. Track:
Resource Tracking Method
Total Food Sum of all armies' FSU
Total Soldiers Sum of all soldiers
Combined Consumption (Total Soldiers ÷ 100) FSU per week
Supply Lines Must feed all armies from same source or multiple sources
61.19 Supply Officers
The War Marshal may designate Supply Officers (typically Stewards or trusted
commanders) to:
Coordinate food distribution
Manage supply trains
Negotiate with merchants
61.20 Foraging Limits
When a Grand Army forages:
Roll once for the entire army (using best terrain among all armies)
Result applies to all armies equally
If result is 1 (Hunters attacked), casualties are spread across all armies
61.21 Supply Strain
A Grand Army strains local resources:
Army Size Effect on Region
1,000-2,500 Local prices +25% (all goods)
2,500-5,000 Local prices +50%, foraging -1
5,000-10,000 Local prices +100%, foraging -2, unrest possible
10,000+ Local economy disrupted, famine risk, refugee generation
61.22 Grand Army Disbandment
A Grand Army may disband when:
The common enemy is defeated
The War Marshal dies or is incapacitated
A major disagreement occurs between commanders
Supply fails completely
61.23 Disbandment Roll
When the Grand Army disbands, each commander's final loyalty to the War Marshal
is checked:
Roll 1d6 for each commander:
Roll Result
1-2 Disgruntled (-2 Loyalty to War Marshal, may bear grudge)
3-4 Neutral (0 change)
5-6 Grateful (+1 Loyalty, may answer future calls faster)
Modifiers:
Successful campaign: +1
Failed campaign: -2

-- 196 of 529 --

Fair loot distribution: +1
Unfair loot distribution: -2
War Marshal died in battle: +2 (heroic memory)
61.24 War Marshal's Legacy
If the War Marshal led a successful Grand Army campaign, they gain:
Success Level Reputation Gain Possible Title
Minor victory +1 "The Marshal"
Major victory +2 "Grand Marshal"
Legendary victory +3 "Warlord" / "Conqueror"
61.25 Historical Example — The Three Armies at Goldengate
When House Virement, House Blackmere, and House Caldrin united against the
northern invasion, they faced a problem: three lords, three armies, three
competing egos. Lord Virement was the strongest, with 2,000 soldiers. Lord
Blackmere brought 1,500. Lord Caldrin contributed 1,200. None would serve under
the others.
They elected Lord Virement as War Marshal — not because they trusted him, but
because his banner was Tier 4, granting him the Strategic Capacity to coordinate
all 4,700 soldiers. His personal capacity was only 1,500, but his name held the
army together.
At the Battle of Goldengate, the Grand Army shattered the invaders. Virement
became known as "Virement the Marshal" thereafter. The alliance held for three
years.
PART 62: COMMANDER DUELS — WHEN LEGENDS CLASH (Mega Patch 6.0)
62.1 Core Principle
Battles are not just clashing lines of anonymous men. Sometimes, a commander
seeks out the enemy standard. Sometimes, the enemy captain rides to meet them.
Sometimes, the fate of the battle hangs on steel meeting steel.
62.2 When Duels Can Happen
A duel may only occur when ALL of the following are true:
Both commanders are alive and on the battlefield
Both commanders are in units that are ENGAGED with each other
At least one commander CHOOSES to seek out the other
The player (if involved) declares the attempt
The NPC commander's personality allows it
62.3 Duel Trigger Check
PLAYER-DRIVEN DUEL
If the player commands a unit engaged with an enemy commander's unit, they may
declare: "I seek out their commander." This replaces their normal action for the
round.
NPC-DRIVEN DUEL
For each engaged pair of commanders where at least one is an NPC, roll 1d6 +
Personality Modifiers. Base DC: 5.
Temperament Modifiers:
Temperament Modifier
Aggressive +2
Proud +2
Loyal +1

-- 197 of 529 --

Disciplined -2
Cunning -1
Wary -2
Priority Modifiers:
Priority Modifier
Glory +2
Victory +1
Survival -2
Situation Modifiers:
Situation Modifier
Outnumbered +1
Winning battle +1
Losing battle +2
Commander wounded -3
62.4 Duel Resolution — Attack Pool
Each commander builds an attack pool:
Weapon Dice (from weapon tree)
+1d6 per 5 personal bodyguards still alive
+1d6 for mount bonus (if applicable)
± Interaction modifiers
Maximum pool: 6d6 (a duel is personal, not mass combat)
62.5 Duel Interaction Table
Attacker vs Defender Modifier
Aggressive vs Aggressive 0
Aggressive vs Defensive -1d6
Aggressive vs Cunning +1d6
Defensive vs Aggressive +1d6
Defensive vs Defensive -1d6
Defensive vs Cunning 0
Cunning vs Aggressive -1d6
Cunning vs Defensive +1d6
Cunning vs Cunning -2d6
Style is determined by commander's TEMPERAMENT.
62.6 Armor Class in Duels
Each commander uses their personal Armor Class. Mounted commanders may have
cover from their horse:
Riding Horse: +0 AC
Warhorse: +1 AC
Heavy Destrier: +2 AC
62.7 Damage Resolution
Each d6 result:
Roll < enemy AC → no effect
Roll ≥ enemy AC → 1 HIT
Roll 6 → 2 HITS (critical blow)
62.8 Duel Outcomes
Result Outcome
One commander has 3+ more hits DECISIVE VICTORY — Loser killed (1-3) or

-- 198 of 529 --

captured (4-6)
One commander has 1-2 more hits VICTORY — Loser wounded, retreats, -1 Tier
permanently
Hits equal or within 1 STALEMATE — Both withdraw, no further effect
62.9 Morale Effects of Duels
IF COMMANDER IS KILLED
Their unit: Immediate morale check at -2
On failure, unit shatters (disbands)
On success, unit fights on but at -1 Tier
Their army: All allied units -1 Morale
Enemy army: All enemy units +1 Morale
IF COMMANDER IS CAPTURED
Their unit: Immediate morale check at -1
On failure, unit retreats (withdraws)
Enemy army: Victor gains +1 Loyalty
IF COMMANDER IS WOUNDED
Their unit: -1 Initiative for rest of battle
Commander out for 1d6 weeks
PART 63: PROTECTION & INTERVENTION (Mega Patch 6.0)
63.1 Core Principle
A lord does not fight alone. Around them are those who would die to keep them
alive: champions, personal guards, banner carriers, and loyal commanders.
63.2 Who Can Intervene
PERSONAL CHAMPION
Must be assigned before battle
Fights in place of the lord
Has own stats (Tier 1-3, weapon, armor)
LOYAL COMMANDER
Must have Loyalty ≥ 4 to the targeted lord
Intercepts the attack, takes the duel themselves
PERSONAL GUARD
Unit of elite bodyguards
Counts as 1d6 extra dice in the lord's duel pool
If lord is killed, 50% die in vengeance
BANNER CARRIER
Can sacrifice themselves to preserve lord's honor
If banner falls but lord lives: +2 Morale

-- 199 of 529 --

If lord falls but banner stands: army fights on
63.3 Intervention Triggers
FOR PERSONAL CHAMPION
Automatically if assigned and alive
Champion takes the duel in lord's place
FOR LOYAL COMMANDER
Roll 1d6, success on 4+
Modifier: +1 if Loyalty ≥ 5, +2 if Loyalty = 6
On success, they intercept
On failure, they are cut down (killed)
FOR PERSONAL GUARD
Automatically if present
Add 1d6 to lord's duel pool
If lord still loses, 1d3 guards die
FOR BANNER CARRIER
Lord may choose to sacrifice them (once per battle)
Banner carrier takes the killing blow instead of lord
Banner falls but lord lives
Army gains +2 Morale (rage/vengeance)
PART 64: CHAMPION'S DUEL — BATTLE BY PROXY (Per Patch 7.22)
64.1 Core Principle
Sometimes armies do not need to clash. Sometimes the gods of war demand a
simpler sacrifice — one champion against another. The winner takes the battle.
The loser takes his chances. This is the Champion's Duel, and every culture sees
it differently. For some, it is the highest honor. For others, it is
foolishness. For all, it is a moment that songs are made of.
64.2 Requirements
Both commanders must agree to the duel
Each side names a champion (themselves or another)
Terms are set before the fight: what does victory mean?
Both sides are bound by honor, not law — the loser may not keep their word
64.3 Why Agree to a Duel?
Reason Explanation
Avoid Mass Casualties Save your army for the next fight
Morale Advantage A won duel inspires troops
Quick Resolution End a siege or campaign faster
Personal Glory Champions become legends
Cultural Tradition Some regions expect this
Reason to Refuse Explanation
You'll Probably Lose Your champion is weaker
You Don't Trust Them They might break their word
You Have Advantage Why gamble when you can win for sure?
Cultural Taboo Some regions find duels dishonorable or foolish

-- 200 of 529 --

64.4 Terms of the Duel
Before the fight, both sides must agree on what victory means.
Term Meaning
Battle Decided Winner's army wins the battle. Loser's army withdraws.
Single Combat Only No other fighting. The duel decides everything.
Champion's Fate Loser may be killed, captured, or ransomed.
Spoils Winner takes loser's horse, armor, weapons (standard).
Oath Both commanders swear to honor the result.
The Oath Problem: An oath is only as strong as the one who swears it. Some keep
their word. Some don't.
64.5 Resolution — The Duel Itself
Use the Commander Duel system (Part 62) with these modifiers:
Situation Modifier
Fighting for your lord's honor +1 to all rolls
Fighting for your own freedom +2 to all rolls
Champion is known to both armies +1 morale to allied army if win
Champion is hated by enemy -1 morale to enemy if they lose
Duel Outcome:
Result Mechanical Effect
Win (decisive) Champion kills or captures opponent. Victory condition met.
Win (narrow) Champion wounds opponent. Opponent yields. Victory condition
met.
Loss Champion dies or yields. Your army must abide by terms.
Stalemate Both champions withdraw. No decision — battle continues normally.
64.6 After the Duel — Keeping the Oath
After the duel, the losing commander must decide: keep the oath or break it?
Roll 1d6 + Culture Modifier + Situation Modifier
Cultural Honor Modifiers:
Culture Base Honor
Central Plains +2 (duels are sacred)
Western Rivers +1 (contract is contract)
Eastern Forests (Settled) +1 (word is bond)
Eastern Forests (Tribal) N/A (do not duel)
Southern Mountains +2 (ancestors watch)
Northern Snowlands +1 (oath is oath)
Nomad Steppe (Traditional) -1 (duels are for fools)
Nomad Steppe (Settled) 0 (depends on situation)
Situation Modifiers:
Situation Modifier
Champion was beloved by army +1 to keep oath
Champion was hated rival -1 to keep oath
Army is stronger than opponent's -2 to keep oath (why honor it?)
Army is weaker than opponent's +2 to keep oath (need mercy)
Commander is known oathbreaker -2 to keep oath
Commander is known honorable +2 to keep oath
Oath Resolution Table:
Total Outcome
1-3 Oath Broken — attack immediately (enemy gains +1 morale from outrage)
4-6 Oath Broken — but with shame (-2 reputation for oathbreaker)
7-9 Oath Kept — but grudgingly (army -1 morale, they wanted to fight)
10+ Oath Kept — honorably (both sides withdraw in peace, +1 reputation)
64.7 Champion's Rewards
The winning champion gains:

PART 65: REGIONAL COMBAT DOCTRINES (Per Patch 7.20)
65.1 Core Principle
Every army fights in the image of its homeland. The terrain that shaped them,
the enemies they've faced, the resources they have — all of it molds their way
of war. When two armies from different regions meet, it is not just steel
against steel. It is philosophy against philosophy.
A Central Plains knight does not understand why the Eastern footmen won't stand
and fight.
An Eastern hunter does not understand why the Plainsmen charge across open
ground to die.
A Mountain lord does not understand why anyone would fight anywhere but a pass.
They are all wrong. They are all right. They are all products of their land.
65.2 Regional Combat Doctrine Table
Region Doctrine Name Core Principle Preferred Terrain Preferred
Action Preferred Units
Central Plains The Thunder Charge Overwhelming force, decisive battle
Open plains, gentle hills Charge Heavy cavalry, shock infantry
Western Rivers The Flood Tide Mobility, control of waterways Rivers,
marshes, floodplains Flank, withdraw, re-engage Light infantry, river
boats, skirmishers
Eastern Forests (Settled) The Hunter's Noose Patience, encirclement, the
killing ground Forest edges, clearings, hills Trap, surround, destroy
Archers, skirmishers, light infantry
Eastern Forests (Tribal) The Green Shadows Invisibility, harassment, never a
fair fight Deep forest, darkness, fog Ambush, vanish, ambush again
Skirmishers, hunters, no heavy units
Southern Mountains The Stone Wall Defense, holding the pass, never
retreat Mountain passes, high ground, fortified positions Defend, brace,
counter-charge Heavy infantry, pikes, crossbows
Northern Snowlands The Iron Wall Endurance, aggression, break them
before they break you Snow, frozen ground, taiga Hold, advance, outlast
Pike infantry, heavy infantry, crossbowmen
Nomad Steppe (Traditional) The Grass Fire Speed, feint, surround, the
endless retreat Open steppe, any flat ground Feigned retreat, encirclement,
horse archers Light cavalry, horse archers, skirmishers
Nomad Steppe (Settled) The Two Wolves Both ways — steppe speed, settled
steel Mixed, adaptable Adapt to enemy, use both strengths Mixed cavalry, heavy
horse, foot archers
65.3 Central Plains — The Thunder Charge
"There is no problem that cannot be solved by riding at it very fast."
Preferred Army Composition:
40-60% Heavy Cavalry (knights, men-at-arms)
20-30% Heavy Infantry (shock troops)
10-20% Light Infantry (screen, scouts)
0-10% Archers (minimal, considered unknightly)
Tactical Advantages:
Situation Bonus
Charging on first round of combat +2d6 to all cavalry units
Fighting on open plains +1 Initiative all units
Enemy unit wavering +1 to morale damage
Tactical Disadvantages:
Situation Penalty
Fighting in forests -2 Initiative

-- 204 of 529 --

Enemy uses feigned retreat -1 to morale checks (impatience)
Siege warfare -1 to all rolls (no room to charge)
Special Doctrine: The First Charge
When a Central Plains army charges in the first round of combat, roll 1d6:
Roll Outcome
1-2 Standard charge, no special effect
3-4 Thunderous impact — enemy front line loses 1d6 morale
5 Crushing blow — enemy front line must check morale immediately
6 Legendary charge — enemy line breaks, route possible in round 1
65.4 Western Rivers — The Flood Tide
"Fight, fall back, fight again. The river does not stop flowing because it meets
a rock."
Preferred Army Composition:
30-40% Light Infantry (mobile, adaptable)
20-30% Skirmishers (javelins, slings)
20-30% River Boats (if water access)
10-20% Archers
0-10% Heavy Cavalry (rare, expensive)
Tactical Advantages:
Situation Bonus
Fighting near rivers +2 to flanking rolls
Withdraw action +1 to withdrawal success
Enemy in marsh terrain +2 to attack rolls
Tactical Disadvantages:
Situation Penalty
Fighting on open plains -1 to all rolls (no cover)
Besieged (no water access) -2 to morale
Enemy heavy cavalry -1 to defense (hard to stop)
Special Doctrine: The Ebb and Flow
A Western Rivers army may, once per battle, choose to withdraw from combat
without triggering a pursuit. They reform and may re-enter combat on a flank the
next round.
"They ran! After them!"
"No, wait — it's a trap —"
Too late. The "fleeing" army has reformed on the ridge and is now charging your
exposed flank.
65.5 Eastern Forests (Settled) — The Hunter's Noose
"Let them come into the forest. The trees have teeth."
Preferred Army Composition:
40-50% Archers (longbows, shortbows)
20-30% Light Infantry (sword and shield)
10-20% Skirmishers
10-20% Heavy Infantry (guarding the center)
Tactical Advantages:
Situation Bonus

-- 205 of 529 --

Fighting in forests +2 to ambush, +1 to all attacks
Enemy marching (not in formation) +2 to first round damage
Night fighting +1 to all rolls (they know the dark)
Tactical Disadvantages:
Situation Penalty
Fighting on open plains -2 to all rolls
Enemy with heavy armor -1 to ranged damage
Cavalry charges -1 to defense (difficult to stop)
Special Doctrine: The Noose Tightens
If a Settled Forest army spends 2+ rounds engaging the same enemy unit without
breaking, they may attempt to encircle that unit.
Roll 1d6 + rounds engaged vs enemy commander's Tactics:
Success Outcome
Fail No effect
Success Enemy unit is flanked (-1 AC, cannot withdraw)
Major Success Enemy unit surrounded (-2 AC, cannot withdraw, morale check)
65.6 Eastern Forests (Tribal) — The Green Shadows
"They never saw us. They never saw anything."
Preferred Army Composition:
60-80% Skirmishers (hunters, ambushers)
20-40% Archers (shortbows, fast-firing)
0% Heavy Infantry (none)
0% Cavalry (none — horses are for trade, not war)
Tactical Advantages:
Situation Bonus
Ambush round +3 to all attacks (devastating)
Forest terrain +2 to stealth, +1 to all attacks
Fog or darkness +2 to all rolls (they are the dark)
Tactical Disadvantages:
Situation Penalty
Open field battle -3 to all rolls (they will not fight here)
Enemy in fortified position Cannot attack directly
Prolonged engagement Must withdraw after 3 rounds or take losses
Special Doctrine: Strike and Vanish
A Tribal Forest army may, after any successful attack, attempt to vanish —
withdrawing from combat without triggering pursuit and without the enemy knowing
which direction they went.
Roll 1d6 + terrain bonus:
Roll Outcome
1-2 Enemy tracks you — pursuit begins
3-4 You escape, but enemy knows general direction
5-6 You vanish completely. They have no idea where you went.
"Where did they go?"
"I don't know. I don't even know if they were ever really there."

They've been holding that pass for three days. Three days of charges, of arrows,

They ran. We cheered. We chased. And then, somehow, they were all around us,

BOOK V: ECONOMY & TRADE — COMPLETE RESTORATION
PART 66: THE LIVING ECONOMY — CORE PRINCIPLES (Per Patch 7.0, 13.0)
66.1 The Four Pillars of Economic Reality
The world's economy rests on four interconnected pillars. No pillar stands
alone. A failure in one will eventually topple the others.
Pillar Description Player Impact
Resources Raw goods extracted from the land (Grain, Iron, Timber, etc.)
Must be gathered, stored, and allocated. Quality matters.
Production Crafting and manufacturing (weapons, armor, tools) Turns raw goods
into usable equipment. Requires facilities and labor.
Currency Silverdew (SD) — the lifeblood of transactions Used for wages,
trade, construction, and tribute. Must flow, not just sit.
Population The people who work, fight, and pay taxes The ultimate resource.

-- 209 of 529 --

Over-exploitation leads to decline.
66.2 The Flow of Wealth — A Closed System
The world economy is a closed system. Coin does not appear from nowhere. It is:
Earned: Through taxes, trade, loot, or tribute.
Spent: On wages, construction, equipment, and tribute.
Circulated: Paid to soldiers who buy food from farmers who pay taxes to lords.
Lost: Through waste, corruption, sacking, or hoarding.
If the player hoards coin, the economy slows. If they spend wisely, it grows.
66.3 The Labor Truth
A soldier holding a sword is not a farmer holding a plow. Every man recruited
for war is one less man growing food, cutting timber, or mining ore. This is the
fundamental trade of the Age of Shattered Oaths.
The Equation:
(Total Population) = (Farmers + Miners + Laborers) + (Soldiers)
If you increase Soldiers, you must decrease the workforce — or import labor
(refugees, slaves) and goods from elsewhere.
PART 67: SILVERDEW COIN SYSTEM (Pages 139)
67.1 Core Currency
Silverdew Coins (SD) are the only currency. Design assumptions:
10-20 SD = arm a small unit cheaply
25-50 SD = elite equipment
100+ SD = major military investment
67.2 Unit Equipment Cost
To equip a unit: Total Cost = Weapon Cost + Armor Cost
Example: Halberds (22) + Chain (25) = 47 Silverdew
67.3 Balance Consequences
If a player has too much silver:
Mercenary prices rise
Bandits become bolder
Lords request "loans"
Assassination attempts increase
If a player has too little:
Soldiers desert
Vassals become restless
Trade partners demand upfront payment
Reputation suffers

Initial Quality: Roll 1d6 at game start:
1-3 = Common
4-5 = High-Grade
6 = Superb
New Construction: Roll immediately upon completion using the same table.
Quality is Fixed: A patch's quality tier is permanent unless a narrative event
explicitly changes it.
Quality Affects:
Sale Price: Value = Base Price × Quality Multiplier
Crafting: Items crafted from higher-tier materials gain combat bonuses
Reputation: Holdings known for Superb goods gain +1 regional reputation

69.1 The Workforce
Every holding has a population. A portion of that population is the Labor Pool —
able-bodied adults who work, pay taxes, and can be recruited.
Holding Type Population Labor Pool (40%) Max Sustainable Garrison

69.2 Labor Allocation
The lord must decide how to allocate the Labor Pool each season:
Occupation Productivity Notes
Farming Produces FSU Essential for food security
Mining/Logging Produces resources Essential for crafting and trade
Crafting Produces goods Converts resources into equipment
Military Service Produces soldiers Reduces all other production
69.3 The Recruitment Cost (Per Patch 7.0)
When a lord recruits a soldier, they are not just paying for equipment. They are
permanently (or semi-permanently) removing a worker from the labor pool.
Total Cost to Recruit 1 Soldier =
(Equipment Cost) + (Lost Productivity) + (Signing Bonus/Incentives)
Equipment Cost: As per crafting tables
Lost Productivity: The value of what that worker would have produced.
Represented by a temporary -10% to holding income for 3 months per 10% of the
labor pool recruited.
Signing Bonus: Typically 1-5 SD per recruit, paid upfront to the soldier's
family.
Example:
A lord recruits 100 soldiers from a Town with a Labor Pool of 800.

-- 212 of 529 --

Cost Type Calculation Cost
Equipment Cost (Spear & Leather) 100 × 22 SD 2,200 SD
Lost Productivity 100 is 12.5% of labor pool -12.5% income for 3 months
Signing Bonus 100 × 2 SD 200 SD
Total Immediate Cost 2,400 SD + 3 months reduced income
69.4 Tax Base Calculation (Per Patch 13.0)
Base Holding Income (SD/month) = (Population ÷ 10) × Tier Multiplier × 2.5
Tier Tier Multiplier Formula Example (5,000 pop)
0-1 1.5 5,000 ÷ 10 = 500 × 1.5 × 2.5 = 1,875 SD/month
2-3 2.0 5,000 ÷ 10 = 500 × 2.0 × 2.5 = 2,500 SD/month
4-5 2.5 5,000 ÷ 10 = 500 × 2.5 × 2.5 = 3,125 SD/month
6-7 3.0 5,000 ÷ 10 = 500 × 3.0 × 2.5 = 3,750 SD/month
8 4.0 5,000 ÷ 10 = 500 × 4.0 × 2.5 = 5,000 SD/month

PART 70: WEEKLY INCOME & ECONOMIC CYCLE (Per Patch 7.0)
70.1 Core Rule
All income sources defined as "per day" are accumulated automatically.
Weekly Ledger Income = (Daily Income × 7) + Weekly Income Sources
Weekly sources include:
Caravan income
Tribute payments
Shadow income rolls
Toll spikes
Event rewards
70.2 Integrated Weekly Economic Cycle
Each week, the following occurs automatically during the Weekly Ledger:
Phase Description
PHASE 1: PRODUCTION All resource patches generate yield based on their Tier
and Labor allocation. All crafting facilities convert resources into goods.
Holdings generate base income from taxes.
PHASE 2: CONSUMPTION Garrisons and field armies consume FSU. Population
consumes FSU. Crafting facilities consume resources from storage.
PHASE 3: TRADE & TRIBUTE Caravans depart/arrive, generating trade income.
Tribute payments are deducted from the paying house and added to the receiver.
Vassal obligations are checked.
PHASE 4: WAGES & UPKEEP Army wages are deducted. Holding maintenance costs are
deducted. Engineer and specialist wages are deducted.
PHASE 5: TREASURY UPDATE Net change is calculated. New treasury total is
recorded. If treasury is negative, Crisis Mode begins.
PHASE 6: TIME CONFIRMATION System pauses and presents a summary of the week's
economic activity. Player confirms continuation or makes adjustments.

PART 72: TRADE & CARAVAN SYSTEM (Per Patch 7.6)
72.1 Core Principle
Trade is not abstract income. It is the movement of actual goods from places of
plenty to places of scarcity. A caravan's profit is the difference between the
purchase price at the origin and the sale price at the destination, minus costs,
risks, and time.

-- 214 of 529 --

The Merchant's Equation:
Profit = (Sale Price - Purchase Price) × Volume - Operating Costs - Risk Premium
72.2 Caravan Profit Factors
Factor Effect on Profit Calculation
Journey Time Longer = higher profit +5% per week of travel (base)
Goods Type Different demand curves See 72.4
Quality Tier Higher quality = exponential value Common: 1.0x, High-Grade:
1.5x, Superb: 2.0x
Volume More goods = more profit, but market saturation risk See 72.7
Regional Demand Each region craves different goods See 72.5
Risk Danger routes demand higher returns +10-50% based on route security
72.3 Base Value Multiplier by Journey Time
Journey Duration Base Value Multiplier Example
Local (1-3 days) 1.1x Village to neighboring village
Short (4-7 days) 1.2x Village to town
Medium (1-2 weeks) 1.4x Town to regional city
Long (2-4 weeks) 1.8x Cross-region trade
Extended (1-2 months) 2.5x North-South continental trade
Epic (3+ months) 4.0x Entire continent crossing
Formula: Base Multiplier = 1.0 + (0.05 × Weeks of Travel)

72.6 Quality Multipliers

Complete Formula:
Final Price = Base Price × Demand Multiplier × Journey Multiplier × Quality
Multiplier × Rarity Premium
Example — Superb Furs from Eastern Forests sold in Central Plains:
Base: 15 SD
Demand: Central Plains craves furs (×1.5)
Journey: 3 weeks (×1.15)
Quality: Superb (×2.0)
Rarity: Premium (×1.25)
Final Price: 15 × 1.5 × 1.15 × 2.0 × 1.25 = 64.7 SD per SU
72.7 Market Saturation & Flooding
If too much of a single good arrives in a market, prices crash.
Market Capacity by Settlement:
Settlement Type Capacity (SU per month per good type)
Village 50 SU
Town 150 SU
City 500 SU
Capital 2,000 SU
Saturation Effect:
Percent of Capacity Filled Price Modifier
0-25% 1.0x - 1.1x (scarcity bonus)
26-50% 0.9x - 1.0x
51-75% 0.7x - 0.9x
76-100% 0.5x - 0.7x
100%+ 0.3x - 0.5x (glut)
Flooding the Market:
When you dump massive amounts:
Flood Ratio = SU dumped ÷ Market Capacity
Roll 1d6 + Flood Ratio:
Roll Outcome
1-3 Minor glut (prices -10% for 1d6 months)
4-5 Market crash (prices -25% for 1d6 months)

-- 216 of 529 --

6+ Economic shock (prices -50% for 1 year)
72.8 Caravan Operating Costs
Every caravan has costs that must be subtracted from profit.
Cost Type Amount Notes
Guards 1 SD per 10 guards per week Minimum 10 guards per caravan
Wagons 1 SD per wagon per week Each wagon carries 20 SU
Drivers 1 SD per 2 wagons per week 1 driver per 2 wagons
Tolls Varies by route 1-10 SD per border/bridge
Bribes Optional 5-50 SD for safe passage
Contingency 10% of cargo value Losses to bandits, spoilage, etc.
Typical Caravan Composition:
Caravan Size Wagons Guards Drivers Capacity (SU) Weekly
Cost
Small 5 10 3 100 23 SD/week
Medium 10 20 5 200 45 SD/week
Large 20 40 10 400 90 SD/week
Massive 50 100 25 1,000 225 SD/week
72.9 Complete Caravan Profit Formula
Step Calculation
Step 1 Purchase Cost = Σ (Goods Volume × Local Price)
Step 2 Sale Value = Σ (Goods Volume × Destination Price)
Step 3 Total Costs = (Weekly Cost × Weeks Travel) + Tolls + Bribes +
Contingency
Step 4 Gross Profit = Sale Value — Purchase Cost
Step 5 Net Profit = Gross Profit — Total Costs
Step 6 Margin % = (Net Profit ÷ Purchase Cost) × 100
72.10 Caravan Profit Examples
Example 1 — Local Grain Trade
Route: Village to Town (3 days, 0.5 weeks)
Goods: 100 SU Grain (Common)
Purchase: 100 SU × 1 SD = 100 SD
Destination Price: Town needs grain (×1.2) × journey (×1.025) = 1.23 SD
Sale Value: 100 × 1.23 = 123 SD
Operating Costs: Small caravan, 0.5 weeks = 12 SD
Net Profit: 123 — 100 — 12 = 11 SD (11% margin)
Example 2 — Cross-Region Luxury Trade
Route: Eastern Forests (North) to Central Plains Capital (3 weeks)
Goods: 50 SU Superb Furs
Purchase: Furs base 15 SD × Superb (×2.0) × local supply (-15%) = 25.5 SD/SU
Total Purchase: 50 × 25.5 = 1,275 SD
Destination Price: Central Plains craves furs (×1.5) × journey (×1.15) × Superb
(×2.0) × rarity (×1.25) = 64.7 SD/SU
Sale Value: 50 × 64.7 = 3,235 SD
Operating Costs: Medium caravan, 3 weeks = 135 SD + tolls (20 SD) + contingency
(324 SD) = 479 SD
Net Profit: 3,235 — 1,275 — 479 = 1,481 SD (116% margin)

-- 217 of 529 --

Example 3 — Strategic Resources in Wartime
Route: Southern Mountains to Northern Snowlands (6 weeks)
Goods: 100 SU High-Grade Iron
Purchase: Iron base 5 SD × High-Grade (×1.5) × local supply (-20%) = 6 SD/SU
Total Purchase: 100 × 6 = 600 SD
Destination Price: North craves iron (+50%) × war demand (+50% situational) ×
journey (×1.3) × High-Grade (×1.5) = 5 × 2.0 × 1.3 × 1.5 = 19.5 SD/SU
Sale Value: 100 × 19.5 = 1,950 SD
Operating Costs: Large caravan, 6 weeks = 540 SD + heavy bribes (100 SD) +
contingency (195 SD) = 835 SD
Net Profit: 1,950 — 600 — 835 = 515 SD (86% margin, but high risk)
72.11 Caravan Risk Table
Each week of travel, roll 1d6 for incidents on standard routes. Dangerous routes
(war zones, bandit country) roll 1d6 with +1 modifier.
Roll Incident Effect
1 Bandit attack Lose 1d10% of cargo, 1d6 guards killed
2 Weather delay +1 week travel, +1d6×10% costs
3 Broken wagon Lose 1d3 days, repair costs 5 SD
4 River crossing issue Toll doubled or 1 week delay
5 Guard dispute Pay 1d6×5 SD bonus or they leave
6 Smooth travel No incident
7+ Disaster! Roll twice on this table
72.12 Caravan Tier Growth
As caravans successfully complete routes, they gain tiers, increasing capacity
and profit potential.
Tier Name Wagons Guards Capacity Profit Bonus
I Trial Caravan 5 10 100 SU Base
II Regular Route 10 20 200 SU +5% to negotiations
III Major Caravan 20 40 400 SU +10% to destination prices
IV Trade Artery 35 70 700 SU +15%, can ignore one toll
V Merchant Prince 50 100 1,000 SU +20%, can demand audience with
lords
Tier Advancement:
I → II: Complete 3 successful journeys
II → III: Complete 6 successful journeys OR one epic journey (4+ weeks)
III → IV: Complete 10 successful journeys OR establish new trade route
IV → V: Complete 20 successful journeys OR receive lord's charter

74.5 Resource Quality & Crafting Bonuses (Per Patch 6.4)
Core Principle: The quality of raw materials directly affects both their market
price and the performance of items crafted from them. For most goods, only the
price is affected. For Iron and Tar, higher quality also imparts combat bonuses.
Crafting with Quality Materials
When a crafter uses high-quality Iron or Tar to create an item, the finished
product gains the corresponding combat bonus.
Quality Name Price Multiplier Crafting Bonus
1 Common 1.0x None
2 High-Grade 1.5x 1 reroll per battle
3 Superb 2.0x 2 rerolls per battle
Weapons and Ammunition: Grants the specified number of rerolls per battle.
Armor: High-quality armor allows the wearer to ignore the first successful hit
in a battle (High-Grade: ignore one hit; Superb: ignore two hits).
Tar: Used in fire arrows, Greek fire, and siege ammunition. Grants +1 to hit
(High-Grade) or +2 to hit (Superb), or adds +1d6 to the attack pool.
Crafting Requirement: To create an item with a quality bonus, the crafter must
use materials of that quality. If materials of mixed quality are used, the
finished item defaults to the lowest quality used.
74.6 Finished Goods Tracking (Per Patch 7.7)
All finished weapons, armor, and mounts are tracked as distinct items in holding
inventories. They are not abstract "supply units" — they are individual items
with location, quality, and history.
text
ITEM TRACKING EXAMPLE
─────────────────────────────────────
Item: Sword (Superb Quality)
Crafted: Velrinport, Day 45 of Sunreach, Year 12
Crafted By: Master Smith Harald
Current Location: Velrinport Armory (in stock)
Quality: Superb (2 rerolls per battle)
Price: 30 SD
─────────────────────────────────────
When a soldier is equipped:
The specific item is removed from inventory
It becomes part of that unit's equipment
If the soldier dies, the item may be looted
If the soldier deserts, the item goes with them
74.7 Equipment Persistence Rules (Per Patch 7.7)
No Item Spawning: Every weapon, armor suit, and mount in the world at game start
is placed during world generation. Nothing appears later except through
crafting, trade, or loot.
Crafted Items Enter Inventory: When a smith finishes crafting a sword, it is
added to the holding's inventory.
Imported Items Enter Inventory: When a caravan arrives, its goods are added to
the destination holding's inventory.

-- 222 of 529 --

Looted Items Enter Inventory: After a battle, recovered equipment is added to
the victor's inventory.
Equipped Items Are Tracked: A soldier equipped with a specific sword now has
that sword.
Inherited Items: Starting equipment from character creation exists in the world
— it was crafted years ago, passed down, or looted by ancestors.

PART 76: ARMY WAGE UPKEEP (Per Patch 6.1)
76.1 Core Rule
All soldiers require payment each week.
10 Soldiers = 1 Silver per Week
Soldiers Weekly Wage
50 5 Silver
100 10 Silver
300 30 Silver
1,000 100 Silver
10,000 1,000 Silver
76.2 Army Upkeep Formula
Army Upkeep = Food Supply Consumption + Soldier wages
76.3 Upkeep for Landed vs. Landless Commanders
Landed Characters (Rulers): The cost of army wages for all units under their
command is deducted from their personal Treasury each week during the Weekly
Ledger.
Landless Characters: Their warband's payment is handled through the Loot System.
If they have no loot, they cannot pay wages, leading to consequences.
76.4 Unpaid Soldiers
If a player cannot or does not pay the required weekly wages:
Consequence Effect
Immediate Morale Loss All affected units suffer -2 Morale
Desertion Roll (Week 1) End of week, roll 1d6 per unit. On 1-2, 1d10 soldiers
desert
Disobedience (Week 2) If unpaid for two weeks, commander must make Loyalty

-- 225 of 529 --

check (DC 5). On failure, unit refuses orders.
Mutiny/Disbandment (Week 3) Unit disbands. Landless warbands may turn on the
player.

PART 78: BRANCH FAMILY ECONOMICS (Per Patch 7.0)
78.1 Branch Family Economic Role
Branch families are not just military assets — they are economic nodes in the
lord's network. Each Branch Family:
Controls 1-3 resource patches or small holdings
Employs 50-500 workers
Pays Tiered Tribute to the main family
Has its own treasury
78.2 Branch Family Tribute Table
Branch Relationship Tribute to Main Family Main Family Obligation
Resentful (-2) 5% of income None (they hide wealth)
Lukewarm (0) 10% of income Protection if directly threatened
Loyal (+1) 15% of income Military support, trade rights
Ambitious (+1 to main, -1 hidden) 10% of income Same as Loyal, but they
plot
78.3 Economic Integration
When a main family builds a Guild Hall, Market Square, or Workshop in their
capital, nearby branch families gain a +10% income bonus.
PART 79: WORLD ECONOMIC RESPONSE (Per Patch 7.0)
79.1 Lords Notice Wealth
If the player's treasury exceeds 10,000 SD:
Neighboring lords demand loans/tribute
Bandit activity increases
Mercenary prices increase
79.2 Lords Notice Weakness
If the player's treasury drops below 500 SD:
Vassals become rebellious
Neighbors press claims
Trade partners demand upfront payment
79.3 Economic Ripples
Every major economic event creates ripples:
A new iron mine creates jobs → population grows → more taxes
A failed harvest causes famine → refugees flee → labor pool elsewhere increases
A sacked city floods the market with loot → prices drop → local producers suffer
79.4 Economic Crisis Triggers

80.1 Core Principle
Buying food in bulk should be cheaper. Lords who plan ahead, negotiate
contracts, and build supply networks should be rewarded with more stable
campaigns.

Roll Outcome

PART 82: NOBLE ALLIANCE & OPINION SYSTEM (Pages 174-178)
82.1 Core Principle
Houses remember. They just don't always say it out loud. Every noble faction
tracks Opinion of every other noble faction. This is:
Persistent
Hidden from the player
Shifted by events
Used to decide alliances
Used to decide betrayals
82.2 Opinion Scale
Score Meaning
-3 Blood Feud — kill on sight
-2 Enemy — open hostility
-1 Distrust — cold relations
0 Neutral — no strong feelings
+1 Favorable — willing to talk
+2 Friendly — would consider alliance
+3 Loyal — would die for you
82.3 What Changes Opinion?
Opinion shifts when:
Wars are fought
Battles are won/lost
Marriages occur
Betrothals break
Hostages die
Lands change hands
Alliances hold
Alliances fail
Plots are revealed
Insults are made
Gifts are given
Debts are repaid
Borders are threatened
82.4 Secret Alliance System
A Secret Alliance exists when:
Two or more houses reach +2 or +3

-- 231 of 529 --

AND have a shared enemy
AND a reason to hide it
Secret alliances:
Do not show banners
Do not declare support
Only activate when needed
Can be discovered
Can collapse
Can be betrayed
82.5 Back Room Deals
A Back Room Deal is a hidden agreement between 2+ houses about a future action.
Examples:
"When war comes, we strike together."
"We divide the south."
"We back your claim."
"We kill him."

PART 85: CLAIMS & INHERITANCE (Pages 189-194 + Per Patch 7.23)
85.1 Title Ownership Record
text
TITLE RECORD
─────────────────────────────────────
Title: ___________________
Current Holder: ___________________
House: ___________________

85.2 Succession Order
Default order:
Eldest legitimate child
Younger children
Siblings of the ruler
Children of siblings
Closest blood relative
If no clear heir exists, a Succession Crisis begins.
85.3 Claim Creation
Source Description
Blood Claim Blood relation to title holder
Marriage Claim Marriage creates future claims through children
War Claim Conquest or political justification
85.4 Claim Strength
Strength Examples
Strong Claim Direct heir, eldest child, uncontested bloodline
Moderate Claim Nephew or cousin, marriage descendant
Weak Claim Ancient claim, distant bloodline, political justification
85.5 Claim Persistence
Claims do not expire. A house may press a claim generations later, though
strength may diminish over time.
85.6 Succession Crisis — When No Clear Heir Exists
A Succession Crisis begins when a ruler dies and no clear heir can be identified
through normal succession.
Triggers:
Ruler dies with no living children
Ruler dies with no living siblings
All potential heirs are underage (under 16) with no regent designated
Multiple claimants have equal strength claims
The late ruler's will is disputed or missing
Assassination with no clear perpetrator
Immediate Effects:
All loyalty scores to the late ruler become 0
All vassals must choose sides within 1d6 weeks

-- 235 of 529 --

Realm Opinion of all factions drops by -2
Trade routes through the region -50% effectiveness
Neighboring realms gain claim to border territories
85.7 Civil War Phases
Civil wars escalate over time. Track the War Phase based on duration.
Phase Duration Effects
Phase 1 — Disputed Succession Weeks 1-4 Loyalty checks daily for all vassals,
no central authority, -1 Opinion all factions, Trade -25%
Phase 2 — Open Conflict Weeks 5-12 Armed clashes, -2 Opinion all factions,
Trade -50%, Refugee generation begins, Food production -25%
Phase 3 — Realm Divided Months 3-6 Full civil war, -3 Opinion all factions,
Trade -75%, Famine risk, Refugee crisis, Neighbors may intervene
Phase 4 — Collapse 6+ months Realm fractures into independent regions,
Central authority dissolved, Former territories become independent, New factions
emerge
85.8 Claimant Generation Table
Roll Claimant Type Claim Strength Starting Support
1 Distant cousin (male) Weak 1-2 minor lords
2 Distant cousin (female) Weak 1-2 minor lords
3 Niece/nephew of late ruler Moderate 2-4 minor lords
4 Sibling of late ruler Strong 1 major house
5 Child of late ruler (illegitimate) Disputed Popular support, few lords
6 Foreign noble with marriage claim Varies Foreign backing (1
neighbor)
Claimant Details Table (1d6):
Roll Age Personality Military Skill Popularity
1 16-20 Reckless +0 High with young lords
2 21-30 Ambitious +1 Moderate
3 31-40 Cunning +2 Low (seen as schemer)
4 41-50 Steady +1 High with veterans
5 51-60 Conservative +0 Moderate
6 60+ Vengeful -1 Low (seen as desperate)
85.9 Choosing Sides
When a succession crisis begins, each vassal must choose a side (or declare
independence).
Side Selection Roll (1d6 + Modifiers)
Base DC: 4
Modifier Effect
+2 Claimant has strong blood claim
+1 Claimant has moderate claim
-1 Claimant has weak claim
+2 Vassal has blood ties to claimant
+1 Vassal has marriage ties to claimant
-2 Vassal has blood feud with claimant's house
+1 Claimant offers major concessions
-1 Claimant demands tribute
+1 per Tier Claimant's military strength (max +3)
Results:
Roll Outcome
1-3 Joins opposing faction
4-5 Declares neutrality (may switch later)
6+ Joins claimant's faction
Neutrality: Neutral vassals may be pressured, bribed, or attacked by either
side. Each season, roll 1d6: on 1-2, they join a side (decide randomly).
85.10 Regency

-- 236 of 529 --

If the heir is underage (under 16), a regent rules until they come of age.
Regent Selection:
Usually the closest adult relative
May be specified in the late ruler's will
May be chosen by council vote
May be contested by rivals
Regent Powers:
Controls all military forces
Collects all revenues
Makes all decisions
Must abdicate when heir comes of age
Regent Risks:
May usurp the throne
May be assassinated
May be unpopular with vassals
May face succession crisis anyway
85.11 Claim Wars
When multiple claimants press their claims, war is likely. Claim wars follow
civil war rules (85.7) with these modifiers:
Situation Modifier
Claimant has strong claim +2 to side selection rolls
Claimant has foreign backing +1 to military rolls
Claimant is popular with smallfolk +1 to levy recruitment
85.12 Marriage Claim Strategy
A house may gain claims through strategic marriages:
Marry the daughter of a title holder → children have claim
Marry the widow of a title holder → may claim through her
Marry a claimant → combine claims
85.13 Claim Renunciation
A claimant may renounce their claim. This:
Removes them from succession
May be demanded as peace terms
May be temporary or permanent
Cannot be undone without reconfirmation
85.14 Extinct House Inheritance
If a noble house goes extinct (no living members), its lands and titles:
Revert to the liege lord

-- 237 of 529 --

May be claimed by cadet branches
May be seized by neighbors
May become disputed territory
85.15 Claim Memory
The system remembers all claims, even if not currently pressed. Ancient claims
may resurface generations later.
85.16 Kinslaying and Inheritance (Per Patch 7.23)
A kinslayer cannot inherit anything from the victim. Additionally:
Situation Effect
Kinslayer was next in line Line passes to next eligible relative
Kinslayer has children Children may inherit only if they publicly denounce
parent
Kinslayer is already ruling Rule is considered illegitimate. Vassals may
rebel.
Victim had no other heirs Title goes to liege lord or nearest collateral
line
Victim's spouse remarries Children of new marriage take precedence
The Tainted Blood: Some cultures believe the kinslayer's children are also
tainted. They may be passed over for inheritance even if innocent.
85.17 Council and Succession (Per Patch 7.31)
If a great lord dies without a clear heir, the council may have a role.
Situation Council Role
Clear heir exists Council confirms, may advise regent
Disputed succession Council may choose — or split, causing civil war
No heir Council may elect new lord from among their houses
Lord assassinated Council investigates, may seek vengeance
Council Election: If the council chooses a new lord, that lord owes them.
Council seats become permanent, power shifts.

-- 238 of 529 --

If multiple heirs exist, the system selects based on:
Legitimacy
Age
Capability (random)

86.4 Heir Transition
When an heir takes over:
They inherit all titles and lands
They inherit ongoing wars and alliances
They inherit grudges and feuds
Reputation resets partially (people remember the house)
Loyalty of vassals may shift
86.5 Underage Heir Options
Option Description
Regency Play Player controls heir during regency; regent governs realm
Play the Regent Player plays as regent until heir comes of age
86.6 No Heir Scenario
Option Description
Play a Dynasty Relative Cousin, sibling, nephew
Play a Loyal Vassal Player continues as vassal in same realm
End the Dynasty House becomes extinct; may continue as new character
86.7 Dynasty Legacy Record
When a player character dies, record in the Hall of Legends:
Name
Years ruled
Major achievements
Final reputation
How they died
86.8 Inherited Reputation
The heir inherits a modified version of the previous character's reputation:
+50% of positive reputation (rounded down)
+100% of negative reputation (people remember failures longer)
Nicknames may be inherited or new ones earned
86.9 Inherited Claims
The heir inherits all claims of the previous character.
86.10 Heir Ambition Reset
The heir may have different ambitions than the previous character. Roll 1d6 for
their driving motivation:
Roll Ambition
1 Avenge the previous ruler
2 Expand the realm

-- 239 of 529 --

3 Consolidate power at home
4 Seek a royal marriage
5 Gain religious favor
6 Be remembered as great
86.11 Dynasty Survival Rule
A dynasty continues as long as there is any blood relative to carry on. Cadet
branches, bastards, and distant cousins all count.

PART 94: THE SCHEMER (Per Patch 7.26)
94.1 Core Principle — The Breaking
A Schemer is not born. They are made. Made by betrayal, by loss, by humiliation
so complete that the person they were dies — and something else crawls out of
the wreckage.
A Schemer is:
Patient (they will wait years, decades)

-- 249 of 529 --

Cunning (they see moves others miss)
Unreadable (their face shows nothing)
Dangerous (they have nothing left to lose)
A Schemer is not:
Obvious (you will not see them coming)
Forgiving (they remember every slight)
Satisfied (revenge is never enough)
Safe (they will burn the world to warm their hands)
94.2 Trigger Condition — Vanishingly Rare
At the start of each year, for every noble lord in the world who has a grudge,
the system makes a silent roll.
Condition Modifier
Base chance 0.5%
Grudge is against blood relative +2%
Grudge has lasted 5+ years +1%
Grudge has lasted 10+ years +2%
Lord has lost land/family/title to grudge +2%
Lord has been publicly humiliated +1%
Lord is already a Schemer Cannot trigger again
Final Chance: 1d100. If the result is less than or equal to the modified chance,
the lord gains the Schemer trait.
The system does not announce this roll. The player will only know if they are
the target — or if the region begins to destabilize.
94.3 The Schemer's Stats
Attribute Effect
Patience +2 to all long-term plot rolls (plans taking 6+ months)
Cunning +2 to espionage rolls, +2 to framing others
Unreadable -2 to others' attempts to read their intentions
Grudge Multiplier Their grudge is now permanent. Cannot be resolved by apology
or restitution.
Network Automatically gains 1d6 spies within 1 year (if none existed)
94.4 The Schemer's Goal — Regional Destabilization
A Schemer does not just target their enemy. They target the entire region. They
want to watch it burn.
Each year, a Schemer activates 1d3 of the following plots:
Plot Effect
Spread Lies -1 Opinion between two random lords in the region
Blackmail One minor lord becomes secretly loyal to Schemer
Sabotage Trade One trade route is disrupted (-50% income for 1d6 months)
Frame Noble One lord is accused of a crime (real or false)
Assassination Attempt Target a noble (use Part 95)
Foment Rebellion Peasants in one holding become restless (-1 loyalty)
Secret Alliance Two lords are secretly allied against a third (unknown)
Murder Kill a minor noble (if assassination succeeds)
Theft Steal important document/heirloom, use as leverage
Each plot is resolved silently. The player sees only the effects — the rumors,
the betrayals, the sudden deaths.
94.5 The Web — How the Schemer Works
A Schemer does not act alone. They build a web.

-- 250 of 529 --

Web Element Description
Spies 1d6 informants across the region
Blackmailed Lords 1d3 minor lords who owe them
False Friends 1d3 lords who think the Schemer is an ally
Contingencies 1d3 plans that trigger if they are discovered
Hidden Resources 1d6 × 100 SD stashed in secret locations
If the Schemer is discovered:
They do not confess
They do not flee
They activate contingencies
Contingency Examples:
A letter revealing another lord's secret is released
A hired assassin targets the accuser
Evidence is planted on a rival
The Schemer "disappears" and starts again elsewhere
94.6 Regional Instability Track
As the Schemer works, the region becomes unstable.
Level Effects
1-2 Minor rumors. -1 to trade.
3-4 Lords suspicious. -1 Opinion between all pairs.
5-6 Trade disrupted. -25% regional income.
7-8 Assassinations become common. Roll 1d6 each month: on 1, a noble dies.
9-10 Open conflict. Civil war possible. Roll 1d6 each season: on 1-2, war
breaks out.
Each successful Schemer plot increases instability by 1.
Each failed plot (discovered, thwarted) increases instability by 1d3 (the
revelation causes chaos).
94.7 Detecting the Schemer
Players and NPCs may attempt to discover who is behind the chaos.
Investigation Roll: 1d6 + Recon Rating + Espionage vs DC 15 + Schemer's Cunning
Success Information Gained
Fail No leads. The web holds.
Success You identify one of the Schemer's agents (spy, blackmailed lord)
Success by 5+ You identify the Schemer themselves
Success by 10+ You discover the full web (all agents, contingencies)
94.8 Confronting the Schemer
If the Schemer is confronted directly, they do not beg. They do not explain.
They smile — a thin, dead thing — and speak:
"You think this ends with me? I am already dead. I died the day [the grudge
happened]. What stands before you is only the ghost of what they made. Kill me,
and the web lives. My letters go out. My agents act. My plans unfold without
me."
"You cannot win. You can only choose how much you lose."
Action Consequence
If killed All contingents activate simultaneously, 1d6 secrets revealed,
instability increases by 2d6
If imprisoned Contingencies activate one by one, over months. The Schemer
waits. Escape is possible.

-- 251 of 529 --

If exiled The Schemer leaves... but where? They may return in years, with new
plans.
94.9 Cultural Reactions to Schemers
Culture View on Schemers
Central Plains Dishonorable. A knight does not scheme. But they respect the
cunning, privately.
Western Rivers Practical. Scheming is just business by other means.
Eastern Forests (Settled) Uncomfortable. The forest has its own ways. This
feels... wrong.
Eastern Forests (Tribal) The ancestors weep. A soul so broken cannot join
them.
Southern Mountains The mountain remembers. The Schemer will be remembered
too — as a warning.
Northern Snowlands Respect. The cold teaches patience. A Schemer is just
very, very patient.
Nomad Steppe (Traditional) Confused. Why not just fight? Why hide?
Nomad Steppe (Settled) Cautious respect. They understand patience. They do not
trust it.
94.10 The Schemer's End — Possible Outcomes
Outcome Description
Victory The Schemer achieves their goal. Enemy destroyed. Region in ruins.
They sit alone, smiling. Was it worth it?
Death Killed by enemy, by accident, by their own web. The region breathes — but
scars remain.
Madness The scheming consumes them. They see plots everywhere. They become
paranoid, useless.
Redemption Rare. Someone reaches them. Shows them another way. The broken
pieces try to heal. (Almost impossible.)
Disappearance They vanish. The web lives on without them. No one knows if
they are dead or waiting.
PART 95: ASSASSINATION SYSTEM (Pages 227-233 + Per Patch 7.26)
95.1 Core Principle
Murder is a dishonorable last resort. Assassination is never a casual action.
Attempting murder immediately creates a Dishonor Risk against the house if
discovered.
95.2 Murder Plot Creation

Assassin Guild 200-500 SD High Medium
The Fifty (if available) Free (but attrition) Very High Low (to
plotter)
Mercenary 100-300 SD Medium Medium
Fanatic Variable Variable Variable
95.4 Cost of Assassination
Base cost depends on target's security level:
Security Level Base Cost
Low (unprotected) 50 SD
Medium (guards) 150 SD
High (fortified) 300 SD
Extreme (maximum security) 500+ SD
95.5 Plot Development Phase
During planning, the plot may be discovered. Roll 1d6 each week:

-- 252 of 529 --

Roll Result
1 Plot discovered! Target knows.
2-3 Suspicion raised (+1 to target's security)
4-6 No issues
95.6 Plot Exposure Risk
Factors affecting exposure:
Factor Modifier to discovery roll
Plotter has high reputation -1
Plotter is known enemy +2
Using guild assassin -1
Using criminal network +1
Target has spies +1 per spy
95.7 Assassination Attempt
When the plot is ready, resolve the attempt:
Roll 1d6 + Assassin Skill vs Target Security (DC 4-7)
Success Outcome
Fail by 3+ Assassin caught, confesses
Fail by 1-2 Attempt fails, assassin escapes
Success Target killed
Success by 3+ Target killed, no evidence
95.8 Assassin Fate
If the assassin is caught, roll 1d6:
Roll Result
1 Immediately executed
2-3 Talks under torture (reveals plotter)
4-5 Killed resisting arrest
6 Rescued by allies
95.9 Blood Feud Trigger
If a murder is proven, the victim's house gains a Blood Feud Claim:
text
BLOOD FEUD
─────────────────────────────────────
House A: ___________________
House B: ___________________
Cause: Murder of _______________
─────────────────────────────────────
95.10 Murder Cooldown
After a successful assassination, the target's house is on high alert for 1d6
months. Further attempts at that house suffer -2.
95.11 NPC Assassination Plots
NPCs may also plot assassinations against the player or other NPCs. These are
resolved using the same mechanics, hidden from the player until they succeed or
are discovered.
PART 96: THE KINSLAYER'S CURSE (Per Patch 7.23)
96.1 Core Principle
Kinslaying is not just murder. It is the violation of blood, of family, of the
fundamental bond that holds society together. A kinslayer is marked for life —
by gods, by men, by the very earth they walk on.
When a character kills a blood relative (parent, child, sibling, spouse by
marriage counts as blood), they gain the permanent trait: KINSLAYER.
This is not a reputation that can be changed. It can only be:
Endured
Avenged

-- 253 of 529 --

Forgotten (never)
Redeemed (impossible)
96.2 The Kinslayer's Reputation Score
Reputation Effect Modifier
Base Opinion with all who know -3 (permanent)
Additional if kinslaying was for power -2
Additional if kinslaying was accidental -1 (still cursed)
Additional if kinslaying was in self-defense 0 (but still cursed)
Additional if kinslaying was witnessed publicly -2
Additional if kinslaying was hidden but discovered -3
Minimum Opinion Cap: No character with the Kinslayer trait can have an Opinion
higher than +1 with anyone who knows.
96.3 The Gods Remember
Faith Reaction
Crownbound Church Excommunication. No burial in consecrated ground. Priests
refuse last rites.
Ashen Covenant "The king fell because the realm was corrupt. You are proof."
+1 to fear, -3 to trust.
Silent Faith Silence. They do not speak to you. They do not look at you.
You are dead to them.
Tribal Spirits The ancestors wail. You will never receive their guidance
again.
Nomad Sky Your name is removed from the clan. You are wind. You are nothing.
96.4 Cultural Views on Kinslaying
Central Plains — The Broken Oath of Blood
"Blood is the first oath. Before lord, before king, before god — family. Break
that, and nothing you ever swear will be trusted again."
Punishment: Exile or execution
Accidental: Exile, monastery, penance
Legacy: Name stricken, children watched
Western Rivers — The Broken Contract
"You signed no contract with your brother. But blood is the oldest contract of
all. Break it, and no one will sign with you again."
Punishment: Economic death, blacklist
Accidental: Severe fines, public apology
Legacy: No contracts, no loans, no trade
Eastern Forests (Settled) — The Hunter Who Preys on His Own Pack
"A wolf does not kill his brother. If he does, the pack drives him out. And the
forest takes him."
Punishment: Cast out, no aid, death if return
Accidental: Year of silence, face tattoo
Legacy: Name forgotten, family may change name
Eastern Forests (Tribal) — The Ancestors Scream
"The ancestors do not forget. They do not forgive. They wait. They watch. They
remember."
Punishment: Immediate banishment

-- 254 of 529 --

Accidental: Still banished, but offerings left
Legacy: Name never spoken, spirit wanders
Southern Mountains — The Mountain Remembers
"The mountain does not forget blood. It soaks into the stone. It becomes part of
the rock. Every generation who walks this pass will feel it."
Punishment: Execution
Accidental: Exile, year of wandering, hand brand
Legacy: Place cursed, family watches children
Northern Snowlands — The Cold Beyond Cold
"The cold kills the weak. But kinslaying? That kills something else. Something
the cold cannot touch. The soul."
Punishment: Cast out into winter
Accidental: Trial of Frost (3 days, no shelter)
Legacy: Shame lasts generations, names changed
Nomad Steppe (Traditional) — The Wind Does Not Carry Your Name
"We are the wind. We are the grass. We are the herd. One horse does not kill
another. If it does, the herd drives it out. The wolves take it."
Punishment: Exile with nothing
Accidental: Still exile, but with horse and water
Legacy: Name erased, clan moves on
Nomad Steppe (Settled) — The Law and the Grass
"We have two laws now — the old and the new. Both say the same thing: do not
kill your blood. The old law exiles you. The new law hangs you. Choose your
death."
Punishment: Execution (settled) or exile (traditional)
Accidental: Prison (settled) or exile with supplies (trad)
Legacy: Family chooses which law to follow
96.5 Kinslaying and Inheritance
A kinslayer cannot inherit anything from the victim. Additionally:
Situation Effect
Kinslayer was next in line Line passes to next eligible relative
Kinslayer has children Children may inherit only if they publicly denounce
parent
Kinslayer is already ruling Rule is considered illegitimate. Vassals may
rebel.
Victim had no other heirs Title goes to liege lord or nearest collateral
line
Victim's spouse remarries Children of new marriage take precedence
The Tainted Blood: Some cultures believe the kinslayer's children are also
tainted. They may be passed over for inheritance even if innocent.
96.6 Kinslaying Through Assassination
If an assassination target is a blood relative of the plotter:
Factor Modifier

-- 255 of 529 --

Discovery chance +3 (servants talk, family notices)
Evidence left Automatic (someone always knows)
Blood Feud trigger Automatic, permanent, multi-generational
Reputation loss -5 (minimum)
If Discovered:
Immediate blood feud with entire family
No negotiation. No ransom. No mercy.
The plotter becomes the most hated person in the region
PART 97: THE LORD'S COUNCIL (Per Patch 7.31)
97.1 Core Principle
Every great lord (Tier 4+) has a council. It is not a modern cabinet of
appointed experts. It is a living assembly of family representatives from the
six most powerful minor houses sworn to him.
These council members are:
Blood relatives of the minor lords
Sent to represent their family's interests
Expected to advise, lobby, and sometimes obstruct
A check on the great lord's power
A source of wisdom — and conflict
97.2 Council Composition
Each great lord's council has six seats, one for each of the six most
significant minor houses in their domain.
Seat Representative Family Role
First Seat Most powerful minor house Usually the lord's brother or eldest
son
Second Seat Second most powerful Cousin or trusted uncle
Third Seat Third most powerful Experienced family member
Fourth Seat Fourth most powerful Ambitious younger son
Fifth Seat Fifth most powerful Elder statesman
Sixth Seat Sixth most powerful Wildcard — varies by house
97.3 Who Sits? Family Representatives
Relation Typical Role
Brother Trusted, speaks with the lord's full authority
Cousin Reliable, may have own ambitions
Uncle Experienced, often wiser than the lord
Nephew Young, eager, learning
Son (not heir) Ambitious, seeking influence
Grandfather (rare) Ancient, respected, speaks rarely
Mother (rare) If she holds wealth or influence
Daughter (rare) If she is the power behind the house
97.4 Women on Council
Women rarely sit on councils — but it happens. Conditions:
Condition Example
She holds significant personal wealth A merchant princess or wealthy widow
She commands trained men A warrior woman with her own retinue
She is the power behind the minor lord The lord is weak; she rules
She is the last of her line No male heirs
She is exceptionally wise or cunning Her advice is too valuable to ignore
97.5 Council Powers
Power Description
Advise The lord may ask — or they may offer unbidden

-- 256 of 529 --

Lobby Members push for policies that benefit their houses
Obstruct If united, they can delay or block action
Inform They bring news from their regions
Judge They watch the lord. If he falters, they remember.
Choose If the lord dies without clear heir, the council may have a voice
Unified Council: If all six members agree on a course of action, the lord faces
a choice:
Accept their advice (+2 loyalty from all minor houses)
Reject their advice (-1 loyalty, risk of obstruction)
97.6 Council Dynamics
Dynamic Effect
Allies Two members support each other. +1 to lobbying.
Rivals Two members oppose each other. -1 to council stability.
Feud Members from feuding houses. Council may deadlock.
Faction Three+ members form voting bloc. Can dominate.
Outsider One member isolated. May seek lord's favor.
Council Stability Track:
Score Meaning
1-3 Constant infighting. -1 to all advice rolls.
4-6 Tense but functional. No modifier.
7-9 Cooperative. +1 to advice rolls.
10 Unified. Rare. Powerful. Dangerous to lord.
97.7 Losing a Seat — Blood in the Water
When a minor house loses its council seat, it is a public humiliation. Other
houses notice. Predators circle.
Effect Description
Reputation Loss -2 to house reputation
Rival Houses +1 aggression from other minor houses
Internal Strife Family may blame the representative
Desperation The house may take risks to regain favor
Succession Questions Is this house still worth allying with?
97.8 Council Sessions
Session Type Frequency Purpose
Regular Session Weekly/Monthly Routine business, reports, minor decisions
War Council As needed Military strategy, levies, supplies
Crisis Session Emergency Invasion, rebellion, lord's illness
Feast Session Seasonal Celebrations, marriages, announcements
97.9 The Lord's Relationship with Council
Approach Effect
Listen Council feels valued. +1 stability.
Ignore Council resents you. -1 stability. May obstruct.
Play Favorites Favored houses +1 loyalty, others -1.
Pit Them Against Each Other Keeps them weak, but stability drops.
Replace Members Can remove individuals, but causes resentment.
Abolish Council Possible? Yes. Wise? No. Rebellion likely.
97.10 Cultural Variations
Culture Council Practice
Central Plains Formal council. Six seats. Strict hierarchy. First Seat speaks
first.
Western Rivers Practical council. Seats based on wealth as much as land.
Merchants may sit.
Eastern Forests (Settled) Council exists, but less formal. Members roam,
speak when moved.
Eastern Forests (Tribal) No council. Elders speak for families, but not in
a lord's hall. They do not gather this way.
Southern Mountains Council is ancient. Seats are hereditary — a house that
loses a seat loses it forever.
Northern Snowlands Council is a circle. No head. The lord sits among them.
All speak freely.

-- 257 of 529 --

Nomad Steppe (Traditional) No council. The khan's brothers and sons advise —
but they ride with him, not sit in a hall.
Nomad Steppe (Settled) Adapted council. Six seats, but members may be women
(steppe tradition of female leaders).
97.11 Generating a Council
Step 1 — Select Six Minor Houses
Choose the six most powerful or loyal minor houses in the lord's domain.
Step 2 — For Each, Determine Representative
Roll 1d6:
Roll Representative
1 Lord's brother
2 Lord's cousin
3 Lord's uncle
4 Lord's nephew
5 Lord's son (not heir)
6 Special (grandfather, mother, daughter, etc.)
Step 3 — Determine Gender (if special)
Roll 1d6 for female representative (only if conditions met):
Roll Outcome
1-4 Male (standard)
5 Female (wealth/influence)
6 Female (exceptional — wise, cunning, warrior)
Step 4 — Generate Personality
Roll 1d6 for each member's disposition toward the lord:
Roll Disposition
1 Loyal supporter
2 Pragmatic ally
3 Neutral
4 Skeptical
5 Ambitious (wants more for his house)
6 Hostile (secretly works against lord)
Step 5 — Generate Relationships Between Members
Roll 1d6 for each pair (or use narrative):
Roll Relationship
1 Blood allies (same faction)
2 Friendly
3 Neutral
4 Rivals
5 Feuding
6 Secret alliance against another
97.12 Council and Succession
If a great lord dies without a clear heir, the council may have a role.
Situation Council Role
Clear heir exists Council confirms, may advise regent
Disputed succession Council may choose — or split, causing civil war
No heir Council may elect new lord from among their houses
Lord assassinated Council investigates, may seek vengeance
Council Election: If the council chooses a new lord, that lord owes them.
Council seats become permanent, power shifts.
PART 98: THE PLAYER ON COUNCIL (Per Patch 7.32)
98.1 Players as Council Members
A player who holds land as a minor lord (Tier 2-3) may be granted a seat on
their liege's council if they are among the six most powerful or favored houses
in the domain.
Gaining a Seat:

-- 258 of 529 --

Method Description
Inheritance Your house already holds a seat; you take it
Favor Your liege grants you a seat due to exceptional service
Conquest You defeat a house that holds a seat
Purchase Rare, but coin can buy influence
Marriage Marry into a house that holds a seat
98.2 Player's Representative on Council
If the player holds a council seat but cannot attend a session personally, they
may send a representative.
Who May Represent:
Representative Acceptance Notes
Blood relative Acceptable Family speaks with family's voice
Spouse Acceptable (if respected) Especially if they hold influence
Retainer Suspicious "You send a servant to advise me?"
Mercenary Unacceptable "I do not take counsel from sellswords."
No one Better than bad choice Absence is noted, but not insulting
The Great Lord's Reaction:
Lord's Personality Reaction to Blood Relative Reaction to Retainer
Patient, understanding Accepts, -0 loyalty -1 loyalty
Proud, traditional -1 loyalty -2 loyalty
Paranoid, suspicious -2 loyalty -3 loyalty
Pragmatic, focused Accepts if competent Accepts grudgingly
98.3 War Council — The Lord's Second
In times of war, the council transforms. It becomes a War Council — and the
rules change.
War Council Attendance:
Who Attendance Rule
The great lord Must attend
Minor lords Expected to attend personally
Council members Expected to attend personally
Representatives Not allowed in War Council
The Exception — The Lord's Second:
If a minor lord cannot attend a War Council personally, they may send a Second —
but only if that Second is:
Acceptable Second Condition
Blood relative Must be of age and capable
Trusted commander Must have proven themselves in battle
The lord's heir Acceptable, even encouraged
Unacceptable Seconds:
Stewards (non-combatants)
Servants
Anyone who has never fought
Anyone the great lord does not know
98.4 The Suspicion
Great lords view absence from War Council with deep suspicion.
Situation Reaction
First absence, good reason -1 loyalty, "See that it does not happen again."
Second absence, any reason -2 loyalty, "Your lord tests my patience."
Third absence -3 loyalty, possible loss of seat
Absence during crisis Automatic -2 loyalty, possible accusations of cowardice
98.5 Player as Second for Another Lord

-- 259 of 529 --

A player may be asked to serve as Second for their liege or another minor lord.
Role Effect
Represent your liege +1 favor with liege, but you speak for him — carefully
Represent fellow minor lord +1 relation with that house, but you carry their
risk
Represent yourself? You cannot — you are either the lord or the second
If the player serves as Second:
They have full voting/advising rights for that session
They are treated as the lord they represent
They are personally responsible for any offense given
If they perform well, they gain reputation
If they perform poorly, they harm their lord's standing
98.6 Political Consequences of Absence
Consequence Effect
Rumors spread "Lord [Name] sends others to speak for him. Is he weak? Ill?
Afraid?"
Rivals gain ground Your absence is their opportunity to lobby against you.
Decisions made without you Policies that harm your interests may pass.
Loyalty drops Your liege wonders if you are committed.
Seat at risk If you miss too many sessions, another house may claim your
place.
Missed Sessions in a Row:
Missed Sessions Effect
1 -1 opinion from liege
2 -2 opinion, rivals +1 to lobbying
3 -3 opinion, liege may demand explanation
4+ Seat may be declared vacant; roll 1d6: 1-3 = lose seat
98.7 Cultural Variations on Attendance

barely tolerated. Blood relatives only.

Suspicion if incompetent.

99.5 The Nature of Regions — Borders That Breathe
Regions do not change at a line on a map. They transition. A traveler leaving
the Central Plains for the Eastern Forest does not step from grassland to deep
wood at a single step. First, the grass grows taller, then scrub appears, then
scattered trees, then thin forest, then finally the ancient canopy. These
transition zones are where cultures blend, where border lords hold sway, and
where travelers must be most alert. A day's travel can take you through three

-- 261 of 529 --

distinct ecological zones — and three different political realities.
99.6 Border Zones — Where Regions Meet
Every border between regions has a transition depth of 1-3 days of travel where
the landscape gradually changes.
Transition Depth Table
Border Transition Days Primary Change
Central Plains — Western Rivers 2 days Grass gives way to marshes, then
open water
Central Plains — Eastern Forests 3 days Grass — scrub — scattered trees —
forest
Central Plains — Southern Mountains 2 days Plains rise into foothills, then
mountains
Central Plains — Northern Snowlands 3 days Grass thins, cools, becomes
tundra, then snow
Western Rivers — Eastern Forests 2 days Marshes become swamp forest, then
dry forest
Western Rivers — Southern Mountains 2 days River valleys narrow, rise into
highlands
Western Rivers — Northern Snowlands 3 days Rivers slow, freeze part-year,
tundra appears
Eastern Forests — Southern Mountains 2 days Forest thins, climbs,
becomes alpine
Eastern Forests — Northern Snowlands 3 days Deciduous gives way to
coniferous, then taiga
Southern Mountains — Northern Snowlands 4 days High peaks gradually lower
into frozen hills
Nomad Steppe — Any region 2-4 days Grass slowly changes to destination
terrain
Transition Zone Effects:
Effect Description
Mixed Terrain Treat as both regions for travel purposes (use slower rate)

Position: South of the Great Falls, where the North Road crosses the Velrin

DESCRIPTION:
Stonebridge is exactly what its name suggests — a massive stone bridge
spanning the Velrin, with a town built on both banks and connected by
the bridge itself. The bridge is fortified with towers at both ends,
and a small castle perches on the island at its center. The sound of
water rushing over stone is constant, a low roar that residents stop
noticing after a week. The town smells of river water, fish, and the
coal smoke from the smithies that line the waterfront.
KEY FEATURES:
• The Bridge itself — 400 feet long, 30 feet wide, with room for two wagons
• The Island Castle — seat of the local lord, commands the crossing
• The Docks — on both banks, serving river traffic

• The Smithy District — famous for high-quality horseshoes and tools
