PART 101: THE ART OF FORMAL INTRODUCTION (Pages 316-318)
101.1 Northern Introduction Customs
"The north does not introduce — it presents. A northern lord's second speaks as
if carving the words into stone."
Format: Direct, minimal flourish, maximum impact
Example:
"Harald of the Northern Hold. Warden of the North. Bear of the Snowlands. Slayer
of the Frost Wolf. He has never broken an oath. He has never lost a friend. He
has never forgotten a face."
Northern Introduction Table (1d6):
Roll Opening Phrase Deed Style Closing
1 "Hearken to the name of..." Battle victory "He remembers."
2 "The north sends..." Hunt of a great beast "The frost knows him."
3 "From the snow comes..." Defense of the weak "Winter tested him.
He passed."
4 "Bear witness to..." Oath kept at great cost "His word is iron."
5 "Let the hall fall silent for..." Land settled/defended "He does not
kneel."
6 "The cold has forged..." Legacy of ancestors "The north
remembers."
101.2 Southern Introduction Customs
"The south introduces with flourish. Titles are savored, deeds are sung, and
every word is chosen to impress."
Format: Elaborate, poetic, designed for courts and feasts
Example:
"I present Lord Caldrin of the Southern Hills, Warden of the South, Keeper of
the Golden Vines, Master of the Sunvault! He whose sword has never faltered,
whose word has never wavered, whose wine has never soured! He who brought the
southern lords to unity! He who gave his daughter to a wolf and gained an
empire! All hail Lord Caldrin!"
Southern Introduction Table (1d6):
Roll Opening Flourish Deed Style Closing Chorus
1 "Behold, the noble..." Tournament victory "All hail!"
2 "Let the trumpets sound for..." Diplomatic triumph "Let his name
be praised!"
3 "The south sends its finest..." Marriage/alliance "Glory to his house!"
4 "In golden light appears..." Construction of wonder "Long may he reign!"
5 "Hear the name acclaimed..." Scholarship/art "Honor to his line!"
6 "The sun itself rises for..." Religious devotion "The light guides
him!"
101.3 Eastern Forest (Tribal) Introduction Customs
"The forest does not shout. The forest whispers. A tribal elder's second speaks
softly, but every word carries the weight of generations."
Format: Quiet, ancient, spoken as if sharing sacred knowledge

Example:
"The wind carries a name. Thorn of the Hidden Vale. She who walks unseen. She
who speaks to the ancestors. She whose arrows find truth. The forest knows her.
The spirits guide her. She is here."

spirits guide."

PART 102: THOSE WHO FOLLOW THE HERDS — COMPLETE TRIBAL LORE (Pages 503-517 + Per
Mega Patch 6.0)
102.1 Core Principle — They Were Never Lost
The lords tell stories about the tribes. They always have. In those stories, the
tribes are "lost" — lost to civilization, lost to progress, lost to the old ways
that sensible people abandoned generations ago.
The tribes do not tell those stories.
They tell different ones. In their stories, they were never lost. They knew
exactly where they were. They knew where the herds went, where the water ran,
where the hidden valleys waited. They knew the land in ways the lords could
never understand, because the lords never asked. They only took.
When the lords came — from the north, the south, the west — the tribes watched.
They saw the first refugees stagger out of the snowlands, starving and
desperate. They saw the traders sail up the rivers, greedy and clever. They saw
the conquerors ride across the plains, cruel and certain.
The tribes could have fought. Sometimes they did. But fighting every newcomer
would have drained them, and the land had taught them patience. So they did
something the lords could not understand: they withdrew.
Not in fear. Not in defeat. In choice.
They walked away from the grass the conquerors wanted. They left the river bends
the traders coveted. They abandoned the valleys the refugees settled. They went
to places the lords would never find — hidden hunting grounds, secret valleys,
high pastures that existed on no map.
The lords thought they had won. They thought the tribes were gone, broken,
finished.
The tribes laughed in their tents and said nothing.
Let the lords build their castles. Let them fight their wars. Let them count
their coins and swear their oaths and die for their kings. The tribes would
wait. The land would wait. The herds would wait.
And when the lords finally needed something they could not get from their
castles — furs for winter, knowledge of hidden passes, warriors who could move
like shadows — the tribes would still be there. Watching. Waiting. Remembering.
They are not lost. They are exactly where they have always been.
102.2 The Coming of the Lords — Three Migrations
102.2.1 The Northern Refugees: Those Who Came Cold

-- 271 of 529 --

The Story the Lords Tell:
Generations ago, the Northern Snowlands grew too harsh even for their hardy
people. Families packed what they could and walked south, seeking warmer lands,
better hunting, a chance to survive. They found the Central Plains. They found
grass, game, rivers. The land seemed empty, and so they settled. They built
their halls along the rivers. They became the Northern lords.
The Story the Tribes Tell:
A band of starving people came out of the snow. They had nothing — no food, no
knowledge of the land, no idea how to survive. The tribes watched from the
forest edge and decided to help. Not because they were kind — kindness is for
those who can afford it. They helped because the starving ones had children, and
children can learn. They taught the newcomers where to find water, how to follow
the herds, which roots fed and which killed. The newcomers learned. They
survived. They settled along the rivers — not taking, just staying. The tribes
did not mind. There was room enough.
Then more came. And more. And they stopped being grateful. They started
building, fencing, claiming. They forgot who had taught them to survive.
The tribes could have fought. Instead, they moved. There were always other
valleys, other hunting grounds, other places the newcomers had not found. The
tribes went there and let the newcomers have the river bends.
The newcomers thought they had won. They called themselves lords. They forgot
the faces of the people who had saved them.
The tribes did not forget. They never forget.
102.2.2 The Western Traders: Those Who Came by Water
The Story the Lords Tell:
Brave explorers from the western kingdoms sailed up the great rivers, seeking
new lands, new trade, new wealth. They found a wild country rich in furs and
timber. They built trading posts at river confluences, forts on high ground.
They traded fairly with the simple folk they found, bringing iron and cloth in
exchange for hides and knowledge. Over time, the trading posts became towns. The
towns became cities. The traders became lords. They brought civilization to a
savage land.
The Story the Tribes Tell:
The river people saw the boats long before they arrived. Strange vessels, high-
sided, moving against the current with oars and sails. The people watched from
the reeds and wondered. When the boats landed, the strangers did not attack.
They offered gifts — shiny metal, colored cloth, things the people had never
seen. They wanted furs. They wanted to trade. The river people were suspicious,
but curious. They traded. A few hides for a few knives. It seemed fair.
The strangers stayed. They built posts at the river bends — small at first, then
larger. They brought more goods, more traders, more strange customs. Some
married into the river people. Some learned the language. Some seemed almost
like kin.
But they never stopped wanting more. More furs. More hides. More land. More
control. When the river people objected, the traders called on their lords.
Armed men came up the river. They did not ask this time. They took.
The river people could have fought. Instead, they faded into the countless
streams, the shallow waters where the big boats could not follow. They went to
places the traders would never find — hidden lakes, marsh villages, camps on
islands that existed on no map.
The traders thought they had won. They called the rivers theirs. They built
their cities on the banks and forgot the people who had shown them the way.

-- 272 of 529 --

The river people did not forget. They wait in the reeds, watching. The big boats
cannot follow. The marshes remember.
102.2.3 The Southern Conquerors: Those Who Came on Horses
The Story the Lords Tell:
Great warriors from the southern kingdoms rode north on magnificent horses,
seeking glory, seeking land, seeking a kingdom of their own. They found a rich
plain, empty of civilization, ripe for the taking. They conquered it in a single
generation, building castles on the best grass, establishing laws where there
had been none. They brought order to chaos. They became the High Kings.
The Story the Tribes Tell:
The plains people felt the thunder long before they saw the riders. The ground
trembled. The herds scattered. Something terrible was coming.
Then they came — men on horses, strange beasts that ran faster than any runner,
carried men farther than any walker. The riders had iron swords, iron
discipline, iron hearts. They did not ask. They did not trade. They took.
They took the best grass. They took the watering holes. They took the places
where the herds gathered. They built castles on the ancient hunting grounds and
said: "This is ours now."
The plains people fought. They had bows and courage and knowledge of the land.
They killed many riders. But the riders kept coming, more and more, and the
horses gave them speed the people could not match.
The elders gathered. They made a choice that broke their hearts: they would
leave. They would go to places the riders could not follow — the deep valleys,
the high pastures, the hidden places where horses could not go.
They walked away from their ancestral grounds. They left the grass to the
conquerors. They went into the margins and survived.
The conquerors thought they had won. They built their castles. They crowned
their kings. They forgot that the land had owners before them.
The plains people did not forget. They wait in the high places, watching. The
horses will age. The castles will crumble. The grass will remember who loved it
first.
102.3 The Hidden Places
The lords have maps. Beautiful maps, drawn with care, showing every castle,
every road, every river. They think their maps show everything. They do not.
The hidden places do not appear on any lordly map. They cannot be found by
scouts. They cannot be reached by armies. They exist in the spaces between,
known only to those born there and those trusted to find them.
102.3.1 The High Pastures
Above the tree line, where the air thins and the wind never stops, there are
valleys that hold grass all summer. The herds come here to calve. The people
come here to hunt. The lords never follow — their horses cannot breathe the thin
air, their soldiers cannot climb the steep approaches.
Mechanical:
Reaching the high pastures requires 3x normal travel time
Horses cannot enter

-- 273 of 529 --

+2 to hunting rolls during summer
102.3.2 The Deep Forest Glades
In the heart of the great forest, where the trees grow so thick that sunlight
barely reaches the ground, there are clearings that exist on no map. They are
surrounded by impenetrable brush, reachable only by paths that shift with the
seasons.
Mechanical:
+4 to detection threshold for these glades
Scouts simply cannot find them without a guide
+2 to ambush rolls for anyone defending these places
102.3.3 The Marsh Islands
The great wetlands stretch for miles — water, reeds, mud, islands of solid
ground that rise just above the waterline. The channels between them shift with
every flood. The river people navigate these channels in their shallow canoes.
Mechanical:
Only canoes can navigate the marshes
River ships cannot enter
+2 to defense when attacked in the marshes
102.3.4 The River Caves
Behind certain waterfalls, where the water thunders down and spray hides
everything, there are caves. Some are small. Some are vast — underground
chambers that hold whole communities. The entrances are underwater, reachable
only by diving.
Mechanical:
These caves are not on any map
They cannot be found without a guide
Have sheltered refugees for generations
102.3.5 The Shield Rock Mazes
In the ancient stone lands, where glaciers scraped the rock into labyrinths of
ridge and ravine, there are places where the stone creates natural mazes. Only
those born here know the paths.
Mechanical:
Movement is 4x normal for anyone without a guide
-4 to all reconnaissance rolls
The land itself confuses outsiders

102.11 Tribal Military (Per Mega Patch 6.0)
Tribal warriors do not fight like lords. They strike from cover, vanish, strike
again. They know every fold of the land. They move where armies cannot.
Band Size Warriors Raiding Parties Special

Large (800-1500) 160-300 80-150 0 stealth

Action Consequence

Scouts in tribal territory 50% never return
Supplies in transit 25% spoiled or stolen
Messengers 1 in 3 intercepted
Border villages 1d3 raided per season

102.12 The Lines That Cannot Be Crossed
Action Tribal Response
Discovering a hidden valley The scout does not return
Killing a sacred animal Blood price demanded; if not paid, blood feud
Breaking a trade agreement No further trade. Caravans raided.
Taking children as wards or hostages War. The tribe will not rest until the
child returns.
Fencing the calving grounds War on the herds. War on the lord.
Cutting old-growth forest The trees will be avenged.
Escalation:
First offense: A warning. A dead animal left at the lord's gate.
Second offense: A raid. A caravan taken. A village granary burned.
Third offense: War. The lord's people die until the lord understands.
102.13 Corrected Flavor Text — The Tribal Voice (Per Mega Patch 6.0)
text
"They look at the river's edge and see water.
They look at the highlands and see empty hills.
They do not see us. They have never seen us.
But we see them. We have always seen them.
From every valley, every glade, every hidden place
they do not know exists — we watch.
If we ever rise together, they will learn
what it means to be outnumbered in their own land.
But rising together means showing ourselves.
And once seen, we can never be unseen again."
— Elder of the Hidden Vale
PART 103: THE STEPPE NATIONS — COMPLETE NOMAD LORE (Pages 517-526 + Per Mega
Patch 6.0)
103.1 Core Principle — The Grass Sea
The steppe is not a place that can be owned. It is a sea of grass, and like the
ocean, it belongs to no one and everyone. Those who try to fence it, to claim
it, to build walls upon it — they do not understand. The grass will grow over
their walls. The wind will erase their names.
The nomads do not own the steppe. They belong to it. They follow the grass, the
water, the herds. Their homes move with the seasons. Their wealth walks on four

-- 276 of 529 --

legs. Their ancestors ride the wind.
103.2 Population
Region Nomad Population
Nomad Steppe 8d6 × 1000
Central Plains (trade camps) 1d6 × 100
Western Rivers (river markets) 1d6 × 100
Eastern Forests None
Southern Mountains None
Northern Snowlands None
103.3 The Call of the Grass
A nomad away from the steppe for more than one year must roll 1d6 each season:
Roll Result
1-2 Growing restlessness (-1 Morale, -1 to all actions until return)
3-4 No effect yet
5-6 Adapting — may stay longer (reset clock)
After three years, if still away, the nomad is considered lost to the grass.
They may settle, or they may simply never go home. They are no longer truly of
the steppe.
103.4 Why Nomads Leave the Steppe
Reason Numbers Duration
Trade Small caravans (20-50) Seasonal (spring/autumn)
Mercenary work Warbands (30-100) Contract length, then return
Exile Individuals or families Permanent — cannot return
Diplomacy Small delegations Short-term
Exploration Scouts (5-10) Temporary, rare
103.5 The Way of the Steppe — Cultural Values (Per Mega Patch 6.0)
The Horse Bond
A nomad without a horse is not a nomad. The bond is sacred.
Bond Level Meaning Game Effect
Foal-bonded Raised horse from birth +2 to mounted rolls, horse will die for
rider
Battle-bonded Survived battles together +1 mounted, horse less likely to
flee
Trade horse Bought, practical Standard mount
Horseless No horse -1 to all rolls (shame), not considered adult
Hospitality
A stranger at the tent must be fed before their name is asked.
Action Effect
Feed stranger without asking +1 reputation among nomads
Ask name before feeding -1 reputation (dangerous, rude)
Refuse hospitality Blood feud possible
Silence
Silence is not empty. It is communication.
Silence Length Meaning
Short pause Considering, respect
Long pause (shared fire) Deep trust, no words needed
Long pause (stranger) Judgment, testing
Silence after question You have answered poorly; try again
103.6 The Warrior's Path — Earning a Name (Per Mega Patch 6.0)
A nomad does not receive a war name. They earn it.
Deed Name Example Recognition
First kill in battle "[Name] Slayer" Acknowledged by clan
Capture 10+ horses in raid "[Name] Herd-Taker" Known in region
Save a clanmate's life "[Name] Life-Giver" Respected

-- 277 of 529 --

Ride 3 days without rest "[Name] Far-Rider" Noted as scout
Kill a known enemy champion "[Name] Eagle-Fall" Feared
Survive alone in blizzard "[Name] Ice-Walker" Legendary
The Naming Ceremony
When a deed is recognized, the clan gathers. The eldest elder speaks the new
name three times. If no one objects, the name is accepted.
If someone objects, the claimant must defend their right to the name — in
combat, in debate, or by producing witnesses.

Khan Clan leader By clan name only
Noyan War leader, commander By war name
Baghatur Champion, proven warrior By war name with honor
Elder Advisor, wisdom-keeper "Grandfather/Grandmother"
Herder Base clan member By trader name
Outcast Exiled, dishonored No name spoken

Nomads have specific ways of interacting with non-nomads.

Level Behavior How to Achieve

PART 104: CAMPAIGN INTRODUCTION SCROLL (Pages 316-318)
104.1 Trigger Condition
The scroll triggers when Character Creation = Complete. Immediately before the
campaign begins, the system generates a Scroll Event.
104.2 The Chronicle Scroll
text
A CHRONICLER'S SCROLL
On the Nature of the Realm
To the one who now carries a banner in these troubled lands,
Know this before you ride into the age before us.
The kings are dead. Their thrones sit empty, their heirs scattered,
and their banners broken across the realm. What remains is a world
held together by fragile oaths, ambitious lords, and the quiet hopes
of the smallfolk who endure the wars of their betters.
You now enter what scholars have begun calling The Age of Shattered Oaths.
Before you march, you would be wise to understand how such a world truly

-- 278 of 529 --

works.
A Lord Must Think Beyond the Sword
Victory is not won only on the battlefield. Armies march on roads and rivers.
Kingdoms rise and fall through alliances and supply. Wars are often decided
by the information you possess before the battle begins.
Send scouts. Use spies. Listen to merchants and messengers. Those who
understand the flow of the realm command more than soldiers — they command
fate itself.
Not Every Battle Requires Your Presence
You may lead your army personally, but many wars are fought by commanders
in your name. When you send forces away, their fate will be carried back
to you through war reports delivered by messengers.
Some victories will arrive as triumphant letters. Some defeats will arrive
stained with mud and blood. Patience is often the greatest weapon of a
commander.
The World Moves Even When You Do Not
The realm does not wait. Seasons change. Minor lords rise. Armies march
along distant roads. Rumors spread across taverns and courts.
Sometimes the wisest choice is to watch the world move before making your
next decision.
Your Banner Is Your Legacy
Whether you command a castle, a company of riders, or nothing but a name
remembered in whispers, your banner represents more than soldiers.
It represents your reputation, your word, and your legacy.
Allies will judge you by it. Enemies will fear it. History will remember it.
Guard it well.
Speak When the Battle Begins
When you ride into battle, your soldiers will look to you. A few words
spoken before a charge can steady the hearts of men — or ignite them into
legends. Do not underestimate the power of a voice heard across the ranks.
Above All Else
Remember that certainty is rare in this age. Messengers may never arrive.
Allies may betray you. Victories may crumble into disasters.
Yet from such chaos, the greatest stories of the realm are born.
Ride wisely. Write your name into the chronicles of this broken world.
Signed and sealed by the Archivists of the Broken Throne.
104.3 After Scroll Completion
Once the scroll is delivered, the system proceeds to:
text
DATE: Day 1 of Greening
TIME: Morning
LOCATION: [Player's Starting Location]
Then gameplay begins.
PART 105: IMPRISONMENT & ESCAPE SYSTEM (Per Mega Patch 6.0)
105.1 Core Principle
An unwatched man can slip away when everyone sleeps. A man in an iron cage with
hands, feet, eyes bound and mouth gagged does nothing but wait for whatever
comes next.

-- 279 of 529 --

105.2 Escape Difficulty Table
Restraint Level Escape Chance Modifiers
Unwatched Camp 1d6, 5-6 escape +1 if familiar with terrain
Guarded Camp 1d6, 6 escape -1 if chained
Wooden Cage 1d6, 6 escape after 3+ days Requires outside help or tool
Iron Cage No escape possible alone Waits. Watches. Hopes.
Dungeon Cell 1d6, 6 with inside help Requires rescue or bribery
Iron Cage, Bound, Gagged, Blindfolded 0% —
105.3 Rescue Attempt Mechanics
Mission Type Difficulty
Night Rescue Depends on guards
Bribe Depends on guard loyalty
Raid Depends on fortifications
Exchange Depends on hostage value
105.4 Prisoner Treatment Effects
Treatment Effect on Prisoner
Well-treated Can be ransomed, may owe favor, no lasting harm
Poorly treated -1 to stats on release, may bear grudge
Starved -2 to stats, may die if prolonged
Tortured Permanent scars, possible broken spirit, eternal hatred
Forgotten Prisoner may die before rescue/ransom
105.5 The Waiting — Life in Captivity
A prisoner does not simply wait. They exist. Days pass. Food arrives or does
not. Guards change. Hope fades or flares.
Each week of imprisonment, roll 1d6:
Roll Result
1 Mistreatment — Guards are cruel. Roll on Mistreatment table.
2 Isolation — No contact. Roll 1d6: 1-2 = despondent (-1 to stats on
release)
3 Routine — Nothing changes. Boring but safe.
4 Contact — A guard speaks. Possible bribe or information.
5 Visitor — Someone comes to see you. Friend? Enemy? Messenger?
6 Opportunity — Escape chance presents itself (see below).
105.6 Mistreatment Table (1d6)
If guards are cruel:
Roll Result
1 Beaten — Light wound. Permanent scar.
2 Starved — -1 to stats on release. May die if prolonged.
3 Tortured — Roll 1d6: 1-2 = broken spirit (-2), 3-4 = defiant (+1), 5-6 =
secrets revealed
4 Humiliated — Reputation loss if witnessed. Grudge forms.
5 Chained — Escape DC increases by +2.
6 Moved — New location, new hope, new risks.
105.7 Escape Opportunities
When an opportunity presents itself:
Roll 1d6 + relevant modifiers:
Roll Result
1-2 Attempt fails. Caught. Punished. Escape DC +1 permanently.
3-4 Partial success. You escape but are wounded/hunted.
5-6 Full success. You are free.
If caught after escape: Executed or moved to Iron Cage (0% escape).
105.8 Ransom Negotiation
When a prisoner is held for ransom:
Step 1 — Determine Value: Use Commander/Noble Value tables (Part 45)
Step 2 — Message reaches their house: Roll 1d6, 4+ success
Step 3 — Response (1d6):

-- 280 of 529 --

Roll Result
1-2 Refuse to pay. "They are dead to us." Prisoner may be executed or rot.
3-4 Negotiate. Counter-offer at 50-75% value. May take 1d6 weeks.
5-6 Pay immediately. Full ransom within 1d6 weeks.
105.9 Prisoner Exchange
Instead of ransom:
Exchange Rate:
1 Noble = 1 Noble of equal rank
1 Commander = 1 Commander of equal tier
10 Soldiers = 1 Noble (insulting, -1 Opinion, "you value your lord so cheaply?")
105.10 Long-Term Imprisonment
For prisoners held more than 3 months:
Roll 1d6 each season:
Roll Result
1 Dies. Illness, mistreatment, or simply gives up.
2-3 Permanent stat loss (-1 on release). The spirit cracks.
4-5 No change. Endurance.
6 Forms bond with captor. Opinion +2. May become ally.
105.11 Execution
If a prisoner is executed:
Blood Feud automatically triggered. The house will not forget.
Reputation: +1 Fear, -2 Honor with civilized houses
Their house: -3 Opinion permanent. Children train to kill you.
Religious response: Depends on faith. Crownbound may condemn.
105.12 The Empty Cell
If a prisoner escapes or is ransomed:
The cell waits. It has been warmed by their body, marked by their presence. It
will be warmed again. There is always another prisoner.
The cycle continues.
PART 106: WOUNDS, HEALING & MEDICINE (Per Patch 2.0)
106.1 Core Principle (Revised)
A wound is not death. But a wound untreated is death waiting. The body can
survive remarkable things if given time, rest, and care. The body can also fail
for no reason at all. This is not a game of hit points. This is a game of
consequences.
Medicine is not magic. It is skill, knowledge, and the right herbs applied at
the right time. A battlefield surgeon with a saw can save your life. They can
also take your arm. Both are survival.
106.2 Wound Levels (Expanded)
When a commander is reduced to 0 in battle, or when narrative demands a wound:
Roll 1d6 on the Wound Table.
Roll Wound Level Effect
1 Dead The wound kills them. They have last words if anyone is near.
2 Near Death Unconscious. Will die in 1d3 days without care. Cannot be
moved roughly.
3 Grievous Wound Cannot command. -2 to all personal rolls. Can be moved
with care.

-- 281 of 529 --

4 Severe Wound -2 Initiative, -1 to all actions until healed. Can
function but suffers.
5 Moderate Wound -1 Initiative until healed. Still effective.
6 Light Wound 1d6 hours of discomfort, then recover. No lasting effect.
106.3 Expanded Healing Time
Wound Level Base Time Healing Roll (1d6 each period) Period
Light 1d6 days 3+ = healed Day
Moderate 1d6 weeks 4+ = healed Week
Severe 2d6 weeks 4+ = becomes Moderate Week
Grievous 1d6 months 5+ = becomes Severe Month
Near Death 1d6 months 5+ = becomes Grievous, else death Month
Healing Roll Modifiers:
Condition Modifier
Attended by trained healer +1
Resting in comfortable conditions +1
Proper medicine available +1
Moved during healing -1
Poor conditions (cold, wet, hungry) -1
Previous wound aggravated -1
Infection present -2

106.10 Death's Door — Final Words
When a character dies, if anyone is present to hear: They may speak final words.
No more than one sentence. These words become true in the narrative sense — they
shape how the character is remembered, what vows are passed on, what grudges
continue. No mechanical effect. Only story.
PART 107: RELIGIOUS SYSTEM (Pages 548-555)
107.1 The Three Great Faiths
Faith Doctrine Behavior
Crownbound Church King chosen by divine will Supports heirs, condemns
oathbreakers, blesses loyal armies
Ashen Covenant King fell because realm was corrupt Encourages civil war,
supports brutal factions
Silent Faith Power must not be centralized Undermines large factions,
supports rebels
107.2 Religious Alignment
Each Noble House rolls 1d6 at game start:
Roll Alignment
1-2 Crownbound
3-4 Ashen
5-6 Silent
107.3 Religious Influence Track
Each House has Faith Influence (0-6). Starts at 2.
Increases by:
Winning battles
Protecting temples
Keeping oaths
Marrying into blessed bloodlines
Decreases by:

-- 284 of 529 --

Betrayals
Burning cities
Killing clergy
Losing capitals
107.4 Blessings (Once Per Month)
If Influence ≥ 4, the House may request a blessing: Roll 1d6
Roll Blessing
1 No blessing
2 +1 Morale (next battle)
3 +1 Initiative
4 +1 Kill per success
5 Enemy suffers -1 morale
6 Divine Favor (reroll one failed combat roll)
107.5 Losing Faith — When Influence Drops
When a house's Faith Influence drops:
Score Effect
1 Priests are cold. Tithes demanded. Blessings cost double.
0 Excommunication risk. Roll 1d6 each season: 1 = excommunicated.
107.6 Excommunication
If excommunicated:
Effects:
-2 Opinion with all houses of that faith
Cannot receive blessings
Priests refuse service (no weddings, no burials, no ceremonies)
Peasant Opinion -1 (they fear for their souls)
May be target of "holy war" justification
Removal: Penance. Major quest for the faith. Or convert.
107.7 Converting Faith
A house may convert to another faith:
Step 1 — Declare intention. Current faith: -3 Opinion, possible excommunication.
Step 2 — Spend 1 year and 500 SD on ceremonies, gifts, rebuilding.
Step 3 — New Faith Influence starts at 2. Old faith: 0, hostile.
Conversion during war: Neighbors of old faith may attack. Neighbors of new faith
may protect.
107.8 Religious Conflict
When two faiths go to war:
Each side may declare "Holy War" justification:
+1 Morale for all troops
+1 Recruitment (faithful flock to defend)
-2 Opinion with neutral houses of other faiths
Cannot surrender easily (faith demands death or victory)

-- 285 of 529 --

107.9 Atheism — The Faithless
A character may reject all faiths.
Effects:
No blessings ever
+1 Opinion with other faithless (rare)
-2 Opinion with all faithful houses
Cannot hold religious office
May be barred from certain ceremonies (weddings, coronations)
107.10 The Faiths Remember
A house's religious history is tracked. Conversion is not forgotten. Old faiths
watch. They wait. They remember.
"If you leave us, you may return. But we will watch you for a generation to be
sure you mean it."
PART 108: CULT SYSTEM (Pages 553-556)
108.1 Founding a Secret Cult
Requirements:
Must control one of: Tavern, Hidden Meeting Place, Friendly Holding
Resource Amount
Gold 120 SD
Timber 20 units
Time 4 weeks
108.2 Cult Effects
Once founded, a cult provides:
+1 to espionage in the local area (members spy for you)
+1 to loyalty from members
Potential recruitment pool (members may become soldiers)
Hidden communication network

Roll Result
1 Cult shrinks (member leaves, -1d6 followers)
2-3 No growth
4-5 Steady growth (+1d6 followers)
6 Rapid growth (+2d6 followers, +1 to local influence)

108.5 Cult Reveal Action

Negotiate (bribe, promise, or deal with authorities)

108.6 Cult Purge System
If authorities move against the cult:
Roll 1d6 + Cult Strength vs 1d6 + Authority Strength
Result Outcome
Cult wins by 3+ Cult Uprising — riots, militia fighting, religious conflict
Cult wins by 1-2 Cult Driven Underground — returns to Hidden state
Tie Stalemate — both sides take losses, cult remains Suspected
Authority wins by 1-2 Cult Driven Underground — heavy losses
Authority wins by 3+ Cult Destroyed — leadership killed or captured
108.7 Possible Purge Results
Result Description
Cult Destroyed Leadership killed or captured
Cult Driven Underground Returns to Hidden State
Cult Uprising Riots, militia fighting, religious conflict

PART 111: THE YOUNG PRETENDER (Per Patch 7.25)

-- 290 of 529 --

111.1 Core Principle — The Lie That Wants to Be True
The Young Pretender is a false claimant to the Empty Throne. He has no royal
blood. No proof. No claim. Nothing but a story — and the desperate hope of the
small folk who need someone to believe in.
He is:
Sixteen years old
Handsome (strikingly so)
Charming (naturally, effortlessly)
Honest (he believes his own story)
Uneducated (cannot read, speaks with peasant's accent)
Magnetic (people want to follow him)
He is not:
A warrior (has never held a sword)
A scholar (cannot write his name)
A politician (does not understand lords and oaths)
A liar (he believes)
111.2 Trigger Condition
The system rolls 1d100 at the start of each year.
d100 Roll Outcome
1 The Young Pretender appears somewhere in the world this year
2-100 Nothing
If triggered: A random village in a random region produces a boy. He is sixteen.
He is beautiful. He claims — or others claim for him — that he is the lost
prince, hidden at birth, raised in secret by loyalists who have since died.
111.3 The Story — How It Spreads
Month Spread
1 Local rumor — known in the boy's home region
2 Regional rumor — known in adjacent regions
3 Widespread — known across the continent
4+ Persistent — everyone has heard the name
111.4 The Boy — Stats and Nature
Attribute Value
Age 16
Appearance Handsome, striking, memorable
Bearing Natural nobility (despite peasant upbringing)
Speech Peasant accent, but words ring true
Education None (cannot read, write, or cipher)
Combat None (has never held a sword)
Charisma 6 (exceptional)
Honesty Believes his own story
111.5 Who Believes — The Small Folk
Group Belief Chance Why
Peasants in his home village 90% They raised him. Of course they believe.
Peasants in his home region 70% The story spread naturally. They want it
true.
Peasants in distant regions 40% They've heard the name. Hope travels.
Merchants 20% Practical folk. They've seen too many lies.
Mercenaries 50% A cause to fight for? Maybe. Coin first, though.
Minor Lords 10% They know the history. But... what if?

-- 291 of 529 --

Great Lords 1% They know. But some pretend, for politics.
The Hidden Heir (real) 0% She knows the truth. She is the truth.
111.6 Small Folk Flocking
Each month, the boy's following grows. Roll 1d6 × 100:
Month Followers Gained
1 1d6 × 100 peasants (unarmed, hopeful)
2 2d6 × 100 peasants (armed with farm tools)
3 3d6 × 100 peasants + 1d6 × 10 mercenaries
4 4d6 × 100 peasants + 2d6 × 10 mercenaries
5+ 5d6 × 100 peasants + 3d6 × 10 mercenaries
111.7 Who Dismisses — The Lords
Landed lords, great and small, know the truth. The old king had no children. The
histories are clear. The scholars agree.
Public Reaction:
"A pretender. A fraud. A peasant boy with a pretty face and a story. Ignore him.
He will fade."
Private Reaction:
"...But what if the small folk believe? What if they march? What if he reaches
the capital with ten thousand behind him?"
111.8 Lordly Options
Option Effect
Ignore The boy grows. The movement swells.
Mock The small folk resent you. The boy gains sympathy.
Suppress Send soldiers. Kill followers. The boy becomes a martyr.
Use Pretend to support him. Control him. Use him against rivals.
Kill Assassinate the boy. The movement dies — or explodes.
111.9 Assassination Attempt
If the boy is killed:
Roll 1d6
Roll Outcome
1-2 Movement dies. Followers scatter. The lie ends.
3-4 Martyrdom. Followers triple. A new leader rises — angrier.
5-6 He wasn't really killed. The story grows. He cannot die.
111.10 The Boy's Fate — Possible Outcomes
Outcome Description
Martyrdom Killed by lords. Becomes a legend. Movements rise in his name for
generations.
Absorption Co-opted by a lord. Becomes a puppet. His followers serve another's
ends.
Disappearance He vanishes. No one knows why. His followers wait. They always
wait.
Revelation Truth emerges. Movement shatters. Some refuse to believe.
Crowning Impossible — but what if? What if he reaches the capital? What if
the people put him on the throne? It would not last. But for one day, the lie
would be true.
111.11 If the Boy Learns the Truth
What happens if someone tells him? If he learns he is not royal, not special,
not the lost prince?
Roll 1d6:
Roll Outcome
1 He denies it. Cannot accept. The lie becomes truth in his mind.
2-3 He accepts, but continues. "They need something to believe. Let them
believe me."
4 He abdicates. Disappears. The movement scatters.
5 He becomes bitter. Angry. The movement turns violent.
6 He kills the messenger. The lie is all he has.
111.12 The Hidden Heir's Reaction

-- 292 of 529 --

If the real Hidden Heir exists, she has complex feelings about the Pretender.
Situation Reaction
He is a fraud She knows. She is the truth.
He has thousands following They should be following her.
He is sincere She cannot hate him for believing.
He is used by a lord She may try to rescue him — or kill him.
If they meet:
She looks at him — this boy, this beautiful lie — and sees herself. What if she
had been raised in a village? What if no one had told her the truth? Would she
believe, too?
He looks at her — this woman, this stranger — and feels something he cannot
name. Not love. Not fear. Recognition. As if they are two halves of the same
story.
"Who are you?" he asks.
She does not answer. She cannot. To tell him the truth would destroy him. To
tell him a lie would make her like him.
She leaves. He watches her go. He does not know why he weeps.

Culture Reaction
Central Plains Dismissive at first. Worried as he grows. Knights laugh, but
nervously.
Western Rivers Pragmatic. Can we use him? Can we trade with his followers?
Eastern Forests (Settled) Curious. A forest lord might shelter him — or hunt
him.
Eastern Forests (Tribal) The ancestors whisper. Is he one of them? A spirit
in flesh?
Southern Mountains The mountain does not care about false kings. But if he
comes...
Northern Snowlands A boy with a story? The north respects stories. They
might listen.
Nomad Steppe (Traditional) A khan without horses? Without clan? He is
nothing. But his followers...
Nomad Steppe (Settled) Interesting. A figurehead. We could use a figurehead.
111.14 The End of the Pretender — Five Endings
Ending Description
Martyrdom Killed by lords. Becomes a legend. Movements rise in his name for
generations.
Absorption Co-opted by a lord. Becomes a puppet. His followers serve another's
ends.
Disappearance He vanishes. No one knows why. His followers wait. They always
wait.
Revelation Truth emerges. Movement shatters. Some refuse to believe.
Crowning Impossible — but what if? What if he reaches the capital? What if

PART 112: THE BLIND TRAVELER (Per Patch 7.24)
112.1 Core Principle
The Blind Traveler appears only once in a generation — if that. He approaches
the lord of a holding. He offers a choice.
112.2 Trigger Condition
The system rolls 1d1000 at the start of each year.
d1000 Roll Outcome
1 The Blind Traveler appears somewhere in the world this year
2-1000 Nothing
Location: The Traveler appears at a random holding. He approaches the lord of
that holding — whoever holds the land and holds authority.

-- 293 of 529 --

112.3 The Arrival — At a Holding
A stranger comes to the gate of [Holding Name]. Old — no, ancient. Blind — eyes
white as milk, yet he walks without stumbling, without hesitation. His clothes
are rags, but clean. His feet are bare, but unmarked by the road.
He stops before the guards and speaks in a voice that carries without effort:
"I have walked far. I have walked long. I have walked since before your
grandfather's grandfather drew breath. And I have come to your lord with an
offer."
He waits. He sees the lord without seeing.
112.4 The Offer — To the Lord of the Holding
The Traveler speaks to the lord:
"Give me three things: a silverdew coin, a wolf pelt, and your left eye. In
return, I will give you guardians. Not servants. Not pets. Guardians. They will
watch your lands as long as you draw breath. They will hunt those who would harm
you. They will not trouble your flocks."
"What say you?"
112.5 The Choice — Refusal (Holding Effect)
If the lord of the holding refuses:
The blind traveler nods slowly, as if expecting this. He speaks then — words no
one has ever heard, in a tongue that makes the guards step back, makes the
horses shift uneasily, makes the very air feel cold.
When he finishes, he turns and walks away. No one sees him leave. He is
simply... gone.
Consequence for that holding:
For the next two months (60 days), no game can be hunted or found within that
holding's lands.
Effect Mechanical
Hunting Automatic failure. No FSU gained from hunting.
Foraging No meat found. Herbs and plants only.
Livestock Unaffected (they are not "game")
Duration 60 days exactly. The land is silent.
112.6 The Choice — Acceptance (Holding Effect)
If the lord of the holding agrees:
The blind traveler smiles — a small thing, barely a movement of ancient lips.
"Good. The coin for the journey. The pelt for the pact. And the eye... the eye
is the seeing."
He produces a knife — where from, no one can say. It is not steel. It is not
stone. It is something older.
"Be still."
The Cutting:
The lord feels no pain. The knife moves, precise and certain. In a moment, it is
done. The traveler holds the lord's left eye in his palm — perfect, whole,
undamaged.
"You will not heal this. No herb, no medicine, no prayer. But you will not
suffer it either. The socket will close clean. You may wear what you wish —

-- 294 of 529 --

glass, leather, cloth. The choice is yours."
No mechanical penalties. No disorientation. No loss of depth perception. The
wound is perfect, and perfectly closed.
The Lord May Choose:
A false eye (glass, stone, carved wood)
An eyepatch (leather, cloth, metal)
Nothing (the socket closed, a shadowed hollow)
This becomes a permanent part of their appearance.
112.7 The Gift — Guardians of That Holding
After the eye is taken, the traveler speaks again:
"It is done. The pact is sealed. They will come."
He turns and walks away. By the time anyone thinks to follow, he is gone.
Within a week, the first wolves appear on that holding's lands.
At first, the people are afraid. Wolves are wolves — killers of sheep, threats
to children. But these wolves do not approach the flocks. They do not come near
the village. They watch from the edges, and what they watch is not the people.
Within a month, there are more. Packs, ranging across the lord's lands. They
hunt the forests, the hills, the valleys. And when bandits come, when enemy
scouts creep too close, when anyone with ill intent sets foot on that soil —
The wolves find them.
112.8 Wolf Guardians — Mechanical Effects
Lasts as Long as That Lord Lives:
Effect Description
Wolf Packs 2d6 wolves roam the holding's lands. They breed and multiply slowly.
Livestock Protection Wolves never attack the holding's sheep, cattle, or
horses.
Bandit Hunting Any bandit or raiding party attempting to operate on these
lands must roll 1d6 each week. On 1-2, they are attacked by wolves (1d6
casualties, may retreat).
Enemy Scouts Enemy scouting parties have -2 to Recon rolls (wolves harass
them).
Enemy Foragers Enemy armies foraging on these lands: roll 1d6 each week. On
1-3, they are attacked (1d6 casualties, foraging yields -50%).
Wolf Mortality Wolves can be killed. If hunted, they fight back. If left
alone, they breed (new pups each spring).
Wolf Pack Stats (if attacked):
Stat Value
Pack Size 2d6 wolves
AC 3 (quick, hard to hit)
Attack Dice 2d6 per pack
Special +1d6 when ambushing, +2d6 when protecting territory
Morale 6 (will not break on home ground)
112.9 The Pact's Nature — Not Magic
These are not magical wolves. They are wolves. They can be:
Hunted (by poachers, by enemies, by foolish locals)
Killed (in battle, by traps, by larger predators)

-- 295 of 529 --

Scared off (by fire, by large armies)
Driven out (by persistent effort)
But they are also guardians. They behave unlike normal wolves because of the
pact — an ancient understanding between the traveler's kind and the wolves
themselves. The wolves choose to protect the player's lands. It is not
compulsion. It is agreement.
If the player abuses them (hunts them, uses them as cannon fodder, neglects
them), the wolves may leave. The pact is not slavery. It is trust.
112.10 Death of the Pact Lord
When the lord who made the pact dies, the pact ends. The wolves:
May stay, if they have grown attached to the land
May leave, seeking new territory
May revert to wild behavior, hunting livestock
Roll 1d6:
Roll Outcome
1-2 Wolves leave within a month. The land is empty again.
3-4 Wolves stay, but become wild. They now hunt livestock.
5-6 Wolves stay, and continue protecting the land. The pact passes to the
heir? (No — the heir did not pay the price. But the wolves choose to stay
anyway. This is rare and mysterious.)
If the lord was killed by an enemy:
Wolves may specifically target that enemy and their forces for 1d6 months
+2 to attack rolls against that enemy's forces on that land
PART 113: THE WARM YEAR (Per Patch 7.21)
113.1 Core Principle
Once or twice in a lifetime, the world changes. Winter does not come. The snows
hold back. The rivers do not freeze. The passes remain open. Armies march
through months that should be impassable.
No one knows why. The priests have theories. The scholars have guesses. The
common folk say the gods are hungry for war.
113.2 The Silent Roll
At the beginning of each year, the system makes a hidden roll — at the
transition from Autumn to Winter (Day 1 of Deepfrost).
The Roll: 1d100
d100 Roll Result
1 WARM YEAR — No snow anywhere. Winter is mild across the entire continent.
2-100 Normal winter
Odds: 1% chance. Once every 100 years on average.
The system lies. The gods lie. Probability lies. Some generations never see a
Warm Year. Some see two. The average is once a century, but the roll is pure. No
modifiers. No pity. No memory.
113.3 The Silence — No Announcement
The system does not announce a Warm Year. No message: "A Warm Year has begun."
No notification. No fanfare.

-- 296 of 529 --

The player simply notices, over time, that the snow isn't coming.
"It's Deepfrost. Should be snowing."
"It's not."
"That's... odd."
The player must observe the change. Must ask questions. Must wonder.
113.4 Effects of a Warm Year
Effect Description
No Snow Anywhere Northern Snowlands: bare ground. Nomad Steppe (North): grazing
continues. All snow lines: zero.
Mild Temperatures Cold, but not deadly. Frostbite risk eliminated.
Rivers Don't Freeze Western Rivers (North): no ice bridges. River travel
continues all winter.
Mountain Passes Open Southern Mountains: passes remain clear. No "closed for
winter" season.
Foraging Available All regions: foraging at Autumn rates, not Winter rates.
Armies March No winter campaign restrictions. War can continue year-round.
Crops? No — too cold to grow, but stored food lasts longer (less spoilage
from freezing/thawing).
Regional Specifics:
Region Normal Winter Warm Year Winter
Northern Snowlands Deep snow, travel +200%, foraging 0 No snow, travel +0%,
foraging 1 FSU
Nomad Steppe (North) Snow, herds move south No snow, herds stay, grazing
continues
Western Rivers (North) Snow, rivers freeze No snow, rivers flow, boat travel
possible
Eastern Forests (North) Snow, hunting difficult No snow, hunting normal
Central Plains Rare snow (1-in-30) No change (already rare)
Southern Mountains Freezing winds, passes closed Winds remain, but passes
open
113.5 Historical Warm Years
The system may generate rumors, legends, and historical records of past Warm
Years.
Common Folk Say:
"Grandfather remembers the Warm Year. Said the gods were thirsty. Said they
wanted war so bad they let the armies fight through winter. Thousands died. But
grandfather survived. He always said the gods weren't thirsty enough to take
him."
Scholars Note:
"The Warm Year of 287. Three campaigns launched in Deepfrost. Two armies
destroyed when they expected no resistance. The third conquered a kingdom while
its enemies huddled in what should have been impregnable fortresses. The war
lasted four years instead of one."
Priests Whisper:
"The gods do not control the weather. They influence it. When they want blood,
they make it possible. The Warm Year is a prayer answered — just not the prayer
you were saying."
113.6 Strategic Implications
Strategy Normal Winter Warm Year Winter
Campaign planning Must end before Deepfrost Can continue year-round
Fortress defense "They can't attack in winter" They can. They will.
Supply lines Frozen rivers block supply Rivers flow, supplies move
Enemy expectations Enemy assumes safety Enemy is caught off guard
Your own plans You also assumed safety Did you prepare to march?
113.7 The Gods of War
The system does not explain why Warm Years happen. It offers possibilities —

-- 297 of 529 --

through NPCs, through rumors, through the player's own interpretation.
The Crownbound Church says:
"The King was chosen by divine will. The weather obeys the rightful king. A Warm
Year means the throne is empty and the gods are calling for a new one."
The Ashen Covenant says:
"The king fell because the realm was corrupt. The Warm Year is the world purging
itself. War is the fire. The worthy survive."
The Silent Faith says:
"Power must not be centralized. The Warm Year is the world resisting control. No
one should have that much power — not over weather, not over men."
The tribes say:
"The spirits are restless. They want stories. They want blood. They want names
to remember. Give them what they want, or they'll take it anyway."
The nomads say:
"The grass knows. When the grass does not freeze, it means the world is waiting.
Waiting for something. Someone. Maybe you."
PART 114: THE SNOW BEAR MIGRATION (Per Patch 7.29)
114.1 Core Principle — The Northern Madness
Once in a generation, something breaks in the far north. The snow bears —
already massive, already dangerous — begin to move. Not in ones or twos, but in
scores. They travel south, out of season, driven by instincts no one can
explain.
They are not magical. They are bears. But they are bears the size of wagons,
bears whose claws can tear through chainmail, bears whose fur changes from white
to a dark, mottled grey as they descend from the ice.
They do not seek men. They seek prey — and whatever they find, they eat.
114.2 Trigger Condition
At the start of each year, the system makes a silent roll.
d100 Roll Outcome
1 The Snow Bear Migration occurs this year
2-100 Nothing
If triggered: Within the first month of spring, reports begin to arrive from the
north. Snow bears are moving south. No one knows why. No one can stop them.
114.3 The Migration Path
Snow bears emerge from the Northern Snowlands and travel south. They split into
three groups:
Group Destination Arrival Time
Western Group Western Rivers (northern border) 1-2 months after migration
begins
Central Group Nomad Steppe (northern tip) 2-3 months after migration begins
Eastern Group Eastern Forests (northern border) 1-2 months after migration
begins
Number of Bears: 3d6 × 10 bears per group (average 105 bears per region, 315
total).
114.4 The Transformation
As the bears move south, they change.
Stage Appearance Size Aggression
Northern Snowlands (origin) Pure white, normal snow bear size 800-1,200 lbs
Territorial, avoid humans
During Migration White fading to grey 1,200-1,500 lbs Hungry, aggressive

-- 298 of 529 --

Arrival at Destination Dark grey/blackish, larger 1,500-2,000 lbs
Voracious, attacks anything
114.5 Snow Bear Stats
Stat Value
Size 1,500-2,000 lbs
Length 10-12 feet standing
AC 4 (thick hide, difficult to wound)
Attack Dice 4d6 (claws) or 5d6 (bite)
Initiative +1 (surprisingly fast)
Morale 7 (fearless when hungry)
Special Can kill a warhorse on a roll of 6
Special Abilities:
Ability Effect
Terrifying Roar Any unit encountering a bear must make a morale check at -1
Thick Hide -1 to hit with bows, -2 with slings (stones bounce off)
Crushing Blow On a natural 6, target is killed instantly (no AC save)
Scent Tracker Can follow prey for days; +2 to tracking
114.6 Ecological Havoc
Effect Description
Game Depletion For the first month after arrival, hunting yields 0 FSU (bears
eat everything).
Livestock Losses Each week, 1d6 livestock are killed per bear in the area.
Villages Threatened Small villages may be attacked. Roll 1d6 each week: on
1, bear attack.
Forest Silence No birds, no small game — everything has fled. Foraging yields
-2.
Trade Disruption Caravans avoid affected areas (-50% trade income for
duration).
Duration: The bears stay until they are hunted/killed, driven off, or summer
heat arrives.
114.7 Responding to the Threat
Response Effect
Hunt Them Organize hunting parties. Each week, roll 1d6: 1-3 = 1d6 bears
killed, 4-6 = 1d6 hunters killed.
Drive Them Off Use fire, noise, organized patrols. Bears retreat to deeper
wilds — but return later.
Ignore Them Bears breed. Cubs are born. The problem becomes permanent.
Contain Them Fortify villages, guard livestock, accept losses until summer.
Military Action Hunting Roll (per week, per 100 soldiers assigned):
Roll Outcome
1 Bears kill 1d6 soldiers, no bears killed
2-3 Bears kill 1d3 soldiers, 1d3 bears killed
4-5 No casualties, 1d6 bears killed
6 No casualties, 2d6 bears killed, +1 morale to troops
114.8 The Breeding Problem
If the bears are not hunted or driven off, they will breed.
Breeding Requirements:
At least 20 bears remain in a region through spring
They find a secluded area (deep forest, remote hills)
No significant harassment for 2 months
If they breed:
2d6 cubs are born
Cubs are dark brown, not white

-- 299 of 529 --

Cubs have shorter fur (adapted to warmer climate)
Cubs have thicker muscles and sharper claws
Cubs grow to even larger size than parents (2,000-2,500 lbs)
New Generation Stats:
Stat Value
Size 2,000-2,500 lbs
AC 5 (even thicker hide)
Attack Dice 5d6 (claws) or 6d6 (bite)
Special Claws can rip through iron (ignore shield AC bonus)
These bears can survive summer. Their dark fur, shorter coat, and massive size
make them heat-tolerant. If they establish a breeding population, they become a
permanent fixture of the region.
114.9 Summer Heat
Original snow bears (white) cannot survive summer in the south. If they have not
bred by the time summer arrives:
Month Effect
Late Spring Bears become lethargic. Hunting effectiveness +1.
Early Summer Bears seek high ground, caves, cold spots. Easier to hunt
(+2).
Midsummer Bears begin dying. 1d6 per week succumb to heat.
Late Summer Remaining bears retreat north or die. Migration ends.
If they have bred: The new generation (dark brown) are unaffected by heat. They
stay year-round.
114.10 Long-Term Consequences
Outcome Effect
Bears eliminated Region returns to normal. Stories told for generations.
Bears driven off They may return next year (roll 1d6: on 1, they come back)
Breeding population established New predator in the region. Hunting yields
-25% permanently. Livestock losses annually. New danger for travelers.
If breeding population established:
Region gains a new "natural wonder" (or menace)
Local lords may offer bounties (5 SD per bear)
Bear Resources:
Resource Treated As Value
Bear Meat FSU (Food) 1d6 FSU per bear
Bear Pelt (white) Furs (Trade Good) 30 SD (rare, beautiful)
Bear Pelt (dark) Furs (Trade Good) 20 SD (tough, warm)
Bear Claws Trade Goods 5 SD each
Bear Teeth Trade Goods 3 SD each
Bear Fat Trade Goods 1 SD per lb
Legendary Bear: If a bear of unusual size (2,500+ lbs) is killed, its pelt is
worth 100+ SD and becomes a family heirloom (treat as Rare Item).
114.11 Cultural Reactions
Culture Reaction to Bears
Northern Snowlands "They left us. The bears are gone. The south will learn
what we've always known."
Nomad Steppe Horses panic. Herds scatter. This is disaster.
Western Rivers Bears? In our forests? This is not right. Something is wrong
with the world.
Eastern Forests (Settled) Hunt them. Hunt them all. Protect the villages.
Eastern Forests (Tribal) The spirits sent them. We must listen. What are
they trying to tell us?

-- 300 of 529 --

Central Plains Bears? We have knights for bears. Send the cavalry.
Southern Mountains Let them come. The passes will break them.
All Fear. Respect. Caution.
114.12 Integration with Existing Systems
System How Bears Interact
Part 61 (Hunting) Hunting yields drop to 0 during active migration. After
breeding, permanent -25% yield.
Part 54 (Goods) Bear resources use existing categories: Furs, Food, Trade
Goods.
Part 49 (Trade) Trade routes through affected areas suffer -50% income.
Part 33 (Combat) If soldiers fight bears, use combat rules. Bears are treated
as "units" with stats above.
Part 42 (Loot) Killed bears provide resources (see above).
Part 84 (Reputation) Successfully defending against bears or leading hunts
increases reputation.
Part 75 (Tournaments) Bear pelts become prized awards. "The Bear Hunt" becomes
a legendary tale.
PART 115: ELDRIC THE BLACK DRAKE (Per Patch 7.30)
115.1 Core Principle — The Last Survivor
Eldric is not young. His hair is grey at the temples. His armor is battered but
well-maintained. His shield is cracked — a heavy blow that should have killed
him — and the paint is worn away until only the black dragon on its face remains
visible. He does not speak of his past, but the weight of it hangs on him like a
cloak.
He is:
A deadly warrior (sword and bow both)
An excellent commander (tactically brilliant)
A natural war marshal (can lead armies)
Patient (waits for the right moment)
Loyal (once sworn, he does not break faith)
Honorable (but his honor is his own, not the plains' code)
He is looking for:
A clever lord (he respects intelligence)
A just ruler (he has seen tyranny)
A cause worth dying for (he has died once already; he will not waste the second
life)
115.2 Trigger Condition
At the start of each year, for every holding in the world, the system makes a
silent roll.
Condition Chance
Base chance per holding 0.1% (1 in 1,000)
Holding is in Eastern Forests (Settled) +0.1% (he is home)
Holding is in Central Plains -0.05% (he is wary of plains lords)
Holding is ruled by a known oathbreaker -0.1% (he will not serve)
Holding is ruled by a known just lord +0.1% (he seeks such men)
Maximum chance: 0.3% per holding per year.
If triggered: Eldric rides to that holding. He arrives alone, on a tired
warhorse, his shield on his arm, his longbow on his back, his black iron sword
at his hip.

-- 301 of 529 --

115.3 The Arrival
A rider appears on the road — alone, slow, deliberate. As he approaches, you see
a man in battered chain, his shield cracked and scarred, the paint worn until
only one thing remains: a black dragon, faded but unmistakable. He stops before
your gate and waits.
When your guards challenge him, he speaks in a voice that has known too many
battles:
"My name is Eldric. They call me the Black Drake. I have fought for lords who
died, for causes that failed, for comrades who now lie in unmarked graves. I am
the last of my company. I seek a lord worth serving."
He waits. He does not kneel. Not yet.
115.4 The Choice — Refusal
If the player refuses:
Eldric nods slowly, as if expecting this. He turns his horse without another
word. As he rides away, you see a banner curled on his back — a black dragon on
a field of faded green. He does not look back.
He is gone before sunset.
Consequence:
Eldric leaves. He may appear at another holding next year.
The player will not see him again this campaign (unless they seek him out based
on rumors).
-1 to "just lord" reputation (rumors spread that you turned away a worthy
knight).
115.5 The Choice — Acceptance
If the player accepts:
Eldric dismounts. For the first time, you see the full weight of him — tall,
broad-shouldered, but with the look of a man who has not slept well in years. He
kneels, one knee, head bowed.
"I am yours, my lord. My sword, my bow, my life. I ask only that you be worthy
of them."
He rises. For a moment, something flickers in his eyes — grief, perhaps, or
memory. Then it is gone, and he is simply a soldier awaiting orders.
Eldric joins the player's forces as a commander.
115.6 Eldric's Stats
Attribute Value
Age 47
Origin Eastern Forests (Settled)
Commander Tier 4 (elite)
War Marshal Tier 5 (legendary — when leading armies)
Loyalty Starts at 4 (cautious, watching)
Temperament Patient (will wait for the right moment)
Priority Victory (he has lost enough)
Fear Betrayal (will not serve oathbreakers)
Personal Combat Stats:
Equipment Quality Bonus
Black Iron Sword Superb 2 rerolls per battle
Forest Longbow Superb +2 to hit, silent

-- 302 of 529 --

Chain Mail High-Grade +1 durability
Heater Shield Damaged Currently +0 AC (until fixed)
Warhorse High-Grade +2 Initiative, 2 rerolls
Shield Note: Eldric's shield is cracked. Until repaired (cost: 10 SD, 2 days at
a forge), it provides only +0 AC. Once repaired, it becomes a High-Grade Heater
Shield (+1 AC, 1 defensive reroll).
115.7 Eldric's Skills
Skill Level Effect
Personal Combat 5 Deadly in duel or melee
Tactical Command 4 +1 to all unit rolls when he leads
Strategic Command 5 When serving as War Marshal, +2 to coordination rolls
Archery 5 Can use longbow from horseback (unique)
Tracking 4 +2 to recon when scouting
Loyalty Variable Starts at 4, increases with just rule
115.8 Raising the Black Drake's Company
If Eldric is given permission to raise a unit, he will create The Black Drake's
Company — an elite force unlike any other.
Unit Composition:
Element Description
Soldiers 40 mounted warriors
Mounts Warhorses (High-Grade, trained for battle)
Armor Chain mail (High-Grade)
Primary Weapon Swords (High-Grade)
Secondary Weapon Longbows (High-Grade)
Shields Heater shields (High-Grade)
Tactics Hit-and-run, skirmish, then charge
Unit Stats:
Stat Value

Tier 3 (veteran)

Weapon Dice (ranged) 4d6 (longbow + skill)

Morale 6 (fearless)

Recruitment Cost:

40 swords (High-Grade)
40 longbows (High-Grade)
40 heater shields (High-Grade)

If equipment of sufficient quality is not available, Eldric will wait. He will
not compromise on quality.
115.9 Eldric's Personality
Trait Description
Patient He does not rush. He watches, waits, learns.
Loyal Once sworn, he does not break faith.
Honorable His honor is his own — not the plains' code. He will retreat if it
saves lives. He will ambush if it wins. He will fight dirty if he must.
Grieving He is the last of his company. He carries them with him.

-- 303 of 529 --

Wary of Tyrants He has seen cruel lords. He will not serve another.
Respects Cleverness A smart plan impresses him more than a brave one.
Seeks Meaning He needs to believe in what he fights for.
Loyalty Changes:
Action Loyalty Change
Lord acts justly +1
Lord acts cruelly -2 (may leave if below 2)
Lord shows tactical brilliance +1
Lord wastes men needlessly -2
Lord keeps oath +1
Lord breaks oath -3 (automatic departure)
Company fights well +1
Company takes heavy losses -1
If Loyalty reaches 0: Eldric leaves. He does not betray — he simply rides away,
seeking another lord. His banner curls on his back, and he is gone.
115.10 Eldric as War Marshal
If Eldric is placed in command of a Grand Army (Part 61), he gains additional
bonuses.
Bonus Effect
Strategic Genius +2 to all coordination rolls
Hunter's Eye Can predict enemy movements; +2 to scouting
Survivor's Instinct If battle goes badly, +2 to withdrawal rolls
Inspiring Presence Allied commanders +1 loyalty while serving under him
War Marshal Special Action — The Hunter's Charge:
Once per battle, Eldric may order a coordinated charge from all cavalry units.
All cavalry gain +2d6 for that round, and enemy units must make an immediate
morale check at -1.
115.11 Eldric's Story — Revealed Over Time
Eldric does not speak of his past. But over time, through conversations, through
moments of quiet, through things he mutters in his sleep, the player may learn:
Loyalty Revelation
5 His name was not always Eldric. He was born "Eldric of the Green Valley,"
a hunter's son.
6 He went west as a young man, seeking adventure. He found war.
7 He was knighted by a Central Plains lord who saw something in him. The
lord died years ago.
8 His last company was called the Black Dragons. Their banner was a green
field with a black dragon — now faded to nothing but the dragon.
9 The charge that killed them was pointless. The lord was impatient. Eldric
begged him to wait. He did not.
10 Eldric survived because his horse stumbled and threw him into a ditch. He
lay there, unconscious, while his comrades died. He has never forgiven himself.
At Loyalty 10, Eldric may share his final secret:
"I should have died with them. Every morning I wake and wonder why I didn't. But
I am still here. Still breathing. Still fighting. Perhaps... perhaps I am meant
to die for a cause that matters. Not a lord's pride. Not a pointless hill.
Something real."
He looks at you. For the first time, there is something like hope in his eyes.
"Perhaps that cause is you."
115.12 Cultural Reactions to Eldric
Culture Reaction
Eastern Forests (Settled) "He is ours. A hunter who became a knight. We are
proud."
Eastern Forests (Tribal) "He walks two paths — the forest and the field.
The spirits watch him closely."

-- 304 of 529 --

Central Plains "A knight? From the forests? ...He fights well. We will not
speak of his origins."
Western Rivers "Practical. Adaptable. We could use a man like that."
Southern Mountains "He has the look of someone who has seen the mountain's
edge. Respect."
Northern Snowlands "He survived when his comrades died. The cold would like
him."
Nomad Steppe (Traditional) "A horseman who hunts? He is almost one of us."
Nomad Steppe (Settled) "He bridges worlds, like us. We understand."
PART 116: THE FIFTY (Pages 233-236)
116.1 Core Principle
The Fifty are not a unit. They are 50 named lives that decay when used. You do
not roll to see if they succeed. You roll to see what it costs you.
116.2 Success Is Not Guaranteed
When you send the Fifty to:
Raid
Sabotage
Assassinate
Infiltrate
Rescue
They will act. They will try. But every mission costs something. Sometimes
everything.
116.3 Resolution Structure
Step 1 — Declare Mission
Player specifies:
Target
Objective
Style (from 116.6)
Step 2 — Assess Difficulty
Target Type Difficulty
Unprotected (lonely road) 3
Lightly protected (patrols) 4
Well protected (fortified) 5
Heavily defended (castle) 6
Nearly impossible 7
Step 3 — Roll for Success
Roll 1d6 + Style Modifier + Situation Modifiers
Style Modifier:
Style Modifier
Silent 0
Greedy -1
Cruel +0 (worse consequences)
Fast +1 (more losses)
Situation Modifiers:
+1 if target scouted
+1 if inside help exists

-- 305 of 529 --

-1 if target is alert
-2 if target expects trouble
Result:
Roll Outcome
Roll < Difficulty Failure (see 116.5)
Roll = Difficulty Partial Success (see 116.4)
Roll > Difficulty Full Success (see 116.6)
116.4 Partial Success Table (1d6)
Roll Result
1 Objective failed, but diversion created
2 Target warned, mission aborted
3 Wrong target hit
4 Half damage/partial objective
5 Objective achieved, evidence left
6 Objective achieved, one captured alive
116.5 Failure Table (1d6)
Roll Result
1 All killed or captured. The Fifty are gone.
2 Most killed. 1d6 survivors return broken.
3 Mission failed, survivors return. Attrition ×2.
4 Mission failed, survivors followed. Enemy knows your location.
5 Mission failed, one survivor turned. They work for enemy now.
6 Catastrophic failure. Enemy has evidence linking to you.
116.6 Attrition Roll (Always)
After any mission (success or failure), roll for losses:
Category Base Modifiers
Dead 1d3 +1 if Fast, +1 if Failure
Crippled 1d3 +1 if Cruel
Broken 1d3 +1 if Greedy
Turned 1d3 +1 if Failure, +1 if target well-defended
These losses are PERMANENT and reduce The Fifty total.
Thresholds:
10 dead — Lose confidence (-1 to future mission rolls)
20 dead — Discipline breaks (may refuse missions)
30 dead — Stop following orders
40 dead — Dissolve as force
116.7 After-Action Report
Player receives narrative describing:
What was attempted
What happened
What was lost
What the world noticed
PART 117: SMALL FORCE INFILTRATION (Pages 237-240)
117.1 Core Principle
You do not roll to see if the plan works. You test: Is the plan believable in
this world?
If yes — it works
If no — it fails catastrophically

-- 306 of 529 --

117.2 Step 1 — Define the Breach Method
The player must describe how they intend to get in. The system evaluates based
on world logic.
117.3 Breach Type Categories
Type Requirements Failure Conditions
Physical Breach Reachable surface, tools, darkness, ladders or grapnels
Smooth stone + alert guards, no tools, daylight
Human Breach Contact inside, reason to betray, signal Guard loyal, no
leverage, no payment
Infrastructure Breach Known entrance, physical access Flooded, collapsed,
patrolled
Social Breach Plausible cover, correct timing Wrong accent, wrong season,
too many men
117.4 Step 3 — World Truth Check (No Dice)
The system asks: Is this possible given:
The target's security?
The time of day?
The season?
Known patrol patterns?
Previous events?
If yes — it works
If no — it fails catastrophically
117.5 Step 4 — Consequence Tiers
Outcome Consequence
Success Objective achieved, infiltrators escape
Partial Success Objective partially achieved, some casualties, pursuit
Failure Mission fails, survivors captured or killed
Catastrophic All killed or captured, enemy knows who sent them
117.6 Step 5 — What They Can Do Inside
Major Actions:
Open gates
Poison water
Assassinate
Burn granaries
Minor Actions:
Kill officers
Sabotage engines
Free prisoners
Steal banners
Spread panic
Limit: An infiltrating unit may attempt:
1 major action, OR

-- 307 of 529 --

2 minor actions, OR
1 major + 1 minor (if unseen)
PART 118: RUMOR & INFORMATION SYSTEM (Pages 245-250)
118.1 Core Principle
Simulate how information spreads across the world:
Slowly
Unevenly
Sometimes inaccurately
118.2 Core Rule
All information that reaches the player does so through in-world channels. No
omniscient knowledge.
118.3 Information Sources
Source Reliability Speed
Messenger High Variable (travel time)
Merchant Medium Fast along trade routes
Soldier Medium Military channels
Refugee Low Follows migration
Tavern gossip Very Low Fast locally
Scout High Depends on distance
Spy High Slow (must be extracted)
118.4 Rumor Accuracy
Roll 1d6 for each piece of information:
Roll Result
1-2 Distorted (key facts wrong)
3-4 Exaggerated (scale inflated)
5 Clear (mostly accurate)
6 False (completely wrong)
118.5 Secret Information
Some information is secret. It cannot be learned through normal channels. It
requires:
Espionage
Intercepted messages
Confession
Treason
118.6 Rumor Spread Speed
Source Range per Week
Messenger 2 regions
Merchant 1 region along trade routes
Soldier 1 region (military areas)
Refugee Follows migration path
118.7 Player Knowledge vs World Knowledge
The player knows only what their character would know. The system enforces this
strictly.
118.8 Strategic Information Advantage
Having better information than your enemies is a strategic advantage. Use
scouts, spies, and networks to gain it.
118.9 Hidden Heir Integration
Information about the Hidden Heir is the rarest and most valuable. It spreads
only through trusted channels.

-- 308 of 529 --

118.10 Political Manipulation
Players may spread false rumors to manipulate enemies. This requires:
A believable lie
A channel to spread it
Time to propagate
Risk of exposure
118.11 Rumor Intensity
Event Rumor Spread
Minor Event Local Rumor
Battle Victory Regional Rumor
Royal Marriage National Rumor
King's Death Realm-Wide Rumor
118.12 Information Decay
Old news becomes less relevant. After 1 season, rumors are considered "old news"
and have less impact.
118.13 False Rumors
Deliberately spread false rumors may be:
Believed
Suspected
Dismissed
Traced back to the source
118.14 Player Control
Players may:
Send messengers
Spread rumors deliberately
Suppress information
Intercept enemy messengers
PART 119: NICKNAMES SYSTEM (Pages 250-255)
119.1 Core Principle
Allow the world to organically grant nicknames to players and major NPCs.
Nicknames reflect how the world sees them, not how they see themselves.
119.2 Core Rule
Nicknames are earned through deeds, not chosen. The system assigns them based
on:
Battle performance
Political actions
Scandals
Repeated behaviors
119.3 Nickname Reputation Effects
Type Effect
Heroic +5% levy loyalty OR +5% mercenary recruitment OR +3% tax confidence

Dark/Fearsome +5% intimidation OR +5% enemy morale damage, but -2%
population happiness
Scandalous -5% noble respect, -3% tax compliance
Amusing +3% tavern rumor spread, +3% mercenary friendliness
119.4 Nickname Persistence Rule
Once earned, a nickname persists. It may be:
Replaced by a more famous one
Forgotten if irrelevant for years
Used ironically if the character changes
119.5 Ledger Tracking
Track nicknames in the character sheet. Each nickname includes:
The name
How it was earned
When it was earned
Its effects

119.7 Dynamic Nickname Expansion
The system may generate new nicknames based on player actions not covered in the
table.
119.8 Player Nicknames
Players earn nicknames through their deeds. The system announces them when
earned:
"After the Battle of Stonebridge, the men have begun calling you 'The Iron
Wolf.' You're not sure how you feel about it."
119.9 Multiple Nicknames
A character may have multiple nicknames from different periods of their life.
The most recent or most famous is used most often.

-- 310 of 529 --

119.10 Reputation Drift
If a character's behavior changes significantly, old nicknames may fall out of
use and new ones may emerge.
PART 120: REPUTATION SYSTEM (Pages 256-258)
120.1 Core Principle
The Player Character begins with no Personality Traits. Only actions define
reputation.
120.2 Words Do Not Create Reputation
What a character says about themselves means nothing. What others say about them
means everything. Reputation is built by deeds, witnessed and remembered.
120.3 Pattern Rule
A reputation trait is only assigned when the same type of action occurs
consistently across multiple events.
One act = rumor
Repeated acts = reputation
120.4 Narrative Filter
Reputation is filtered through narrative. The player never sees a "reputation
score" — they hear what people say about them.
120.5 Reputation Can Change
If behavior changes over time:
Old traits weaken
New traits emerge
Conflicting rumors exist
120.6 Player Intent Protection
The system does not assign reputation based on player intent. Only on actual
outcomes and how they are perceived.
PART 121: NEGOTIATED SURRENDER (Pages 258-261)
121.1 Core Principle
A negotiated surrender is not a test of persuasion. It is a test of reality.
The outcome is determined by:
What the defender believes will happen if they resist
What the attacker is known to do
Whether survival seems possible
Whether honor still has value
Whether relief is expected
121.2 Step 1 — Check if Negotiation is Possible
Negotiation is possible if:
Both sides can communicate
The defender is not fanatical
The attacker is willing to accept surrender
There is time to talk

-- 311 of 529 --

121.3 Step 2 — Establish World Truths
The system determines:
Defender's remaining strength
Attacker's reputation for mercy or cruelty
Expected relief (if any)
Cultural norms regarding surrender
121.4 Step 3 — Defender Calculation (No Roll)
The defender decides based on:
Factors favoring surrender:
Food low (less than 2 weeks)
Morale broken
No relief expected
Attacker known to spare prisoners
Defensible position compromised
Factors favoring continued resistance:
Relief expected within days
Attacker known to execute prisoners
High morale
Strong position
Religious duty to fight
121.5 Step 4 — Outcome Tiers
Outcome Occurs When
Full Surrender Food low, morale broken, attacker known to spare, no relief
Conditional Surrender Defender still has strength, attacker reputation
uncertain, relief possible
False Surrender Defender expects relief, attacker cruel, walls strong
No Surrender Defender has hope, relief imminent, attacker known cruel
Fight to Death Defender has no hope of mercy, religion demands martyrdom,
blood feud active
121.6 Full Surrender Terms
When full surrender occurs:
Garrison disarmed
Commander captured
Soldiers become prisoners
City/free held intact (if terms agreed)
Attacker may occupy immediately
121.7 Conditional Surrender Terms
When conditional surrender occurs, terms may include:

-- 312 of 529 --

Free withdrawal of garrison
Ransom for commander
Surrender of weapons but not soldiers
Temporary truce while relief is awaited
121.8 False Surrender
If the defender falsely surrenders:
Surprise round for defenders when attackers enter
Attacker may execute prisoners afterward
Reputation: defender gains "Oathbreaker" trait
121.9 No Surrender
If the defender refuses:
Siege continues
Assault likely
If stormed, city may be sacked
121.10 Fight to Death
If defenders fight to the death:
All defenders are killed
No prisoners
Attacker may have heavy casualties
Reputation: defender's house gains +2 Honor, attacker may gain Fear

C.1 CORE MECHANICS QUICK REFERENCE
COMBAT QUICK REFERENCE
Rule Summary
Attack Dice Weapon + (People ÷ 20)d6 + modifiers
Critical Hits (Base) Natural 6 = 2 kills
Critical vs Superb Shield Natural 6 = 1 kill
Critical with Mace vs Plate (AC5) Natural 6 = 3 kills
Ambush Round Double dice, defender no action
Defend Action Fights back when attacked
Morale Check When unit ≤25% — 1d6, 1-3 = break
Morale DC 5 - Tier (minimum 2)
Half Strength Penalty Lose half commander's morale bonus
WEAPON QUICK REFERENCE
Weapon Base Init Special
Mace 3d6 +0 +1d6 vs armored, 6 vs AC5 = 3 kills
Crossbow 5d6 -2 Armor Piercing (AC5 on 4+)
Pike 3d6 -2 +2d6 vs Charge, Formation
Light Javelin 2d6 +1 Skirmish, One-Use
